<meta>This page continues the technical justification for the planetary corrections introduced on the previous page. While the previous page listed the specific correction values (Rāmabīja) for various planets, Sudhākara now provides the mathematical "proof" or derivation (upapatti) for how these factors are determined, specifically focusing on the Moon's motion using continued fractions and observations over time.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>In the *Delight of Calculation*</header>

#### Sudhākara:—
This is for the sake of clarity. The logical proof <term>upapatti: mathematical derivation or rationale</term> is as follows: By multiplying by 12,000 <note>original: 'khābhrakhārkair' — kha (0), abhra (0), kha (0), arka (12)</note> and so on, the result in seconds of arc <term>vikalā: 1/3600th of a degree</term> is obtained. When this is corrected by the difference between the actual observed planet and the planet calculated from the day-count, the true position is revealed. For example, to understand the correction factor <term>bījakarma: a correction applied to mean motion to align it with observation</term> for the Moon in arc-seconds, first, the daily motion in degrees is taken as $\frac{1400080}{106257}$. Its continued fraction expansion <term>vitatamāna: a method of successive approximations</term> is:

$13 + \frac{1}{9 + \frac{1}{5 + \frac{1}{1 + \frac{1}{9 + \frac{1}{29 + \frac{1}{1 + \frac{1}{2 + \frac{1}{1 + \frac{1}{3 + \frac{1}{1 + \frac{1}{3 + \frac{1}{6}}}}}}}}}}}}}$

From this, the approximate values <note>These are the 'convergents' of the fraction</note> are: $\frac{13}{1}, \frac{66}{5}, \frac{79}{6}, \frac{224}{17},$ and so on.

In calculating the planet's position from the day-count, the Teacher <note>referring to Bhāskara II, the author</note> adopted this value: $\frac{224}{17}$. This can be expressed in another form as $\frac{224}{17} = 13 - \frac{14}{17}$. By calculating the difference between this and the true value, a second small fraction $\frac{1}{8600}$ was taken due to the minimal discrepancy. From this, the Moon’s completed cycles <term>bhagaṇa: a full 360-degree revolution through the zodiac</term> and remaining parts over one solar year are determined as: $13\text{ cycles, } 4\text{ signs, } 12^\circ, 46', 30'', 46''', 20''''$.
The true Moon in cycles and parts is: $\frac{57753300000}{4210000000} = \frac{192511}{14400} =$

<column-break/>

<header>Chapter 1: Mean Motion</header>
<page-num>17</page-num>

$13\text{ cycles, } 4\text{ signs, } 12^\circ, 46', 30'', 0''', 0''''$. The difference between these two in arc-seconds is a positive (additive) $1, 29, 13, 40$ <note>represented in sexagesimal parts: seconds, thirds, fourths</note>. Likewise, the correction factor <gloss>bījakarma</gloss> in arc-seconds for one year is negative (subtractive) $1130$. The difference between these is a negative $0, 0, 46, 20$, which equals $\frac{139}{3 \times 60 \times 60} = \frac{1}{78}$ for one year. For any desired year, this is $\frac{\text{Years}}{78}$. Thus, the correction for the Moon is proven. In this way, the corrections for all planets should be understood.

### Conclusion of the Chapter on Mean Motion:—
> Thus ends the calculation of the positions of the heavenly bodies <note>original: 'nabhoga-madhya-sādhanam' — literally 'the accomplishment of the middle of the sky-goers'</note>
> in this *Karaṇakutūhala* (The Delight of Calculation) composed by Bhāskara,
> a work beloved by those of refined intellect. || 1 ||

#### Sudhākara:—
The meaning is clear.
> By the son of Śrī Kṛṣṇālu, in this "Ornament of Explanation" <gloss>Vāsanāvibhūṣaṇa</gloss>,
> the excellent explanation of the mathematics of mean planetary positions
> has been completed, endowed with fine sayings and logical proofs.
> Thus concludes the First Chapter on Mean Motion in the *Vāsanāvibhūṣaṇa* commentary on the *Karaṇakutūhala*. || 1 ||

This is the first chapter, "Mean Motion," from the two commentaries by Sumatiharṣa and Sudhākara Dvivedī, edited by Satyendra Mishra.

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of planetary astronomy, moving from the general correction factors (bīja) discussed on the previous page to the specific fixed points of the planetary orbits—the apogees—and the methods for calculating true planetary positions from mean positions.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Positions—2</header>

# Chapter on True Positions—2

**The Apogees—**

The apogee <term>mandocca: the point in a planet's orbit furthest from the Earth where its motion appears slowest</term> of the Sun is 78 degrees <note>original: 'gajādrbhāgā' — gaja (8), adri (7) = 78</note>. For Mars and the others, the values are: 128 and a half; 225; 172 and a half; 81; and 261 respectively. ॥ 1 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa's Commentary:—**

The apogee of the Sun is 78 degrees, which is 2 signs and 18 degrees <note>In Indian astronomy, 1 sign = 30 degrees. Thus 78° = 2 signs (60°) + 18°</note>. The apogee of the Moon was already stated in the section beginning with "The group [of planets] is twofold." 

For Mars <term>Bhauma</term>, the value "128 and a half" <note>original: 'sadalāṣṭasūryāḥ' — sūrya (12), aṣṭa (8), and dala (half)</note> means 128 degrees and 30 minutes, which is 4 signs and 8 degrees and 30 minutes. 

For Mercury <term>Budha</term>, the value "twenty-five and two hundred" <note>original: 'tattvāśvinaḥ' — tattva (25), aśvin (2)</note> means 225 degrees, which is 7 signs and 15 degrees. 

For Jupiter <term>Guru</term>, the value "half-added two, seven, and one" <note>original: 'sārddhayama-adri-candrāḥ' — yama (2), adri (7), candra (1)</note> means 172 degrees and 30 minutes, which is 5 signs, 22 degrees, and 30 minutes. 

For Venus <term>Śukra</term>, the value is 81 degrees <note>original: 'kvaṣṭau' — ku (1), aṣṭa (8)</note>, which is 2 signs and 21 degrees. 

For Saturn <term>Śani</term>, there are two readings of the text. The reading "moon, limbs, and twins" <note>original: 'śaśāṅka-aṅga-yamāḥ' — śaśāṅka (1), aṅga (6), yama (2)</note> gives 261 degrees, which is 8 signs and 21 degrees. Alternatively, there is a reading "elephants, fire, and twins" <note>original: 'matanga-agni-yamāḥ' — matanga (8), agni (3), yama (2)</note> which gives 238 degrees.

The reason for this is explained in the *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi*: In the school that holds there are 54 revolutions <term>bhagaṇa</term> of Saturn's apogee in a Kalpa <term>an immense cycle of time</term>, the reading 238 is used. In the school that holds there are 41 revolutions <term>kusāgara</term>, the value 261 is produced. The school asserting 54 revolutions follows the *Ārya Siddhānta* <note>The astronomical system of Āryabhaṭa</note>, as it is said: "The apogees of the Sun and these others were created by Brahmā..." and so on. Similarly, in the *Sūrya Siddhānta*, the value for Saturn's apogee is given as 7 signs, 28 degrees, and 37 minutes, which agrees with Āryabhaṭa. 

However, in the *Mṛgāṅka Karaṇa Pradīpa* and other commentaries, the value 238 is adopted. This is expressed in the mnemonic verse: "Planetary positions: mountains, elephants, kings, and zero (7-28-56-0)... the apogee of Saturn is elephants, fire, and twins (238)." The Teacher has accepted the value 238 for Saturn as a specific variant of his own school. As it is said:

---
1. "Elephants, fire, and twins" (238) is a textual variant.

<column-break/>

> "Astronomy is a traditional science <term>āgama-śāstra</term>; it is not proper for us to argue when there is disagreement. We should not simply choose by our own will, but I shall state the opinions of many." ॥ 1 ॥

->**Values of the Apogees**<-

| Sun | Mars | Merc. | Jup. | Ven. | Sat. |
|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| 2 | 4 | 7 | 5 | 2 | 8 |
| 18 | 8 | 15 | 22 | 21 | 21 |
| 0 | 30 | 0 | 30 | 0 | 0 |
| 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
<note>The rows represent: Signs, Degrees, Minutes, and Seconds of arc.</note>

**Sudhākara's Commentary:—**
This is for the sake of clarity. Since the apogees are essentially stationary <note>They move extremely slowly over millennia</note>, the Teacher calculated and recorded them for his own time. The underlying logic is clear.

**The Fast Points (Conjunctions)—**

The values are 81, 44, 23, 87, and 13 respectively. For Mars and the others, the mean Sun is the "Fast Point" <term>śīghrocca: for outer planets, this point corresponds to the Sun's position</term> for Jupiter, Mars, and Saturn. ॥ 2 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa's Commentary:—**

81, 44, 23, 87, and 13 are the maximum values <term>parākhyāḥ</term> for Mars and the others. These are the "Greatest Equations" <term>parama-phala</term> in the form of a Sine <term>jyā</term>. Their corresponding arcs—the maximum "Fast Corrections"—are: for Mars 42° 40'; for Mercury 22° 30'; for Jupiter 11° 0'; for Venus 46° 15'; and for Saturn 6° 15'. 

Now he describes the "Fast Point" <term>śīghrocca</term> of the planets: The Fast Points for Mercury and Venus were already stated in the chapter on mean motion. For the others—Jupiter, Mars, and Saturn—the Mean Sun should be understood as their Fast Point. ॥ 2 ॥

**Positive and Negative Values of the Slow and Fast Anomalies—**

By subtracting the planet from its Slow Apogee and Fast Point, the Slow Anomaly and Fast Anomaly are produced. These should be divided into quadrants of three signs each. The resulting correction <term>phala</term> is positive or negative depending on whether the anomaly starts in Aries or Libra. ॥ 3 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa's Commentary:—**

When the Mean Planet (purified by corrections for longitude and the *bīja* factor) is subtracted from its Slow Apogee <term>mandocca</term> and its Fast Point <term>śīghrocca</term>, the results are respectively the Slow Anomaly <term>manda-kendra</term> and the Fast Anomaly <term>śīghra-kendra</term>. The twelve signs of the zodiac are divided into four quadrants <term>pada</term>. The first and third quadrants are called "odd," while the second and fourth are...

The previous page established the fixed values for the apogees (points of slowest motion); this page details how to handle the "anomaly" (the distance between the planet and its apogee) to find the "sine" components needed for final calculations. It specifically introduces a rare correction for the apogee of Mars and provides a table of sine differences.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>In the Karaṇakutūhala</header>

The term for this is "equal." If the anomaly <term>kendra: the angular distance of a planet from its apogee</term> is within the six signs starting from Aries <note>the first 180 degrees of the orbit</note>, the resulting correction is added to the mean planet to find the corrected-mean position; if the anomaly is in the six signs starting from Libra <note>180 to 360 degrees</note>, the correction is subtracted. ॥ 3 ॥

### Calculating the Base and Perpendicular Components—

If the anomaly is less than three signs <note>90 degrees</note>, it is the base <term>bhujā: the sine-related arc</term>. If it is greater than three signs, subtract it from six signs <note>180 degrees</note>. If it is greater than six signs, subtract six signs from it. If it is greater than nine signs, subtract it from twelve signs <note>the full 360-degree circle</note>. To find the perpendicular <term>koṭi: the cosine-related arc</term>, subtract the base from three signs <note>90 degrees</note>. ॥ 4 ॥

### Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:—

If the anomaly is less than three signs, that value itself is the base. If the anomaly is above three signs, then six signs minus that value is the base. If the anomaly is more than six signs, then the anomaly minus six signs is the base. For an anomaly greater than nine signs, subtract the anomaly from twelve signs to get the base. Furthermore, three signs minus the base shall be the perpendicular. ॥ 4 ॥

### Correction of the Apogees of Mars and Others:—

For the fast anomaly <term>śīghra-kendra: an anomaly used to account for the Earth's own motion</term> of Mars and Venus, take the smaller of the "elapsed" or "remaining" minutes of the current quadrant and divide by four hundred <note>original: 'khakha-veda' — kha (0), kha (0), veda (4) = 400</note>. The resulting degrees should be subtracted from or added to the apogee of Mars when the anomaly is in the signs starting with Cancer or Capricorn respectively, to make it "true." ॥ 5 ॥ 
The "other" true value for the Son of the Earth <note>Mars</note> is found by subtracting one-third of the resulting degrees. ॥ 5 1/2 ॥

### Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:—

Regarding the calculation of the fast anomaly of Mars: one should take the fast anomaly derived from the corrected-mean position. As stated in the *Siddhāntasiromaṇi*: "The corrected-mean position is calculated first, then the fast anomaly." Also in the *Narapati*: "The result of the apogee should be calculated as before... the apogee corrected by that becomes perfectly true."

This same method was demonstrated by Lakṣmīdāsa, Mṛgāṅka, and others. Therefore, the opinion of the ancient teachers—that one first calculates the result using the mean apogee and then calculates it again from the true apogee—is also our preferred view. 

Now, the fast anomaly is divided into quadrants of three signs each. One looks at the "elapsed" portion of the quadrant or the "remaining" portion (the quadrant minus the elapsed amount). Whichever is smaller is converted into minutes and divided by 400. The result in degrees and minutes is the correction. If the fast anomaly of Mars is in the six signs beginning with Cancer, the correction is subtracted from Mars's apogee; if it is in the six signs beginning with Capricorn, it is added. This results in the "true" apogee of Mars. Furthermore, by subtracting one-third of this correction from the result, the "other" true value for Mars is established. ॥ 5, 5 1/2 ॥

---

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Positions—2</header>

### Sudhākara’s Commentary:—

This is clear. Here is the mathematical proof: The maximum "fast-results" for Mars and the others are calculated based on a radius of 120; these are the values traditionally taught. 

Now, the formula for the correction related to Mars's apogee is:
= (20 × degrees × 2) / (75 × 3) 
= (20 × degrees × 60 × 2) / (75 × 3 × 60) 
= (20 × 2 × minutes of arc) / (75 × 3 × 60)
= (2 × minutes of arc) / (85 × 3 × 3) = (2 × minutes of arc) / 765, which is approximately (minutes of arc) / 383.
For simplicity, the text uses (minutes of arc) / 400. Thus, the root text is justified. Similarly, the actual circumference is derived from the calculated degrees... [and] all is consistent with what has been stated. ॥ 5 1/2 ॥

### Determining the Sines—

Twenty-one, twenty, nineteen, seventeen, fifteen, twelve, nine, five, and two. ॥ 6 ॥
These are the sine-differences <term>jyākhaṇḍa: segments used to calculate the sine of an angle</term> in terms of degrees for every ten degrees. The "passed" segments are summed, then the remaining degrees are multiplied by the "current" segment and divided by ten <note>original: 'kh-endu' — kha (0), indu (1) = 10</note>. Adding this to the sum of the passed segments gives the total sine. ॥ 7 ॥

### Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:—

The nine segments are: 21, 20, 19, 17, 15, 12, 9, 5, and 2.

->**Sine Segments Table**<-

| 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 | 9 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 21 | 20 | 19 | 17 | 15 | 12 | 9 | 5 | 2 |
| **21** | **41** | **60** | **77** | **92** | **104** | **113** | **118** | **120** |

<note>The first row shows the individual differences; the second row shows the cumulative sine totals for every 10 degrees up to 90.</note>

These are the nine sine-segments. To find the sine of a desired angle, convert the angle into degrees and divide by ten. The quotient gives the number of "passed" segments. The remainder, in degrees and minutes, is multiplied by the next segment (the "current" segment), divided by ten, and added to the sum of the previously passed segments. This total is the sine. If the degrees are less than ten, the first segment is used for the calculation. ॥ 7 ॥

This section moves from those base components to finding the corresponding arc and calculating the "Equation of Center"—the primary correction needed to find a planet's true position in the sky.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>In the Karaṇakutūhala</header>

### Determining the Arc—

Subtract the segments from the remainder multiplied by ten; the unpurified quotient obtained shall be the arc in degrees and so on. This should be added to ten multiplied by the number of segments already subtracted. With the segments reversed, the reverse arc and sine are also found. ॥ 8 ॥

### Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:—

To find the arc <term>dhanu: the angular measure or inverse sine</term> of a given sine <term>jyā: the chord-half used in Indian trigonometry</term>, one begins with the first segment and subtracts as many segments as possible. From the remainder, multiplied by ten and divided by the "unpurified" segment <note>the next segment in the table that was too large to be fully subtracted</note>, the resulting degrees are added to the number of fully subtracted segments (each representing ten degrees). This gives the arc in degrees. 

Now, if the segments—25, 5, 12, 15, 17, 19, 20, 21—are used in reverse order, the "inverse arc" and sine can be found through the same method. However, there is no practical need for that here; it is mentioned only in passing. 

Furthermore, because of the small margin of error and for the sake of simplicity, even though the teacher <note>Bhāskara II</note> has already spoken, the clarification of the "current sine difference" <term>bhogya-khaṇḍa: the segment of the sine table currently being traversed</term> is found in the *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi* <note>Bhāskara II's foundational theoretical work, written around 1150 CE</note>:—

> "The difference between the past and future segments, multiplied by the remaining degrees and divided by twenty, is added to or subtracted from half the sum of the past and future segments. This results in the 'clear' or corrected future segment to be used in calculating the sine or its inverse."

For example, when calculating the sine for 24 degrees: dividing 24 by ten gives a quotient of 2 and a remainder of 4. The "past" segment is 20 and the "future" segment is 19. From their sum (39), half is 19;30. Because this is a standard sine calculation, subtracting the correction yields 19;14 as the "clear" future segment. Using this corrected segment in the calculation, the sine is found to be 48;43. This should be known as the sine of the maximum declination <note>the tilt of the Earth's axis, approximately 24 degrees in this system</note>. 

Now, for finding the arc from the sine, the method for correcting the future segment according to the *Siddhāntas* is:—

> "Subtract the segments from the remainder; multiply by the difference between the past and future segments, and divide by the future segment. The result is added or subtracted to the future/past segment to find the corrected future segment for the purpose of determining the arc."

By this method, the difference between past and future is 1. Multiplied by the remaining degrees 4, it is 4. Divided by twenty, it is 0;12. Following the rule "The result is added or subtracted...", we find: for the sine of the maximum declination 48;43, the subtracted segments total 29;20, leaving a remainder of 7;43. Half of this is 3;52. The difference between past and future (1) is multiplied by 3;52 and divided by the future segment (19), yielding 0;12. This is subtracted from half the sum of past and future (19;30), resulting in 19;18 as the "clear" future segment. Using this in the rule for the "unpurified quotient," the arc is found to be 24 degrees. This refined method should be used by those desiring high precision. ॥ 8 ॥

<column-break/>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Planets—2</header>

### Sudhākara:—The explanation for clarity and the logic are simple.

### Calculating the Equation of Center—

The sine of the mean anomaly of the Sun and other planets should be multiplied by two and then divided by: 550 (for the Sun); 238 (for the Moon); 107 (for Mars); 198 (for Mercury); 228 (for Jupiter); 784 (for Venus); and 157 (for Saturn). ॥ 9 ॥
The resulting degrees are added to or subtracted from the planet's position depending on the anomaly. For the Sun and Moon, this makes them "clear" <note>true/accurate</note>; for the others (Mars, etc.), they become "corrected-mean" positions. ॥ 10 ॥

### Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:—

"The Sun and others..." One must calculate the base-sine <term>bhuja-jyā</term> of the mean anomaly <term>manda-kendra: the angular distance from the planet's apogee</term> of the Sun and other planets. This sine is multiplied by two (which is the same as multiplying by ten and dividing by five). 

If it is for the Sun, divide by 550. The resulting correction in degrees, etc., is 1;34;32. When this is applied to the Mean Sun (1s 1° 33' 26"), the Sun corrected by the Equation of Center becomes 1s 3° 7' 58". From this, the correction for terrestrial latitude <term>cara: ascensional difference</term> is subtracted (86), resulting in the True Sun: 1s 3° 6' 32".

If that sine is for the Moon, divide by 238. 
For Mars <note>Bhauma</note>, divide by 107. 
For Mercury <note>Budha</note>, divide by 198. 
For Jupiter <note>Guru</note>, divide by 228. 
For Venus <note>Śukra</note>, divide by 784. 
For Saturn <note>Śani</note>, divide by 157. 

These degree-corrections are added or subtracted based on the anomaly: if the anomaly is in the half-circle starting with Aries (0–180°), the correction is added; if in the half-circle starting with Libra (180–360°), it is subtracted from the mean planet. By doing this, the Sun and Moon become "clear" (their true positions). For the others, like Mars, they are called "corrected-mean" <term>manda-sphuṭa</term> because they still require the second correction (the Equation of Conjunction). 

The maximum Equation of Center <term>parama-mandaphala</term> in minutes for each is: 
Sun: 130;50; Moon: 302;31; Mars: 672;54; Mercury: 362;10; Jupiter: 315;43; Venus: 110;0; Saturn: 458;33. ॥ 10 ॥

### Sudhākara’s Commentary:—

This is for the sake of clarity. Here is the mathematical logic. Having brought the maximum equations of center according to the rules of the *Siddhāntas*, a proportion is made. For example, for the Sun: if for a base-sine of 120, the maximum correction in degrees is obtained, then for the desired sine, what is obtained? 
The Sun's Equation of Center = $\frac{\text{sine} \times 130}{120 \times 60} = \frac{\text{sine} \times 10}{554}$ 
Here, the equation is $\frac{120}{60}$. Because this is slightly more, a simplified integer is used for the divisor to provide a very close approximation...

This page applies those trigonometric values to correct the Moon's position based on the Sun and introduces the "Equation of Motion"—the correction for a planet's daily speed—as well as the complex "Equation of Conjunction" for the five star-planets.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>In the Karaṇakutūhala</header>

The logic for all these calculations is to be understood as follows: after performing the division of the sine's multiplier by ten, the remainder is indeed clear. ॥ 10 ॥

### Finding the True Rising from the Mean Sun’s Rising—

The Sun’s Equation <note>original: "bhānoḥ phalaṃ"; the correction needed to move from mean to true position</note> is multiplied by twenty-seven and applied to the Mean Moon; the Sun’s increase or decrease is likewise applied. ॥ 10 ½ ॥

**Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:—**
Regarding the phrase "of the Sun": divide the sine by five hundred and fifty <note>550</note>. The result is the Sun’s Equation. When this is divided by twenty-seven, the resulting degrees and minutes should be added to or subtracted from the Mean Moon. If the Sun's Equation is positive, add it to the Mean Moon; if it is negative, subtract it. Then the Moon becomes "corrected." This is not done for the other planets because the difference would be negligible. ॥ 10 ½ ॥

**Sudhākara’s Commentary:—**
Here is the mathematical proof: If the equatorial rising <note>nirakṣodaya: the time it takes for a sign to rise at the Earth's equator</note> is obtained for thirty degrees, what then for the degrees of the Sun’s Equation? Furthermore, if the Moon’s motion in degrees is obtained for thirty-six hundred water-measures <term>pala: a unit of time equal to 24 seconds</term>, what is obtained by the previously calculated time-units? The form of this correction in degrees is as follows:

-> (Eq. Rising × Sun’s Eq. × Moon’s Motion) <-
-> -------------------------------------- <-
-> 30 × 3600 × 60 <-

Since mean values are used everywhere here, the equatorial rising is taken as equal to thirty hundred <note>3000</note> palas. Similarly, the Moon’s motion in minutes is taken as eight hundred <note>800</note> for the sake of simplicity. Therefore, the rearranged form of the correction is:

-> 3000 × 800 × 2 × Equation <-
-> ------------------------- <-
-> 30 × 3600 × 60 <-

-> 8 × 2 × Equation &nbsp;&nbsp; Sun’s Eq. <-
-> ---------------- = --------- <-
-> 36 × 6 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 27 <-

Thus, it is proven. ॥ 10 ½ ॥

### The Equation of Motion for the Sun and Other Planets—

For the Sun, multiply the current sine segment <term>bhogya-khaṇḍa: the specific segment of the sine table the planet is currently in</term> by twenty and divide by nine hundred; for the Moon, multiply by thirteen and divide by four. ॥ 11 ॥

For Mars and Mercury, multiply by two and divide by seven. For Jupiter and the others, divide by fifty, twelve, and one hundred and twenty respectively. This is the Equation of Motion; it is added or subtracted starting from the signs of Cancer or Capricorn respectively. ॥ 12 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:—**
For whichever planet one seeks the "Equation of Daily Motion" <term>bhukti-phala</term>, if one is calculating for the Sun, take the current sine segment of its "base" <term>bhuja-jyā: the sine of the anomaly</term>, divide it by nine, and the resulting minutes are the Sun’s Equation of Motion. Similarly, for the Moon, take its current sine segment, multiply by thirteen, and divide by four. For Mars, multiply by two and divide by seven.

<column-break/>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Corrections—2</header>

This result is the Equation of Motion for Mars. For Mercury, use the segment as is <note>divided by 7, as per the previous rule</note>. For Jupiter, take its current sine segment and divide by fifty. For Venus, divide the segment by twelve. For Saturn, divide the current sine segment by one hundred and twenty. 

Each respective Equation of Motion is added to the planet’s mean daily motion when the "Slow Anomaly" <term>manda-kendra: the angular distance from the planet's apogee</term> is in the half-circle starting with Cancer. It is subtracted when the anomaly is in the half-circle starting with Capricorn. When this is done, the daily motions of the Sun and Moon become "True" <note>sphuṭa</note>; for Mars and the others, they are called "Slow-Corrected" <note>manda-sphuṭa: corrected for the first anomaly but not yet for the second</term>. ॥ 12 ॥

**Sudhākara’s Commentary:—** Now, for the calculation of the Equation of Motion, the proportion is: If the sine segment is obtained for ten degrees, what is obtained for the motion of the Slow Anomaly in degrees? This is the difference between the sines of today and tomorrow. The resulting Equation of Motion in minutes for the Sun is:

-> Current Seg. × 60 × 90 × 60 &nbsp;&nbsp; Current Seg. <-
-> --------------------------- = ------------ <-
-> 90 × 60 × 551 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 9 <-

The proof for all the other planets should be understood in the same way, making the entire system faultless.

### Calculating the Equation of Conjunction for Mars and Other Planets—

The "perpendicular-sine" <term>koṭijyā: the cosine</term> produced from the "Variable Anomaly" <term>cala-kendra: the anomaly related to the planet's orbit around the Sun</term> is multiplied by its own specific multiplier <note>the planet's specific constant</note> and then doubled. This is added to or subtracted from sixty. 

When the anomaly is in the half-circle starting with Aries or Capricorn, add the square of the multiplier to fourteen thousand four hundred. The square root of that sum is the "Hypotenuse" <term>śravaṇa: the distance from the Earth to the planet</term>. The "base-sine" <term>dorjyā</term> multiplied by the planet's constant and divided by the Hypotenuse gives a value; the arc of that value is the "Fast Correction" <term>śīghra-phala</term>. This should be added to or subtracted from the Slow-Corrected planet to find its True position. ॥ 13 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:—**
Subtract the Slow-Corrected position from the "Fast Apex" <note>śīghrocca: the point of conjunction</note>; the remainder is the "Variable Anomaly." Find the cosine <note>koṭijyā</note> of that anomaly. Multiply it by the planet's specific multiplier, then multiply by two and divide by sixty. Add this to sixty. Then take the square of the planet's multiplier and add it to 14,400. Subtract the doubled cosine from this when the anomaly is in the Cancer half-circle. The square root of this—found by the method "Subtract from the last odd place" described in the *Līlāvatī* <note>the famous mathematical treatise by Bhāskara II</note>—is known as the "Hypotenuse" <term>karṇa</term>. 

Then, the sine <note>dorjyā</note> of the Variable Anomaly is multiplied by the multiplier and divided by the Hypotenuse. Find the arc of this result using the previously mentioned sine segments. This arc is the "Fast Correction." It is added to or subtracted from the Slow-Corrected planet based on whether the anomaly is in the half starting with Aries or Libra. In the Aries half-circle, it is added; in the Libra half-circle, it is subtracted. Then the planet becomes "True" <note>sphuṭa</note>. ॥ 13 ॥

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical methods for determining planetary positions and velocities. The previous page detailed the correction of the Moon's position relative to the Sun. This page focuses on the specific iterative corrections required for Mars and the general calculation for the daily true velocity of all planets.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>In the Karaṇakutūhala</header>

### Finding the True Position of Mars—

> If the mean planet is corrected by the first motion <note>the 'manda' or Equation of Center</note> arising from that, it becomes "more true." For Mars, however, this is done repeatedly, first using half of the two equations and then using the full equations. ॥ 14 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:—**
Regarding the phrase "arising from that": the correction arising from the "manda" <note>Equation of Center</note> is used to correct the mean planet; then it becomes "more true." This means that the corrected position is treated as the new mean position. By calculating the "manda" result again in the manner previously described and applying it to the mean, it becomes more accurate. This is done repeatedly (*asakṛt*) until the value becomes stable. However, there is a special rule for Mars (*Kuja*): first, use half of the "manda" result and half of the "śīghra" <note>Equation of Conjunction</note> result. After correcting with these halved values, one then applies the full "manda" result to the mean, calculates the "śīghra" from that, and applies the full "śīghra" result. This process of using half-values then full values is repeated until no further change occurs. Some claim that this "halving" method applies to Mercury and other planets as well, but that is incorrect, as stated by the astronomer Lakṣmīdāsa. This special rule for the motion of Mars is also mentioned in the *Siddhānta-śiromaṇi*:

> "The mean planet corrected by the Equation of Center becomes the 'manda-corrected' planet. From this, after determining the 'fast anomaly,' the planet becomes 'true' by applying the Equation of Conjunction repeatedly. For Mars, however, one first uses the two equations halved, and then the full equations repeatedly. The Sun and Moon are known to be 'true' by the 'manda' correction alone, as they lack the 'śīghra' <note>conjunction</note> apex."

In this context, the instruction to use "half the results" applies only to Mars and not to the others. Thus, Mars is first corrected by half of the two results, then by the two full results, becoming true through an iterative process until the value is stable. Other planets become true through a single application of the full results. ॥ 14 ॥

**Sudhākara’s Commentary:—**
This is for the sake of clarifying the meaning. Here is the mathematical proof: By assuming the radius <term>trijyā: the radius of the local celestial circle</term> to be 3438, and using the rule **"of one's own cosine and the sine of the maximum equation, the sum in the signs beginning with Capricorn and Cancer, etc."** the derivation of the hypotenuse <note>karṇa</note> is clear. Now, for the proof of the rule "the remainder of the root plus one, multiplied by sixty":
Let the non-square number = *A*. Let its approximate integer root = *r*. Let the actual root = *x + r*. Then:
(*x + r*)$^2$ = *A*
Or, *x*$^2$ + 2*xr* + *r*$^2$ = *A*
Alternatively, *x*$^2$ + 2*xr* = remainder (*rem*). Therefore:
2*x*$^2$ + 2*xr* = *x*$^2$ + *rem*. Therefore:
*x* = $\frac{x^2 + rem}{2x + 2r}$. 
Here, for a small difference, if we substitute 1 in place of *x*,
the result is *x* = $\frac{1 + rem}{2 + 2r}$. This explains why it is multiplied by sixty to find the fractional parts. ॥ 14 ॥

---
<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Positions—2</header>

### Clarifying the True Motion <note>Velocity</note>—

> The mean motion corrected by the [manda] equation is the "manda-motion" <note>the planet's speed corrected for its elliptical orbit</note>. That motion, when subtracted from the motion of the planet's own "fast-anomaly" <note>śīghra-kendra</note>, becomes the speed of the anomaly. ॥ 15 ॥

> The motion of the "fast-anomaly" is multiplied by the "sine-difference" of the conjunction and by forty, then divided by seven times the hypotenuse. This result should be subtracted from the motion of the "fast-apex" <note>the Sun's mean position for planets</note>; the result is the specific true motion of the planet. ॥ 16 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:—**
Regarding "fast-anomaly": one should subtract the "manda-corrected" motion from the motion of the "fast-apex" <note>śīghrocca-bhukti</note>; the remainder is the motion of the "fast-anomaly." This is multiplied by the "sine-difference" of the śīghra-arc <note>the rate of change of the equation of conjunction</note>. Then it is multiplied by forty <note>khābdhi</note> and divided by seven times the value called the hypotenuse (*karṇa*). The resulting minutes and seconds are subtracted from the motion of the "fast-apex." The remainder is the True Motion (*sphuṭa-bhukti*). If the result is greater than the motion of the "fast-apex" so that it cannot be subtracted, then by reverse subtraction, the motion of the "fast-apex" is subtracted from the result. The remaining value is the True Motion, and in that case, "retrograde motion" (*vakra-gati*) should be understood. This means the planet is moving backward. Some perform this calculation for motion repeatedly, but that is incorrect, because the motion is only determined in the final step, and the previous motion is of no use. There is a special point here: when a planet is at its maximum "śīghra" result, its mean motion is the same as its true motion, as stated in the *Siddhānta-śiromaṇi*:

> "At the intersection of the center of the orbit and the transverse line of the eccentric circle, the mean motion is indeed the true motion; that is where the planet's equation is at its maximum."

When the planet is at its "fast-apex" <note>conjunction</note>, its true "fast-motion" is said to be at its maximum. ॥ 16 ॥

<summary>This page explains the iterative mathematical correction required to find the true position of Mars and provides formulas for calculating a planet's true daily velocity, including the conditions for retrograde motion.</summary>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>In the Karaṇakutūhala</header>

**Sudhākara:** — Here, the true velocity should be understood through the "quick arc" <note>original: *āśu-cāpena*, referring to the arc of the Śīghra or conjunction equation</note>; the rest is self-explanatory.

**The Mathematical Proof:** — If the "sine segment to be traversed" <note>term: *bhogya-khaṇḍa* - the difference between two entries in a sine table used for interpolation</note> is for ten degrees, then what is it for the motion of the anomaly? The result is the difference between the sines of the Equation of Conjunction <note>term: *śīghra-phala*</note> and the greater anomaly. If that difference is for the hypotenuse <note>term: *karṇa*</note>, what is it for the radius? Then, for the sake of the arc, if ten degrees are obtained by the first sine segment, what is obtained by the currently calculated value? Thus, the clear velocity of the anomaly <note>term: *kendra-gati*</note> is produced in minutes.

Tabular Diff. × Anomaly Motion × 120 × 90 × 60 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Tabular Diff. × Anomaly Motion × 40
<br>----------------------------------------------------------- = ----------------------------------------------------
<br>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 90 × Conjunction Hypotenuse × 21 × 60 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 7 × Conjunction Hypotenuse

Therefore, it is proven as stated: "multiplied by the sine of the difference between the degrees of the equation and ninety," etc. The same geometric figure used in that proof should be understood here. The rest of the proof is very clear. ॥ 16 ॥

### Calculation of the Precession of the Equinoxes (Ayanāṃśa)—

->Now, the Precession of the Equinoxes is the number of minutes equal to the years elapsed since the epoch of this handbook, added to eleven <note>original: *bhavāḥ*, a code word for 11</note>. This total is added to the mean Sun. ॥ 17 ॥<-

**Sumatiharṣa:** —

To ensure success <note>literally "to bring about bowing" or "salutation"</note> or for an auspicious beginning, the author states: The minutes (*kalā*) equal to the years elapsed since the epoch of this handbook <note>term: *karaṇābdāḥ* - the years since the composition of the Karaṇakutūhala in 1183 CE</note> are added to "eleven" (*bhava*) degrees to become the Precession of the Equinoxes <note>term: *ayanāṃśa* - the shift between the sidereal and tropical zodiacs</note>. The specific detail here is that at the start of the epoch, the Precession was 11 degrees and 24 seconds. The author has taken only the 11 degrees as the base. 

Now for the method: in this system, the Precession increases by one minute every year. In that case, the maximum Precession is 30 degrees. In other systems where 54 seconds are produced per year, the maximum Precession is 27 degrees. As stated in the commentary on the *Sūrya Siddhānta*: "One minute per year; one degree in sixty years; one sign <note>30 degrees</note> in 1,800 years; one full revolution <note>21,600 minutes</note> in 21,600 years. In the years of a *Catur-yuga* <note>4,320,000 years</note>, two hundred revolutions occur."

Therefore, in the *Sūrya Siddhānta*, the shifting degrees reach a limit of 30 degrees. During the eastward movement, the Precession is added by calculators and never subtracted. Currently, due to being in an odd-numbered sign, the movement is eastward, and the degrees traversed are the increasing Precession. When the westward movement occurs in the future in an even-numbered sign, the traversed degrees are subtracted from 30; thus the remainder is known as the Precession. From the Sun corrected by this, the declination and other values are calculated.

> "The Precession should always be applied by the wise to the Ascendant, the declination, and the 'ascensional difference' (*cara*); also in the 'visibility corrections' (*dṛk-karma*) of the planets when they are with or without a sign. Once the calculation is done, they should be discarded—but they are not discarded during solar eclipses." Further:

---

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>True Position: Chapter 2</header>

> "The time of a planet should be calculated using the longitude with the Precession added, as well as the portion of the sign already traversed."

Thus, it is proven that however many years have passed, that many minutes are the Precession; this is detailed further in other texts. For example, in this handbook, if 436 years have passed, those are 436 minutes. Divided by sixty, this is 7 degrees and 16 minutes. To this degree value, eleven degrees (*bhava*) are added, resulting in 18° 16' 0". Every month since the start of the month of Caitra, five seconds are added. Since two months have passed, ten seconds are added. Thus, the Precession of the Equinoxes is 18° 16' 10". ॥ 17 ॥

**Sudhākara:** — "The minutes equal to the years of the handbook" means the minutes are equal to the years since the start of this *Karaṇa* text. The rest is self-explanatory.

**The Mathematical Proof:** At the start of the text, the Precession was eleven degrees <note>original: *rudra-lava*, where Rudra = 11</note>. Since the motion is one minute per year, the calculation for the Precession is derived from that slight difference.

### Describing the Equation of Time (Udayāntara)—

->The sine of the longitude of the Sun doubled, divided by five, gives the seconds. For the Moon, those are minutes divided by twenty-one.<-
->These are added or subtracted respectively when the Sun is in even or odd quadrants; this operation is called the Equation of Time. ॥ 18 ॥<-

**Sumatiharṣa:** —

Regarding "doubled": The sine of the arm <note>term: *bhuja-jyā*</note> of the mean Sun corrected by the Precession and then doubled, is divided by five (5). The resulting seconds (*vikalā*) are added to or subtracted from the Sun depending on whether the tropical Sun is in an even or odd quadrant. Add when in an even quadrant, subtract when in an odd quadrant. The same sine value divided by twenty-one (21) gives minutes and so on, which are added or subtracted for the Moon just as for the Sun. This operation is called the Equation of Time <note>term: *udayāntara* - the correction for the difference between the mean solar day and the true solar day</note>. It is not performed for other planets because the difference is negligible. 

For example: With the Precession at 18° 16' 10", the mean Sun at 11s 13° 31' 26" <note>s = signs</note> becomes 11s 19° 49' 36" when added. Doubled, it is 3s 9° 39' 12". Its "arm" <note>the distance from the nearest equinox</note> is 2s 20° 20' 48". Its sine is 118' 4". Divided by five (5), the result is 23 seconds. This is the Sun's Equation of Time. Since the tropical Sun is in an odd quadrant, it must be subtracted from the true Sun. Similarly, this same sine 118' 4" divided by twenty-one (21) results in 5 minutes and 37 seconds. This should be subtracted from the Moon just like the Sun. ॥ 18 ॥

**Sudhākara:** — Now, through the rule "The Equation of Time is brought from the mean Sun joined with the Precession, doubled, with the sine multiplied by the velocity and divided by 470..." etc., the operation for the Equation of Time is brought forth. For the Sun:

The previous page discussed the calculation of planetary velocity and the specific corrections needed for the Precession of the Equinoxes and the Equation of Time. This section focuses on calculating the "correction for ascensional difference" and the "Equation of the Equinoxes."</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>In the Karaṇakutūhala</header>

Sine of the Base <note>original: *bhujājyā*</note> × 60 / 270 = Sine of the Base / 5. Thus, the result for the Moon in minutes and seconds is: Sine of the Base × 790 / (270 × 60)

= Sine of the Base / 21. For other planets, the result is stated with a slight difference because of their slower motion. || 18 ||

## The Correction for Ascensional Difference <note>term: *cara-karma* — a correction used to determine the length of day and night based on the observer's latitude</note>

> From the number of days derived from the degrees of precession <note>term: *ayanāṃśa* — the shifting of the equinoxes</note> before the Sun enters Aries <note>term: *meṣa-saṅkrānti*</note>,
> calculate the noon shadow of the equinox at that time. || 19 ||
> Multiply that equinoctial shadow <note>term: *akṣabhā* — the shadow of a 12-digit gnomon at noon on the equinox</note> by ten, eight, and ten.
> Divide the last result by three. These three values are the segments of ascensional difference
> for the Sun, when increased by the degrees of precession. || 20 ||
> Sum the segments corresponding to the number of signs in the Sun's base longitude.
> For the remaining degrees, multiply by the next segment and divide by thirty.
> The resulting "ascensional difference" <note>term: *cara*</note> is added or subtracted depending on the hemisphere <note>term: *gola* — Northern or Southern</note>.
> Multiply the velocity of the planet by these minutes of ascensional difference and divide by sixty; the result is in seconds.
> This is added or subtracted at the time of sunrise, and the reverse for setting planets. || 20 ||

## Commentary by Sumatiharṣa:

Regarding the correction for ascensional difference: Based on the days generated from the degrees of precession <note>original: *ayanāṃśa*</note>, prior to the Sun's entry into Aries, the shadow of a twelve-digit gnomon at noon is the equinoctial shadow. Now, to calculate the days from the precession: take the degrees of precession, subtract one forty-second part of themselves, and the result is the "precession days." 

Alternatively, if the Sun is at the beginning of Aries, multiply the 598 degrees of precession and divide by sixty to obtain the precession days. At noon on that day, the shadow of the twelve-digit gnomon is the equinoctial shadow <note>original: *akṣabhā*</note>. This shadow value is placed in three positions and multiplied by ten, eight, and ten respectively. The last value (multiplied by ten) is then divided by three. These are the three segments of ascensional difference <note>original: *cara-khaṇḍa*</note>. These segments are accurate as long as the equinoctial shadow is eight digits; beyond that, they require further consideration due to increasing variance. 

Then, calculate the "base" <note>original: *bhujā*</note> of the true Sun after adding the degrees of precession. Sum the segments of ascensional difference according to the number of signs in that base. When the Sun (with precession added) is in the six signs starting from Aries (Northern Hemisphere), the correction is negative <note>original: *ṛṇa*</note>. When in the six signs starting from Libra (Southern Hemisphere), it is positive <note>original: *dhana*</note>. 

Then, multiply the planet's velocity by the minutes of ascensional difference calculated earlier and divide by sixty. The result is in seconds <note>original: *vikalā*</note>. This is added or subtracted at sunrise as previously described. For the time of setting, the rule is reversed: what was positive becomes negative, and what was negative becomes positive. Thus the planet is corrected by the ascensional difference. 

There is a special rule mentioned: the addition or subtraction is applied as calculated for sunrise. 

<column-break/>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>Chapter on the True Positions of Planets — 2</header>

At the time of setting, reverse the sign. At noon and midnight, there is no correction for ascensional difference because the planet is on the meridian <note>original: *dakṣiṇottara-vṛtta* — the north-south circle</note>. However, what should be done when the planet is between these four points? If it has moved a certain number of hours from sunrise, or is at noon, or setting, or at midnight, it should be understood that the correction follows the sunrise rule when moved by half the velocity for the setting time, and the reverse is understood everywhere. Having applied a quarter or three-quarters of the motion to reach the meridian, it should be known that the correction is absent at that limit.

Example: Degrees of precession = 18° 16' 10". Multiplied by the Sun's motion 58' 35" and divided by sixty, the days are 17, 50, 17. Alternatively, 18° 16' 10" multiplied by its 1/42 part (0° 26' 5") and subtracted gives 17, 50, 5. On the noon of these days before the Aries ingress, the equinoctial shadow in the city of Shivapuri <note>note: likely the observer's location, possibly near Gwalior or Ujjain</note> is 5 digits and 30 minutes. Placed in three spots and multiplied by 10, 8, and 10, we get 55, 44, and 55. The last divided by three gives the segments: 55, 44, 18. 

The true Sun, corrected for the equation of time, is 9 signs, 2° 7' 34". Adding the precession 18° 16' 10", we get 9 signs, 21° 23' 44". The "base" <note>original: *bhujā*</note> is the same. Since the base is in the 1st sign, we take one segment (55). The remaining degrees are 21° 23' 44". Multiplying these by the next segment (44) and dividing by thirty gives 31' 22". Adding this to the first segment (55) gives a total ascensional difference of 86 minutes and 22 seconds. For simplicity, we drop the 22. Since the Sun (with precession) is in the northern hemisphere, this is negative.

Now, multiply the planetary velocities by these 86 minutes of ascensional difference and divide by sixty to get the correction in seconds:
- **Sun's true motion:** 57' 9" → 42 seconds
- **Moon:** 120 seconds
- **Mars:** 7 seconds
- **Mercury:** 148 seconds
- **Jupiter:** 16 seconds
- **Venus:** 47 seconds
- **Saturn:** 10 seconds
- **Rahu (Lunar Node):** 4 seconds

Except for Rahu, all these are negative. Because Rahu is always retrograde <note>original: *vakriṇi*</note>, the rule is reversed and its correction is positive. Thus, all the true positions for sunrise are calculated and recorded in the instrument. || 20 ||

## Calculation of Lunar Days, Half-Days, Constellations, and Combinations

> Subtract the Sun's longitude from the Moon's. Divide the degrees by twelve for the Lunar Day <note>term: *tithi*</note> and by six for the Half-Lunar Day <note>term: *karaṇa*</note>. || 21 ||
> Starting from the "Bava" half-day, the four fixed half-days like "Shakuni" occur in the dark half of the month.
> Multiply the planet's minutes by the minutes of the Sun and Moon, and divide by 800 for the Constellation <note>term: *nakṣatra*</note> and Combination <note>term: *yoga*</note>. || 22 ||
> Divide the remaining minutes by their respective velocities to find the time passed or remaining in Ghatis <note>term: *ghati* — a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note>. || 22 ||

## Commentary by Sumatiharṣa:
Subtract the Sun's position from the Moon's. Place the resulting degrees in two places: divide one by twelve and the other by six...

It provides a specific example for the city of Shivapuri and lists the resulting corrections for all major planets, including the reversal for the retrograde node Rahu.</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for planetary and calendrical calculations. While the previous page dealt with the correction for the Earth's curvature (ascensional difference), this page transitions into calculating the basic units of the Hindu calendar—the Lunar Day (Tithi), the Half-Lunar Day (Karana), the Lunar Mansion (Nakshatra), and the Yoga—as well as the distances of celestial bodies from the Earth.</meta>

<page-num>32, 33</page-num>
<header>In the Karaṇakutūhala, Section on Clarity — 2</header>

The **Lunar Days** <note>term: *tithi* — a lunar day, defined by the Moon's elongation from the Sun in 12-degree increments</note> are obtained by dividing the difference [between the Moon and Sun] by twelve, starting from the first day of the bright fortnight. As it is said:
> “When the Moon departs from the Sun and travels toward the East, 
> ->its status as a 'Lunar Measure' is known by twelve-degree increments.”<-

The **Half-Lunar Days** <note>term: *karaṇa* — there are 11 Karanas in a lunar month; 7 are repeating and 4 are fixed</note> are declared by dividing by six and subtracting one. These begin with *Bava* from the second half of the first lunar day of the bright fortnight. From the second half of the fourteenth day of the dark fortnight, the four fixed Karanas—*Shakuni* and the others—occur. As it is said:
> From the latter half of the fourteenth day, the four beginning with *Shakuni* occur: 
> *Shakuni*, *Chatushpada*, *Naga*, and *Kinstughna*. From the first half of the first lunar day, they begin with *Bava*.

Regarding **Planetary Minutes**: to find the **Lunar Mansion** <note>term: *nakshatra* — one of 27 segments of the ecliptic, each 800 minutes in arc</note> of any planet, its minutes are divided by eight hundred. The quotient represents the number of mansions passed, starting from *Ashwini*. Similarly, the sum of the Sun’s and Moon’s minutes is divided by eight hundred to find the elapsed **Yogas** <note>term: *yoga* — a period where the joint motion of the Sun and Moon covers 800 minutes</note>, such as *Vishkambha* and others. 

In all cases, the remainder from the division is the elapsed portion. That elapsed portion subtracted from the divisor (the total minutes of the segment) gives the remaining portion. The elapsed and remaining seconds are divided by the daily motion <note>term: *bhukti*</note> to find the elapsed and remaining **Ghatikas** <note>term: *ghaṭikā* — a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note>. A specific rule applies: if the calculation uses total minutes, the remaining time is found by dividing by the velocity in seconds.

Just as the time for Lunar Days and Half-Lunar Days is obtained by dividing by the difference between the Sun's and Moon’s motion, the time for a Lunar Mansion is obtained by dividing by the planet’s own motion, and the time for a *Yoga* is obtained by the sum of the Sun’s and Moon’s motions.

**Example:**
The Sun is at 1s 3° 6' 12" and the Moon is at 0s 8° 47' 21". The difference is 11s 5° 41' 59". In degrees, this is 335° 41' 59". Dividing by twelve, the quotient is 27. Thus, 27 lunar days have passed, meaning we are in the 12th day of the dark fortnight <note>original: *kṛṣṇa-dvādaśī*</note>. The remainder 11° 41' 59" has passed of the 13th day. Subtracting this from the total (12°) gives 0° 18' 51" remaining. The elapsed seconds are 2,524,140, and the remaining are 67,460 <note>note: These are large-scale sexagesimal calculations typical of Indian astronomy</note>. Using the difference in motion between the Sun and Moon (75,152 seconds), and dividing by the specific divisor (46,912), the elapsed time is 53 hours and 46 minutes (Ghatikas). The remaining time is 1 hour and 26 minutes (Ghatikas). 

On the calendar <note>original: *tithipatra* — an almanac or Pañcāṅga</note>, it shows 54;20. Why is there a difference for the 13th day? The reason is given: due to the end of the month, the thirty-four minutes of the "Rama-correction" <note>term: *rāma-bīja* — a specific corrective parameter used by the author to reconcile old tables with observed positions</note> are subtractive. The lunar day calculated from the Moon, when corrected by this "Rama-correction," is more accurate by two hours. For example, on this day the Sun is 1s 3° 6' 12". The corrected Moon is 9s 7° 24'. Thus, the lunar day is the 14th (Chaturdashi), and the time is 55;9.

For the New Moon <note>original: *amāvasyā*</note>, using the thirty-four minute Rama-correction, the clear Sun is 1s 4° 5' 29" with a motion of 57' 28". The Moon is 0s 3° 17' 16" with a motion of 840' 50". From these, the New Moon time is 50;51;9. The calendar shows 48;45. The difference should be understood as resulting from the Rama-correction and nothing else. I have stated this in detail for the 

<column-break/>

instruction of students; the wise will understand it themselves. 

Now for the **Half-Lunar Day** <note>term: *karaṇa*</note> calculation: The degrees of the Sun and Moon's difference are 3,352. Dividing by six, the quotient is 55. Subtracting one gives 54. Dividing by seven, the remainder is 5. Counting from *Bava*, five Karanas have passed, and the sixth, *Vanija*, is currently occurring. Its elapsed portion is 5° 41' 9" and the remaining is 0° 18' 51". The elapsed seconds (1,228,140) divided by the difference in motion (46,912) gives 26 hours and 10 minutes (Ghatikas). The remaining seconds (67,860) divided by the same gives 1 hour and 26 minutes (Ghatikas).

Now for the **Moon's Mansion** <note>term: *nakshatra*</note>: The Moon is at 0s 8° 47' 21", which is 527' 21". Dividing by eight hundred (800), the quotient is 0. Thus, it is in the first mansion, *Ashwini*. The elapsed minutes are 527' 21". Dividing the elapsed seconds (1,898,460) by the Moon's motion (50,360) gives 37 hours and 41 minutes (Ghatikas) elapsed. The remaining minutes (272' 39") divided by the motion gives 19 hours and 29 minutes remaining.

Now for the **Sun**: It is at 1s 3° 6' 12", or 1,986 minutes. Dividing by 800, two mansions have passed from *Ashwini*. It is currently in the third, *Kruttika*. The remainder is 386' 12". To find the time passed: if we calculate the time for the Sun to traverse the quarter-segments <note>term: *pāda* — a 'foot' or quarter of a Nakshatra</note> of 3,448 seconds, the Sun has spent 3 days, 14 hours, and 24 minutes in the second quarter of *Kruttika*. The remaining time in that segment is 0 days, 14 hours, and 24 minutes. In this way, the elapsed and remaining days and hours for all planets should be calculated by one's own intelligence. 

Now for the **Yoga**: The sum of the Sun and Moon is 1s 11° 53' 33". Converting to minutes and dividing by 800, the quotient is 3. The current Yoga is the fourth, *Saubhagya*. The remainder is 113' 33" elapsed and 686' 27" remaining. Using the sum of their motions (89,865;48) as before, the remaining time is 45 hours and 55 minutes (Ghatikas).

# Conclusion of the Section on True Positions

> Thus, in this "Wonder of Planetary Astronomy" <note>original: *Grāhāgame Kutūhale*</note> spoken by Bhāskara, which is beloved by the wise, the calculation for the true positions of the heavens is always established. ॥ 2 ॥

**Sudhākara:** The meaning is clear. Through the logic of the established astronomical systems <note>term: *siddhānta* — a formal treatise on Indian astronomy</note>, the underlying explanation <note>term: *vāsanā* — the geometric or physical "rationale" behind a mathematical rule</note> of all these methods is also very easy to follow. ॥ 19-22 ॥

> In this work composed by the son of Śrī Kṛṣṇa, like an excellent ornament of explanations, the rationale for the mathematics of the true planets is joined here with beautiful verses and logical proofs.

->Thus ends the Section on True Positions in the *Jewel of Explanations* of the *Karaṇakutūhala*. ॥ 2 ॥<-

This is the second section, the Section on True Positions, from the commentaries of Sumatiharṣa and Sudhākara Dvivedī, edited by Satyendra Mishra.

<column-break/>

<header>Section on the Three Problems</header>

# Section on the Three Problems—3

## Calculating Local Rising Times and the Ascendant—

> The rising times at the equator <note>term: *lankodaya* — the time it takes for zodiac signs to rise at the Earth's equator</note> are 278, 299, and 323 *vināḍīs* <note>term: *vināḍī* — a unit of time equal to 24 seconds; also called a *pala*</note>. ॥ 1 ॥
> These times, in direct and reverse order, are decreased or increased by one's own local ascensional differences <note>term: *charakhandas* — the variation in rising times caused by the observer's latitude</note>, which are themselves applied in direct and reverse order. ॥ 1 ॥
> This gives the rising times for the six signs starting with Aries at one's local place; the six signs starting with Libra are the same but in reverse order. ॥ 2 ॥
> Take the Sun for the desired time and add the degrees of precession <note>term: *ayanamsha* — the correction for the shifting of the equinoxes</note>. Multiply the remaining degrees of the sign the Sun is in by its own rising time. ॥ 2 ॥
> Divide this by thirty <note>original: *khāgni* — literally "sky-fire," a code for 30</note>; subtract this "remaining time" of the Sun from the desired elapsed time. ॥ 3 ॥
> From that remainder, subtract the rising times of the subsequent signs. Multiply the final remainder by thirty and divide by the rising time of the sign that could not be fully subtracted to get the degrees. ॥ 3 ॥
> Add these degrees to the previous signs passed to find the Ascendant <note>term: *lagna* — the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</note>, then subtract the degrees of precession. ॥ 4 ॥
> If the desired time is less than the Sun's remaining time, divide the time by the rising time, add to the Sun, and that is the Ascendant. ॥ 4 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa:** "Nāga-Turanga-Dastra" <note>a code where Naga=8, Turanga=7, Dastra=2</note> means 278 *palas*, which is the measure of the rising time at the equator for Pisces and Aries. "Go-Aṅka-Ashvin" <note>Go=9, Anka=9, Ashvin=2</note> means 299 for Taurus and Aquarius. "Rāma-Rada" <note>Rama=3, Rada=32</note> means 323 *vināḍīs* or *palas* for Gemini and Capricorn. Then for Cancer and the others, these are used in reverse order. These same equatorial rising times are then "decreased or increased" by the local ascensional differences calculated previously. By doing this, we get the rising times for the six signs starting with Aries at the observer's specific location. Those for the six starting with Libra are the same as these but in reverse order.

Now for the explanation of the three verses [regarding the Ascendant]. Following the method to be described, one calculates the Sun for the specific moment and adds the degrees of precession. Take the degrees the Sun has yet to travel in its current sign (30 minus the degrees already passed) and multiply that by the local rising time of that specific sign. Divide by 30 (*khāgni*). The result is called the "remaining time" of the Sun. Subtract this from the "desired time" (the time elapsed since sunrise). From the remainder, subtract the rising times of the signs that follow in order. When a sign's rising time is larger than the remainder, multiply that remainder by 30 and divide by that sign's rising time to get the degrees. Add these to the count of signs already passed to get the raw Ascendant. Finally, subtract the precession (*ayanamsha*) to get the "clear" or true Ascendant. 

A special note is added regarding calculating planets for a specific moment: "Multiplied by sixty..." means the time passed since or remaining until sunrise should be taken in civil time units. When calculating, the Sun's daily motion should be multiplied by the rising time units...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on spherical astronomy, specifically the methods for calculating the Lagna (the Ascendant or rising sign) and the Iṣṭakāla (the time elapsed since sunrise). It bridges the gap between the general theory of rising times and their practical application in timekeeping.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>In the Karaṇakutūhala</header>

...the value 1800 is divided; when that is added to the original form, the result is the divisor sixty. This is how the direction should be understood. <note>The number 1800 refers to the 30 degrees of a zodiac sign multiplied by 60 minutes.</note> Now, regarding the special rule for finding the "instantaneous" position of the Sun for the sake of the <term>Lagna</term> <note>The point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment.</note>: if the desired time is in <term>Sāvana</term> <note>Terrestrial or solar time, measured from sunrise to sunrise.</note> units, then the instantaneous position must be used; if it is sidereal time, then it is not required. As stated in the *Golādhyāya* <note>The "Chapter on the Celestial Sphere" from Bhāskara's larger work, the *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi*.</note>:

> "If the desired minutes for the Ascendant are terrestrial (*Sāvana*), they should be converted using the calculation for the instantaneous Sun. The sidereal <note>original: *ākṣya*</note> rising times are subtracted from similar values here; but if the time is already sidereal, the instantaneous position is not calculated." ॥ *Tri. Vā.* 27 ॥

---

Now, regarding the current topic of "when the remaining time is small": if the desired time is very short, the following is said—
If the remaining portion of the Sun's sign <note>term: *bhogya* — the portion of a zodiac sign yet to be traversed by a planet.</note> cannot be subtracted from the desired time, then that desired time is multiplied by thirty <note>representing the 30 degrees of a sign</note> and divided by its own rising time to find the degrees and minutes. This, added to the Sun (minus the precession), gives the Ascendant. 

For example, if the desired time is 11 minutes <note>term: *ghaṭī* — a unit of 24 minutes</note>, these are multiplied by the Sun's true motion of 57;28, resulting in 632;8. Divided by sixty, we get 10;32 minutes of arc. This is added to the Sun's position at sunrise (1s 3° 6' 12'') to get the instantaneous Sun at the desired time: 1s 3° 16' 44''. Adding the <term>Ayanāṃśa</term> <note>The value of the precession of the equinoxes, used to convert sidereal longitudes to tropical ones.</note> of 18° 16' 10'', we get 1s 21° 32' 54''. Subtracting this from the 30 degrees of the sign, the remaining portion to be traversed <note>term: *bhogyāṃśa*</note> is 8° 27' 6''. The rising time for Taurus <note>original: *Vṛṣa*</note> is 255 *palas* <note>term: *pala* — a unit of time equal to 24 seconds.</note>. Multiplied by the remaining degrees, it is 2155;10;30. Divided by thirty, the time for the Sun to finish the sign is 71 *palas*. 

If the desired time is 11 *ghaṭīs* (660 *palas*), we subtract the 71 *palas*, leaving 589. From this, the rising time of the next sign, Gemini <note>original: *Mithuna*</note>, which is 305 *palas*, is subtracted, leaving 284. This is multiplied by thirty (8520) and divided by the rising time of the next sign, Cancer <note>original: *Karka*</note>, which is 341 *palas*. The result is 24° 59'. This is added to the signs already passed (Gemini), resulting in 3s 24° 59'. Subtracting the precession, the final Ascendant is 3s 6° 42' 57''. 

Alternatively, if the desired time is small, say 1 *ghaṭī* (60 *palas*), the Sun at that time is 1s 21° 32' 22'' and the remaining time to traverse the sign is 73 *palas*. Since the desired 60 *palas* is less than the 73 *palas* needed to finish the sign, the 60 is multiplied by thirty (1800) and divided by the rising time of Taurus (255), giving 7° 31' 31''. Adding this to the Sun's position and subtracting the precession, the Ascendant for that short time is 1s 10° 10' 43''. ॥ 1-4 ॥

### Calculating the Desired Time from the Ascendant —

->The Sun's remaining time, plus the Ascendant's passed time, added to the rising times of the signs in between, is the time elapsed from the Ascendant. ॥<-
->If the Sun and the Ascendant are in the same sign, the difference in their degrees is multiplied by the rising time and divided by thirty. ॥ 5 ॥<-

<column-break/>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>Section on the Three Problems—3</header>

->If the Ascendant is less than the Sun, the time should be subtracted from the full day and night, or from the night. ॥<-
->For a desired time at night, calculate the Ascendant from the Sun plus six signs, then find the time as before. ॥ 6 ॥<-

**Sumatiharṣa:** Regarding the verse "The Sun's...": The time remaining for the Sun is calculated by multiplying its remaining degrees by the rising time and dividing by thirty. Similarly, the "passed time" of the Ascendant <note>term: *bhuktakāla* — time already spent in the current sign</note> is found by multiplying its passed degrees by its rising time and dividing by thirty. The sum of these two, plus the rising times of the signs situated between the Sun's sign and the Ascendant's sign, gives the desired time from the Ascendant. 

For example, if the Ascendant is 3s 6° 42' 57'' and the <term>Sāyana</term> <note>The position adjusted for precession.</note> Ascendant is 3s 24° 59' 7'', its passed degrees are 24° 59' 7''. Multiplying by the rising time of Cancer (341 *palas*) gives 8519;58;47. Divided by thirty, the passed time of the Ascendant is 283 *palas*. Adding the Sun's remaining time (71) and the Ascendant's passed time (283) equals 354. Adding the rising time of Gemini (305), which lies between the Sun and the Ascendant, the total is 659 *palas*. Dividing by sixty, we get 10 *ghaṭīs* and 59 *palas*. This is the desired time.

If they are in the same sign, the difference in degrees is multiplied by the rising time and divided by thirty to get the desired time. If the Ascendant is less than the Sun <note>implying a full circle has nearly passed</note>, it should be subtracted from sixty *ghaṭīs* to find the time from sunrise. Alternatively, if it is to be subtracted "from the night," it means the time is measured from sunset. 

For example, if the *Sāyana* Ascendant is 1s 28° 26' 53'' and the *Sāyana* Sun is 1s 11° 23' 22'', the difference is 17° 3' 31''. Multiplied by the rising time of Taurus (255) and divided by thirty, the desired time is 59 *palas*. Here, since the Ascendant is greater, this is the time. 

If the observer wants the terrestrial time <note>term: *sāvana*</note>, the instantaneous Sun must be used. If sidereal time is wanted, the Sun at sunrise is used. As it has been said: "If the desired hours of the inquirer are terrestrial, then use the instantaneous Ascendant and Sun; if sidereal, use the values from sunrise."

Now, a method for calculating the "Negative Ascendant" <note>term: *ṛṇa-lagna* — used for calculating time before sunrise or for specific astronomical corrections</note>, though not explicitly mentioned, is described: The passed degrees of the instantaneous tropical Sun are multiplied by the rising time and divided by thirty to get the passed time. This is subtracted from the desired time. Then, the rising times of the preceding signs are subtracted from the remainder. The final remainder is multiplied by thirty and divided by the rising time of the "un-subtracted" sign; this result is then subtracted from that sign and the precession is removed to find the Ascendant. 

For example, if 10 *ghaṭīs* remain in the night, and the Sun is 1s 21° 56' 38'', its tropical position is 1s 21° 12' 38''. Its passed degrees are 21° 12' 48''. Multiplied by the rising time (255) and divided by thirty, the Sun's passed time is 180 *palas*. Subtracting this from the desired 10 *ghaṭīs* (600 *palas*) leaves 420. From this, the rising time of the preceding sign, Aries <note>original: *Meṣa*</note>, which is 223 *palas*, is subtracted, leaving a remainder...
