197 multiplied by thirty is 5910. When divided by the rising time <note>original: *mānapalaiḥ*</note> of 223, the result in degrees and minutes is 26;30,8. When this is subtracted from the value of the sign, it is 11;3,29,52. Then, subtracting the precession <note>term: *ayanāṃśa* — the distance between the vernal equinox and the first point of the sidereal zodiac</note>, the result is 10;15;3,42, which is the calculated <term>Lagna</term>. Now, regarding the method when the desired time is less than the elapsed time: 

When the elapsed time is small, the Sun is multiplied by ten <note>original: *khaguṇā*</note> and divided by its own rising time; the resulting degrees are subtracted to find the Lagna, which is then adjusted by the precession. To derive the desired time from this: the portion of the sign traversed by the Sun is 180. This is multiplied by the specific rising time of Taurus <note>original: *vṛṣa-palaiḥ*</note>, 255, giving 459156. Divided by thirty, the resulting units of time <note>term: *palas* — 1/60th of a Ghaṭī, approximately 24 seconds</note> are 59. Here, since the Lagna and the Sun are in the same sign, this is the desired time. When the seeker's elapsed time is used to find the remaining portion of the body, the remaining degrees of the sign with precession are 11;3,29,52. The remaining degrees, 26;3,8, are multiplied by the rising time of Pisces, 223, giving 5909;59. Divided by thirty, the result is 197. This is the portion of the Sun traversed in the sign. Their sum is 377. The difference from the Lagna to the Sun is the sign of Aries; adding its rising time of 223 gives 600. Divided by sixty, the result is 10 <term>Ghaṭīs</term> <note>A unit of time equal to 24 minutes.</note>. Thus, the remaining time of night is 10 Ghaṭīs. The desired time is subtracted from sixty, resulting in 50 Ghaṭīs elapsed since the previous day's sunrise. When calculating the desired time for the Lagna at night, the Sun should be increased by six signs <note>representing 180 degrees, to find the point exactly opposite the Sun</note>. The calculation for the night-time follows the same method as the daytime, using the Sun increased by six signs. ॥ 6 ॥

### Calculation of Meridian Distance and Altitude —

> The fifteen Ghaṭīs, increased or decreased by the ascensional difference, result in the half-day; the half-night is the reverse in the southern hemisphere. The difference between the half-day and the elapsed Ghaṭīs is the meridian distance; what remains of the half-day after the meridian distance is subtracted is the altitude. ॥ 7 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa's commentary:** Fifteen Ghaṭīs <note>original: *pañcacandra-nāḍikāḥ* — 'five and moon' equals 15</note> are treated in two ways: by subtracting and adding the ascensional difference <note>term: *charapala* — the difference between the actual rising time of a sign and its rising time at the equator</note>. In one place it is subtracted, in the other it is added, resulting in the half-day and half-night respectively. Where it is added, that is the half-day; where it is subtracted, that is the half-night. This applies in the northern hemisphere when the Sun is in the six signs starting with Aries. In the southern hemisphere, when the Sun is in the six signs starting with Libra, it is the reverse: where it is subtracted is the half-day, and where it is added is the half-night. 

For example: The ascensional difference is 86 palas, which is 1;26 Ghaṭīs. Because it is the northern hemisphere, 15;0 Ghaṭīs plus 1;26 results in a half-day of 16;26. Subtracted, it results in a half-night of 13;34. This is the half-day of the solar day. Similarly, by imagining another planet as the Sun and calculating its specific ascensional difference, the length of its day can be found; this is its own solar day. The sidereal day of the Sun is written here because of its unique application. As stated in the *Sūrya-siddhānta*:

> "The motion of the planet multiplied by the breaths of its rising, divided by the total breaths in a circle, results in the breaths to be added to find its own day-and-night." ॥ *Spa.* 59 ॥

<column-break/>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>Chapter on the Three Questions — 3</header>

Thus, the palas multiplied by six become breaths <note>term: *asu* — a unit of time based on a single respiration, 6 asus equal 1 pala</note>. The rising breaths of Taurus, 1530, multiplied by the Sun’s motion, 57;28, equals 87924. Divided by the minutes in a sign, 1800, the resulting breaths are 48. A quarter of this is 12. Divided by six to get palas, the result is 2, which added to fifteen is 15;2. Adding the previous ascensional difference of 86 palas for the northern hemisphere results in 16;28. This is the half-day of the sidereal-solar day. This subtracted from thirty Ghaṭīs results in the sidereal-solar half-night of 13;22. 

The difference between the half-day and the Ghaṭīs elapsed in the day is the meridian distance <note>term: *nata* — the "hour angle" or time-distance from the meridian</note>. If the elapsed Ghaṭīs fall within the half-day, it is the "East meridian distance" <note>original: *prāṅ-nata*</note>. If the half-day is subtracted from the elapsed Ghaṭīs, it is the "West meridian distance" <note>original: *para-nata*</note>. The half-day decreased by that meridian distance results in the altitude <note>term: *unnata* — time from the horizon</note>. In this context, the meridian distance should be known for the calculation of time. In other cases, such as in a horoscope, it is different. For example: the half-day is 16;26. Subtracted by 11 desired Ghaṭīs, the East meridian distance is 5;26. The half-day 16;26 decreased by that 5;26 meridian distance results in an altitude of 11;10. ॥ 7 ॥

-> Sudhākara says: Both methods mentioned in the *Siddhāntas* are clear for the sake of understanding. <-

### Calculating the Shadow from the Desired Time and the Desired Time from the Shadow —

> The half-day minus five is the "meridian divisor." The square of the meridian distance is set in two places: one multiplied by fifty, and the other added to nine hundred. The first is divided by the second, and the result is subtracted from the meridian divisor to find the "intended divisor." ॥ 8 ॥
> 
> However, if the meridian distance is greater than fifteen, it is subtracted from the half-day to find that divisor. The first segment divided by two, the ascensional difference multiplied by itself and decreased by one-sixth, added to ten and divided by the equinoctial shadow's hypotenuse, is the multiplier. This multiplier divided by the divisor results in the shadow's hypotenuse in digits. ॥ 9–10 ॥
> 
> The square root of the hypotenuse squared minus the gnomon squared is the shadow. The hypotenuse is the square root of the shadow squared plus the gnomon squared. ॥ 10 ॥
> 
> The multiplier divided by the hypotenuse becomes the divisor. Subtractions from the meridian divisor result in the remainder. This remainder, divided by nine hundred and subtracted from fifty, gives the square root of the result as the meridian distance. If the remainder is greater than ten, then the divisor itself is the altitude. ॥ 11 ॥
> 
> Thus, the calculation of the shadow is achieved simply, without the use of trigonometric sines or arcs. ॥ 12 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa's commentary:** The half-day minus five <note>original: *viśaraṃ* — *śara* (arrow) represents the number 5</note> is the meridian divisor <note>term: *khahara* — the value used to calculate the shadow at noon</note>. Now, the square of the meridian distance — the meridian distance multiplied by itself — is placed in two separate locations. In one place, it is multiplied by fifty. In the second place, it is added to nine hundred. The value from the first place is divided by the second, and the result is subtracted from the meridian divisor. The remainder is the divisor for the desired time. For example, the half-day...

<summary>This page explains the mathematical procedures for calculating the length of day and night based on the Sun's position and the observer's latitude. It also introduces a simplified trigonometric formula for determining the length of a shadow and the time of day without using complex sine tables.</summary>
<keywords>Lagna, Iṣṭakāla, half-day, meridian distance, altitude, shadow calculation, gnomon, Sumatiharṣa, Karaṇakutūhala</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical examples from the previous section, moving from the calculation of the Lagna (Ascendant) to the specific methods for determining the length of a shadow and the time elapsed from noon, known as the "hour angle."</meta>

# In the Karaṇakutūhala

16;26 minus 5 is 11;26; this is the **base divisor** <note>original: *khahara*</note>. Now, for the hour angle of 5;26, performing multiplication by the **zigzag method** <note>term: *gomūtrikā* — a method of multiplication used in ancient Indian mathematics, named for the "zigzag" path of a cow's urine</note> by this same value, the square of the hour angle is 29;31. In one place, 29;31 is multiplied by 50, giving 1475;50. In another place, adding the value 900, the square of the hour angle 29;31 is divided by the converted value 55771. The two converted values are 8856. The result in **Ghaṭīs** <note>term: *Ghaṭī* — a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> is 1;35. This is subtracted from the base divisor of 11;26, resulting in 9;51. This is the **desired divisor** <note>original: *iṣṭahara*</note>.

Now, he speaks of the **hypotenuse of the desired shadow**: If the hour angle is greater than fifteen Ghaṭīs, then the half-day duration minus the hour angle shall be the desired divisor. For example, if the hour angle is 16, and the half-day is subtracted, the result is 0;26. This is the desired divisor, which usually occurs at sunrise. In the first section, the **ascensional difference** <note>term: *carapalāni* — the difference between the time of actual sunrise and the time of sunrise on the equator</note> in seconds of time should be divided by the first segment. Half of the quotient, or half the ascensional difference divided by the first segment, gives the same result. Then, that value multiplied by itself and reduced by one-sixth of itself, then increased by ten and multiplied by the **equinoctial hypotenuse** <note>term: *palakarṇa* — the hypotenuse of the shadow cast by a 12-digit gnomon at noon on the equinox</note>, becomes the **divisor** <note>original: *hṛti*</note>.

Now, the calculation of the **latitude hypotenuse** <note>original: *akṣakarṇa*</note>: The equinoctial shadow is the base, the gnomon (12 digits) is the perpendicular; the square root of the sum of their squares is the hypotenuse. When divided by the desired divisor, the result in digits is the **desired hypotenuse**. For example: divide the ascensional difference of 86 by the first segment 55; the quotient is 1;33. Half of this is 46. Alternatively, half of the ascensional difference (43) divided by the first segment (55) results in the same: 46. The square of this is 35. This, minus its own sixth part (6), is 29. Adding ten (10) makes 10;29. To find the latitude hypotenuse, the equinoctial shadow (5;30) is the base, and the gnomon (12) is the perpendicular. The square of the base is 30;15. The square of the perpendicular is 144. Their sum is 174;15. Its square root is 13;13, which is the **latitude hypotenuse**. This is multiplied by the 10;29 found earlier, resulting in 138;33. This is the divisor. When divided by the desired divisor, the **desired hypotenuse** in digits is 14;5. Adding the Sun's position... the square root of the product of the hypotenuse plus and minus twelve: in one place add twelve (26;5), in the other subtract (2;5). The product of both (54;20) has a square root of 7;23. Thus, at the desired time, the **shadow of a twelve-digit gnomon** is 7;23 digits. Now, to know the desired time from the shadow: the Sun's light divided by the hypotenuse... the gnomon is 12, the shadow is the light; the square root of the sum of their squares is the hypotenuse. The remaining explanation is easy.

Example: the gnomon (12) squared is 144. The shadow squared is 54;30. The square root of their sum is 14;7, which is the hypotenuse. The previously calculated divisor 138;32 is divided by this, giving 9;49 as the desired divisor. This 9;49 is subtracted from the base divisor, leaving 1;37. This remainder is placed separately and multiplied by 900, giving 1455;0. This is divided by 50 minus the remainder 1;37...

<column-break/>

## Chapter 3: On the Three Questions

...which is 48;33. The square root of the result (30;4) is 5;26. This is the **hour angle**, identical to what was calculated before. If there is a slight difference, it is not considered an error because it is negligible. Thus, the hour angle 5;26 subtracted from the half-day 16;26 gives the **desired time** as 11;10. Now, if the remainder of the base divisor minus the desired divisor is greater than ten, then the divisor itself shall be the hour angle. For example, if the base divisor 11;26 is reduced by the desired divisor 0;26, the remainder 11;10 is greater than ten. Thus, the desired divisor 0;26 is the **time from sunrise** <note>original: *unnatam*</note>. The half-day (16) minus this gives the hour angle. This method is done without the use of chords or arcs, making the calculation of the shadow quick and easy. || 8-12 ||

**Sudhākara says:** The "base divisor" <note>original: *khahara*</note> is the middle divisor. The "first segment" relates to the ascensional difference of one’s own location. The rest is clear. Here is the derivation: The difference between the half-day and fifteen Ghaṭīs is the ascensional difference. In the northern hemisphere, this is: Ascensional difference = Half-day - 15. In degrees, this is: 6 × Half-day - 90. Doubling this (due to a small difference) in a circle of radius 12, the Sine of the ascensional difference is 12 × Half-day - 180. Adding the radius gives the "Total Sine" <note>original: *antyā*</note> = 12 × Half-day - 60, which equals 12 × (Half-day - 5). Therefore, (Half-day - 5) is named the **base divisor**. Similarly, in the southern hemisphere, the calculation leads to the same: (Half-day - 5) = base divisor. Thus, the origin of the base divisor is clear. Now, the Total Sine minus the Versine of the hour angle is the desired Sine. There, 6 × hour angle = degrees of the hour angle. The cosine is 90 - 6 × hour angle.

> "The degrees of the base and perpendicular, multiplied by each other and then by 180...
> subtracted from the product of 405, 12, and 10...
> these multiplied by the radius and divided, the result is
> the sines and cosines, even without using sine tables."

-> This is according to the method of Śrīpati <-

### Determining the Hour Angle—

Here, "the result divided by the hypotenuse shall be the divisor; that, subtracted from the base divisor, leaves a remainder,"

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 50 - n²

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 50 n²
Then the equation is: —————————— = Remainder <note>original: *śeṣa*</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 900 + n²

Therefore, 50 n² = 900 Remainder + Remainder n²

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 900 Remainder &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Remainder n²
Then n² = ———————— + —————————— The root of this, without the remainder, shall be
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 50 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 50

the measure of the hour angle. However, if in the above-written equation the Remainder = 10, then the hour angle is greater than fifteen; there, the desired divisor = Half-day duration - n = Hour angle. Thus, everything is proven.

### Determining Declination through Declination Segments—

> The segments of declination are 362, 341, 299, 
> 226, 110, and 52. These are the parts of the arm of the planet 
> increased by the degrees of precession. || 13 ||
> Divided by fifteen, the results obtained are to be added 
> to the remainder multiplied by the next segment. 
> By fifteen parts, the minutes of declination are formed, 
> having the same direction as the planet's hemisphere. || 14 ||

**Sumatiharṣa** <note>a famous commentator on Bhāskara's work</note>: The segments of declination are — "Yama-anga-rama" <note>a Sanskrit numerical code</note> means three hundred and sixty-two (362); "Kva-dvay-agnaya" means three hundred and forty-one (341); "Go-nava-bāhava"...

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivation from the Karaṇakutūhala, moving from the calculation of the sine of the hour angle to the calculation of planetary declination and terrestrial latitude.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>In the Karaṇakutūhala</header>

Two hundred ninety-nine (299), <note>original: "षट् अश्विनः" - six-twos, implying the next digits</note> two hundred thirty-six (236), one hundred fifty (150) <note>original: "खेषुभुवः" - sky(0)-arrows(5)-earth(1)</note>, and fifty-two (52) <note>original: "द्विबाणा" - two-arrows</note>. These are the six segments <term>khaṇḍakas<note>intervals used for trigonometric interpolation</note></term>. Now, for whichever planet the declination <term>krānti<note>the angular distance of a body north or south of the celestial equator</note></term> is desired, it should be added to the precession of the equinoxes <term>ayanāṃśa<note>the shift of the equinoxes over time</note></term>. The resulting arc <term>bhuja</term> in degrees should be divided by fifteen (15). The quotient indicates how many full segments have passed; the sum of these passed segments should be calculated. Then, the remainder of the degrees is multiplied by the "current" segment <note>the one currently being traversed</note> and divided by fifteen. Adding this to the sum of the previous segments gives the minutes of declination. Whether the declination is North or South depends on the planet's position relative to the celestial equator after adding the precession.

->**Example:** The Sun at a desired time is 1s 21° 32' 54". Its arc <term>bhuja</term> is 1s 21° 32' 54".<-
The degrees are 51° 32' 54". Divided by fifteen, the quotient is 3. The sum of these three segments <note>299, 236, and 150</note> is 685? <note>Note: The text states 1002, likely reflecting different segment values or a specific calculation step in this tradition</note>. The current segment is 236. The remainder of degrees 6° 32' 54" multiplied by 236 and divided by 15 gives 103' 1". Adding this to the previous sum 1002 results in 1105' 51". These are the minutes of declination. Dividing by sixty (60) gives the degrees: 18° 25' 1". Since the Sun with precession is in the Northern hemisphere, the declination is North.

**Sudhākara:** In this method, the segments for the declination are calculated based on fifteen-degree intervals of the longitude with precession. The reasoning is easy to follow.

### An Alternative Method for Calculating Declination

**The number 180 <term>khanāgendavaḥ<note>literally: sky(0)-mountains(8)-moon(1)</note></term> is multiplied by the arc of the longitude and by 180 minus the arc. This product is then divided by 443 degrees and 18 minutes, from which is subtracted the product divided by 77. This gives the declination in degrees and minutes even without the use of segments. || 15 ||**

**Sumatiharṣa:** The number one hundred eighty (180) is multiplied by the arc of the planet (with precession). One must subtract the arc from 180 and multiply by the arc itself. This value is placed in two positions. In one position, it is divided by seventy-seven (77). The result is subtracted from 443 degrees and 18 minutes <note>original: "त्रिवेदाब्धिभिः" - three(3)-four(4)-four(4)</note>. This result becomes the divisor. The value in the second position is then divided by this divisor. The result is the declination in degrees and minutes. This means declination can be found without the previously mentioned segments.

->**Example:** For the Sun with precession at 1s 21° 32' 54", the arc degrees are 51° 32' 54".<-
Subtracting 51° 32' 54" from 180 gives 128° 27' 6". These multiplied by the arc 51° 32' 54"

<column-break/>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>Chapter 3: The Three Inquiries</header>

results in 6629° 28' 9". This is placed in two spots. In one spot, it is divided by seventy-seven (77), giving 86° 5' 35". This is subtracted from 443 (specifically 442° 42' after subtracting the 18 minutes mentioned in the rule), resulting in 356° 36' 25". Dividing the value in the second spot (6629° 28' 9") by this gives a North declination of 18° 33' 45". The slight difference <note>compared to the first method</note> is due to the different mathematical approach. || 15 ||

**Sudhākara:** Here, the Sine of the arc is approximated as:
->(180 - arc) $\times$ arc $\times$ 480<-
->-----------------------<-
->40500 - (180 - arc) $\times$ arc<-

Therefore, the Declination is:
->(180 - arc) $\times$ arc $\times$ 480 $\times$ 24<-
->-----------------------------<-
->120 { 40500 - (180 - arc) $\times$ arc }<-

= ->(180 - arc) $\times$ arc $\times$ 96<-
->-----------------------<-
->40500 - (180 - arc) $\times$ arc<-

= ->(180 - arc) $\times$ arc<-
->-------------------------<-
->422 18/60 - (180 - arc) $\times$ arc / 96<-

Here, in the denominator, a slightly larger number is taken for the subtractor and the divisor to yield a result with minimal error. Thus:

->(180 - arc) $\times$ arc<-
Declination = ->---------------------------<-  Thus, everything is proven. || 15 ||
->443 18/60 - (180 - arc) $\times$ arc / 77<-

### Calculating Terrestrial Latitude

**The equinoctial shadow <term>akṣabhā<note>the shadow cast by a vertical 12-digit gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</note></term> increased by four hundred and ten (410) is divided by sixty. The quotient is added to the equinoctial hypotenuse <term>akṣakarṇa<note>the distance from the top of the gnomon to the tip of the equinoctial shadow</note></term>; this becomes the divisor. The equinoctial shadow multiplied by ninety (90) is then divided by this divisor. The result is the degrees of latitude <term>palāṃśa</term>. These are always South of Lanka. When these latitude degrees are combined with the declination—subtracted if the directions are different, added if the same—the result is the zenith distance <term>natāṃśa</term>. || 16 ||**

**Sumatiharṣa:** One's local equinoctial shadow <term>akṣabhā</term> should be increased by 410. Then divide by 60. The result is added to the local equinoctial hypotenuse <term>akṣakarṇa</term> to form the divisor. The equinoctial shadow multiplied by ninety (90) is divided by this divisor; the result is the degrees of latitude <term>akṣāṃśa</term>. These latitude degrees are always "South" in relation to the North of Lanka <note>This indicates the observer's position relative to the celestial equator</note>. These latitude degrees are then adjusted by the declination degrees: if the directions are different, take the difference; if the same, take the sum. This gives the zenith distance <term>natāṃśa</term>.
For example, if the equinoctial shadow is 5;30, adding 410 gives 415;30. Divided by 60, the result is 6;55. Adding the equinoctial hypotenuse 13;13, the divisor becomes 20;8. The equinoctial shadow multiplied by 90...

<summary>This page explains two methods for calculating celestial declination—one using traditional sine segments and another using an algebraic approximation—and begins a formula for determining terrestrial latitude based on gnomon shadows.</summary>
<keywords>declination, kranti, latitude, akshabha, equinoctial shadow, Bhaskara's sine approximation, Indian astronomy, Karana-kutuhala</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the calculation of latitude and zenith distance before transitioning into a rigorous mathematical proof by the 19th-century commentator Sudhākara Dvivedī. It marks the end of the third chapter of the Karaṇakutūhala, which deals with the "Three Problems" of celestial direction, place, and time.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>In the Karaṇakutūhala</header>

The <term>equinoctial shadow<note>palabhā; the shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</note></term> is 4;5. Divided by the divisor 20;8, the result is the <term>latitude degrees<note>akṣāṃśa</note></term> 24;35;9 South. By these degrees, the pole is elevated from the horizon. The <term>declination<note>krānti; the angular distance of a planet north or south of the celestial equator</note></term> parts are 18;33;45 North. Because the directions are different <note>one South, one North</note>, their difference results in the <term>zenith distance<note>natāṃśa; the angular distance from the point directly overhead</note></term> of 6;1;24. Because the latitude is greater, the direction is South. ||16||

**Thus ends the Chapter on the Three Problems in the commentary on the Karaṇakutūhala. || 3 ||**

**Sudhākara <note>Sudhākara Dvivedī, the commentator</note> :-**—Here is the mathematical proof <note>upapatti</note>. Let the <term>colatitude<note>lamba; the complement of the latitude (90° - latitude)</note></term> = 90 - A <note>where A is latitude</note>,

->Sine of Colatitude = [(90 + A) (90 - A) × 480] / [40500 - (90 + A) (90 - A)]<-

->= [(15 + A/6) (15 - A/6) × 12 × 40] / [1125 - (15 + A/6) (15 - A/6)]<- 
Here, let A/6 = *pha* <note>a placeholder variable</note>, therefore:

->Value of Sine of Colatitude = [(9000 - 40 *pha*²) 12] / [900 + *pha*²]<-

->12 × 120 / (pa ka)<- or, ->(225 - *pha*²) / (900 + *pha*²) = 3 / (pa ka)<-

By cross-multiplication: 225 (pa ka) - *pha*² (pa ka) = 900 × 3 + 3 *pha*²
By rearranging the sides: 225 (pa ka) - 900 × 3 = *pha*² (pa ka + 3)
Therefore: *pha*² = [225 (pa ka - 12)] / (pa ka + 3)
Or: *pha*² = [225 (pa ka - 12) (pa ka + 12)] / [(pa ka + 3) (pa ka + 12)]
= [225 (vi)²] / [(pa ka)² + 15 (pa ka) + 36]
Then *pha* = Square root of [[225 (vi)²] / [(pa ka)² + 15 (pa ka) + 36]] = [15 (vi)] / Square root of [(pa ka)² + 15 (pa ka) + 36]
Here, the Square root of [(pa ka)² + 15 (pa ka) + 36]...

<column-break/>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>Chapter 3: The Three Problems</header>

= Square root of [(pa ka)² + 15 (pa ka) + 36 - (490/60)² + (490/60)²]
= Square root of [(pa ka)² + (49/3) (pa ka) - (49/3) (pa ka) + 15 (pa ka) + 36 - (490/60)² + (490/60)²]
= (pa ka) + 490/60 + [((49/3) (pa ka) + 36 - (490/60)²) / (2 (pa ka) + 490/60)] due to small difference. Here, the value of this fractional number [((49/3) (pa ka) + 36 - (490/60)²) / (2 (pa ka) + 490/60)] for one unit of equinoctial shadow = 3/60.
Then, by proportion, the value for the desired equinoctial shadow = (vi)/60. Therefore, the transformation of the previous root is:
= (pa ka) + 490/60 + (vi)/60. Therefore *pha* = 15 (vi) / [(pa ka) + (410 + vi)/60]
Multiplied by six, the latitude degrees = 90 (vi) / [(pa ka) + (410 + vi)/60]
Thus, all is proven. ||15||

**Conclusion of the Chapter on the Three Problems:**
> Thus, in this *Kutūhala* <note>Wonder</note> concerning the movement of the planets spoken by Bhāskara,
> which is dear to the refined intellect, the clear calculation of the Three Problems [is concluded]. || 3 ||

**Sudhākara :-**
Composed by the son of Shri Kripalu, <insert>in the [commentary named] Ornament of Excellent Exposition</insert>, this excellent
explanation of the mathematics of the Sun's shadow and other factors is joined here with good sayings and logic.
Thus ends the "Chapter on the Means of Calculation for Shadows and Related Factors" in the *Vāsanāvaravibhūṣaṇa* <note>The Ornament of Excellent Exposition</note> commentary on the *Karaṇakutūhala*. ||3||
This is the third chapter, the "Chapter on the Three Problems," in the two commentaries by Sumatiharṣa and Sudhākara Dvivedī, edited by Satyendra Mishra.

# Chapter 4: On Lunar Eclipses

## Calculation of the Zenith Distance Correction—

> Subtract the <term>zenith distance<note>nata; specifically the hour angle from the meridian</note></term> from thirty <note>original: "khaguṇa"; kha=0, guṇa=3</note>, then multiply the result by the zenith distance itself. Divide eleven thousand two hundred and fifty <note>original: "kha-śara-bhānu-bhuvaḥ"; a word-numeral for 11250</note> by this product, and then subtract ten from the quotient. || 1 ||
>
> The result is the Solar Divisor <note>ravihara</note>. The Lunar Divisor <note>candrahara</note> is found by subtracting one-tenth of the Solar Divisor from itself. The Solar and Lunar <term>equations of center<note>mandaphala; the correction for the planet's elliptical orbit</note></term> are divided by their respective divisors. || 2 ||
>
> Apply these results as an addition or subtraction depending on whether the Sun or Moon is in the eastern or western <term>hemisphere<note>kapāla</note></term>. This calculation, based on the <term>Sine<note>kramajyā</note></term>, is approved by <term>Brahmagupta<note>original: "Jiṣṇuja"; meaning "son of Jiṣṇu"</note></term>. The methods used by others involving the <term>Versine<note>utkramajyā</note></term> for the visibility correction are not accepted here. || 3 ||

**Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:—** Now the chapter on lunar eclipses is explained. First, the procedure for calculation is recorded:—

In the year <term>Samvat 1677<note>approximately 1620 CE</note></term>, the year beginning with Āṣāḍha, <term>Śaka year 1542</term>, on the 15th lunar day (Full Moon) of the bright half of the month of Mārgaśīrṣa, a Wednesday. The time elapsed since sunrise is 38 <term>ghaṭīs</term> and 24 <term>palas</term>. To observe the lunar eclipse, following the calculations of the *Śrī-Brahma-tulya* <note>a manual based on Brahmagupta's system</note>, the elapsed years are 437, intercalary months are 162, the total month count is 5414, and omitted lunar days are 2542. The <term>Ahargaṇa<note>the total count of civil days elapsed since the start of the epoch</note></term> at sunrise is 159,893. By adding the half-day motion, we obtain the positions for that specific time. The <term>precession of the equinoxes<note>ayanāṃśa</note></term> is 18° 17' 42". Since the positions of the Moon and other planets are well-known, the calculated <term>solar equation of center<note>manda-phala</note></term> is a subtraction of 0° 38' 4", and the motion correction is an addition of 2' 13". The <term>lunar equation of center</term> is a subtraction of 4° 19' 52", and its motion correction is an addition of 39'. Because the Moon is in the southern hemisphere, the <term>ascensional difference<note>caraphala</note></term> in palas is added; however, because it is sunset, it is subtracted by 6. The time of the full moon after sunset is 11 ghaṭīs and 52 palas. The half-day duration is 13;11, the total day length is 26;22, the half-night duration is 16;49, and the total night length is 33;38. Now, the knowledge of whether an eclipse is possible or impossible on this lunar day is stated by the <term>Parvamālin<note>a specific astronomical authority or text</note></term>:—

> "If the remainder of the Sun's position at the end of the new moon or full moon is less than one ghaṭī or greater than five, an eclipse occurs in that period."

As stated by the rule "from the ghaṭīs passed since midday," etc.—The zenith distance <note>nata</note> is subtracted from 30, then multiplied by the zenith distance. Divide 11,250 <note>original: "khasharabhanubhuvaḥ"</note> by this. The resulting degrees, etc., minus ten, becomes the Solar Divisor. That same Solar Divisor, 
<meta>subtracted by</meta>

<column-break/>

subtracted by its own tenth part, becomes the Lunar Divisor. Now, if the Sun’s <term>equation of center<note>mandaphala</note></term> is divided by the Solar Divisor, the result in minutes etc., is the Solar Zenith Correction (5 minutes). If the Moon’s equation of center is divided by the Lunar Divisor, the result in minutes etc., is the Lunar Zenith Correction. If the Sun is in the western hemisphere <note>kapāla</note>, the solar correction is added; if in the eastern hemisphere, it is subtracted. Here, "western hemisphere" refers to the time from midnight to midday. However, in a lunar eclipse, the night is treated as the "day," so the hemisphere arrangement for the Moon is reversed: from midday to midnight is the eastern hemisphere, and from midnight to midday is the western hemisphere.

Now, if the Moon is in the eastern hemisphere and its equation of center is negative, the zenith correction is added to the Moon. In other words, if the Moon is in the west and the equation is negative, the correction is subtracted. Otherwise, if the equation is positive, the correction is subtracted regardless of whether the Moon is in the eastern or western hemisphere. Having made the planets "truer" <note>refined</note> for the eclipse in this way, the exact time of the <term>tithi<note>lunar day</note></term> should be calculated from them. Some practitioners perform this calculation repeatedly, but since it is not explicitly mandated, it is not repeated here. Similarly, some adjust the daily motion <note>bhukti</note> of the Sun and Moon by the zenith correction. For this, the solar motion correction should be divided by the Solar Divisor, and the result used to adjust the relative motion of the Sun and Moon. If the Moon is in the eastern hemisphere and the motion correction is negative, it is added to the total motion; otherwise, it is subtracted. 

For one desiring high precision, the zenith correction for the Sun and Moon should be performed separately when calculating the timing. It is said: "This zenith correction for eclipses is proper, though the ancients did not do it because the difference is small." The intent is that it must be done to ensure the calculated eclipse matches the observed reality. This zenith correction using the <term>Sine<note>kramajyā</note></term> is what is taught in the *Siddhānta* texts, and I have expressed it in this way. The calculation of the number "11250" is derived from the Sine. This is the opinion of the teacher Brahmagupta. The method mentioned by others using the <term>Versine<note>utkramajyā</note></term> for the deflection and visibility correction is not our view. 

According to the teaching that "the Moon’s day is the [Earth's] night," half of the Sun's night is half of the Moon's day. The half-night is 16;49. The desired ghaṭīs (the full moon time) are 11;51. The difference between them is 4;57. This is the eastern zenith distance. The altitude <note>unnata</note> is 2;51. To find the Sun's zenith distance at the end of the night or at the time of birth, the ghaṭīs are added to the half-day to get the zenith ghaṭīs for the east or west. Thus, the Sun's western zenith distance is 2;51. The Moon's altitude is the Sun's zenith distance. 30 minus the zenith distance (4;57) is 25;3. Multiplying this by the zenith distance (4;57) gives 124;0. Dividing 11250 by this result gives 90;43;32. Subtracting ten gives 80;43;32, which is the Solar Divisor. This, minus its own tenth part (8;4;21), results in the Lunar Divisor of 72;39;12. The solar equation of center...

<meta>The text continues from the correction of the Sun and Moon positions based on the hour angle (nata). The previous page established the divisors used to adjust these positions for the exact moment of the lunar eclipse. This page provides the mathematical proofs for these divisors and proceeds to calculate the "instantaneous" positions of the planets and the celestial latitude of the Moon.</meta>

0|38|4 divided by the Sun-divisor gives the result in degrees, etc., as 0|0|28. The Moon's equation of center <term>mandaphala</term> is 4|19|52.
Divided by the Moon-divisor, the result is 0|3|34. Because it is in the eastern hemisphere, the result is negative; therefore, it is added <note>In this system, subtracting a negative result is equivalent to addition</note>. Since the motion is very close, the hour-angle result <term>nataphala</term> is neglected. The Sun corrected by the hour-angle is 8|0|4|17 and the Moon is 1|27|35|18. From these, the remaining lunar day <term>tithi</term> is 11|38. ॥ 1-3 ॥

**Sudhakara's Commentary:** The meaning is clear.
Here is the derivation <term>upapatti</term>. The Sine <term>jīvā</term> of the hour-angle <term>nata</term> at the end of the lunar day is calculated according to the method of Shripati <note>Shripati was a prominent 11th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note>. For the Sun, the measure of the hour-angle correction is:

-> (180 - 6n) 6n x 480 x Sun-Correction <-
-> = --------------------------------- <-
-> 4920 {40500 - (180 - 6n) 6n} <-

-> (30 - n) n x 480 x Sun-Correction <-
-> = ------------------------- <-
-> 4920 {1125 - (30 - n) n} <-

-> 480 x Sun-Correction <-
-> = ------------------------- <-
-> 492 { (11250 / (30 - n) n) - 10 } <-

-> Sun-Correction <-
-> = ------------------ (approximate) <-
-> (11250 / (30 - n) n) - 10 <-

Here, the denominator is designated as the "divisor" <term>hara</term>. Similarly for the Moon:

-> 480 x Moon-Correction <-
-> 436 { (11250 / (30 - n) n) - 10 } <-

-> Moon-Correction <-
-> = ------------------------------ <-
-> 436 { (11250 / (30 - n) n) - 10 } <-
-> ------------------------------------ <-
-> 480 <-

-> Moon-Correction <-
-> = -------------------------- <-
-> 9/10 { (11250 / (30 - n) n) - 10 } <-

The remaining explanation <term>vāsanā</term> is easy to follow.

## Calculation of the Instantaneous Planet—

> **The daily motion <term>bhukti</term> multiplied by the passed or remaining time <term>nāḍīs</term> and divided by sixty is subtracted or added. This yields the instantaneous planet <term>tātkālika</term> along with its own correction for ascension <term>cara</term>; at the end of the lunar period <term>parvānta</term>, the Moon and Sun are thus at the same longitude.** ॥ 4 ॥

<column-break/>

५१ <note>Page 51</note>
Chapter on Lunar Eclipses — 4

**Sumatiharṣa's Commentary:** *Passed or remaining time*—the daily motion of the planet is multiplied by the desired time (in <term>ghaṭīs</term>) that has either passed or is yet to come, and then divided by sixty. The result is applied to the planet at sunrise. If the time has passed, it is subtracted; if it is yet to come, it is added. If the planet is retrograde <term>vakrin</term>, this is reversed. Thus, the instantaneous planets are obtained, further corrected by the result of their ascensional difference <term>caraphala</term>. These corrected Sun and Moon positions are then at the same longitude. Here, in calculating the end of the lunar period, any remainder is discarded. Because the Moon's motion is fast, there is a small difference; therefore, the degrees of the Sun and Moon are divided by twelve, and the remainder is subtracted from the divisor. Their minutes are then multiplied by the daily motion and divided by the difference in motion to yield the minute-correction.
For example, with the remaining time of 11|38, the Sun's motion 61|21 is multiplied, giving 713|42. Divided by sixty, the result is 11|53 in minutes, which is added. Similarly, the Moon's motion 829|35 is multiplied by the passed time 3|11 and divided by sixty. Even if there is a slight difference in seconds <term>vikalā</term> because the time is yet to come, it is not a flaw. At the conjunction <term>amāvāsyā</term>, the Sun and Moon have the same signs and degrees, and at the full moon <term>pūrṇimā</term>, they are exactly six signs apart. The instantaneous Moon is 2|0|16|8, the Sun is 8|0|16|10, and the ascending node <term>pāta</term> is 4|13|6|15. ॥ 4 ॥

## Calculation of Latitude —

> **The Sine <term>jīvā</term> of the Moon's longitude including the node, multiplied by three and divided by four, is the latitude <term>śara</term> in digits and minutes. The direction is the same as the hemisphere of the Moon-plus-node; the six signs starting with Aries <term>meṣādi</term> are the northern hemisphere.** ॥ 5 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa's Commentary:** *Including the node*—one must take the Sine of the arm <term>bhujajyā</term> of the instantaneous Moon added to the instantaneous node. That value is multiplied by three and divided by four <note>original: "kṛtaiḥ", a word-numeral for 4</note> to obtain the latitude <term>śara</term> in digits <term>aṅgula</term>. The direction follows the hemisphere of the Moon-plus-node: if it is in the northern hemisphere, the latitude is north; if in the southern, the latitude is south. He explains the direction: the six signs starting with Aries are the northern hemisphere, and the other six signs starting with Libra are the southern hemisphere.
Example: The node 4|13|6|15 and the Moon 2|0|16|8 together make the Moon-plus-node 6|15|22|23. The "arm" <term>bhuja</term> <note>The distance from the nearest equinoctial point</note> is 0|15|22|23. Its Sine is 356. Multiplied by three, it is 1148; divided by four, it is 2|57. This is the latitude in digits. Since the Moon-plus-node is in the southern hemisphere, the latitude is south. ॥ 5 ॥

## Determination of the Solstitial Points and another method for Latitude—

> **South and north of the signs Cancer and Capricorn, these are the two solstitial points <term>ayana</term>. Space-Horses (20), Arrow-Nines (95), Six-Arrows (56), Fire-Vedas (43) are the segments of the latitude...** ॥ 6 ॥
> **With these segments, calculate the latitude like the declination; the result in minutes divided by three gives the latitude in digits.**

It also introduces the calculation of the Moon's celestial latitude (śara) based on its distance from its node.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating astronomical phenomena. The previous page established the corrected positions of the Sun and Moon; this page moves into the calculation of the Moon's celestial latitude and the physical diameters of the celestial bodies to determine the extent of an eclipse.</meta>

# In the Karaṇakutūhala

**Sumatiharṣa:** — The six signs starting with Capricorn constitute the Northern Solstice path <term>uttarāyaṇa</term>, and the six signs starting with Cancer constitute the Southern Solstice path <term>dakṣiṇāyana</term>. The following are the six segments <term>khaṇḍas</term> for calculating the Moon’s celestial latitude <term>śara</term>: 70, 65, 56, 43, 27, and 9. Using these, following the method for calculating declination <note>original: "krānti-sādhana-vidhi"</note>, the latitude is found in minutes <term>kalā</term> by dividing the base arc by fifteen. When this result is divided by three, it is converted into digits <term>aṅgula</term>.

**Example for the Moon with Node:** The base arc <term>bhuja</term> is 6 signs, 15 degrees, 2 minutes, 23 seconds. The relevant portion is 0s 15° 2' 23". Since this is exactly at the fifteen-degree mark, the previous segments are exhausted. We multiply the remaining arc (15° 2' 23") by the first segment (70), resulting in 131;6. Dividing this by fifteen gives the latitude in minutes as 8' 44". Dividing that by three gives the latitude in digits as 2 digits, 55 minutes. There may be a slight difference due to varying methods, but the digit-based latitude is what is utilized here; henceforth, minutes are used for all practical applications. ॥ 6 ॥

**Sudhākara:** — The meaning is clear. The underlying theory <term>vāsanā</term> for calculating the instantaneous latitude is very simple. It uses the Rule of Three <term>trairāśika</term>: if the maximum latitude is achieved at the total radius, what is it for the sine of the desired arc? To convert minutes to digits, the value is divided by three. The formula is:
-> $\frac{270 \times \text{Sine of arc}}{120 \times 3}$ <-
-> $= \frac{3 \times \text{Sine of arc}}{4}$ <-
In another method for latitude, the segments are provided in minutes for every fifteen-degree interval.

**Calculation of the Diameters of the Moon, Sun, and Earth’s Shadow:**
> The diameter of the Moon is its daily motion divided by thirty-four. For the Sun, it is the motion multiplied by two and divided by eleven. ॥ 7 ॥
> The Moon’s daily motion multiplied by three and divided by sixty-seven, then reduced by one-seventh of the Sun’s daily motion, is the diameter of the Earth's shadow. Rahu, situated in the shadow of the Earth, covers the Moon; the Moon, in turn, covers the solar disk. ॥ 8 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa:** — The Moon's true daily motion divided by seventy-four <note>Note: The verse says *yugāgni* (34) but the commentator uses *yugādri* (74), a common variation in manuscript traditions</note> gives the Moon's diameter in digits. The Sun's true daily motion multiplied by 2 and divided by 11 gives the Sun’s diameter. The Moon's motion multiplied by 3 and divided by 67, minus one-seventh of the Sun's motion, is the diameter of the Earth's shadow <term>bhā-chāyā</term>. The shadow is calculated at the Moon’s distance. For example, if the Moon’s motion is 829' 35", divided by 74, the Moon’s diameter is 11 digits, 12 minutes.

<column-break/>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Lunar Eclipses: 4</header>

The Sun’s motion is 61' 21". Multiplied by two (122;42) and divided by eleven, the Sun’s diameter is 11 digits, 9 minutes. The Moon’s motion (829' 35") multiplied by three is 2488;45. Divided by 67, it is 37;32. Subtracting one-seventh of the Sun’s motion (8;45) leaves the diameter of the Earth’s shadow as 28 digits, 22 minutes.
**Calculation of diameter in leagues <term>yojana</term>:** The circumference of the orbit is multiplied by the diameter in minutes and divided by the minutes in a circle (21,600). Thus, the Sun’s diameter is 6,522 leagues and the Moon’s is 480. We shall demonstrate these in their proper places.
Regarding the "eclipser" <term>chādaka</term>: Rahu, entering the Earth's shadow, eclipses the Moon. The Moon, entering the Sun's path, eclipses the Sun. Therefore, in a lunar eclipse, the Moon is the "eclipsed" <term>chādya</term> and the shadow is the "eclipser." In a solar eclipse, the Sun is the "eclipsed" and the Moon is the "eclipser." ॥ 8 ॥

**Calculation of the Magnitude of the Eclipse:**
> Half the sum of the diameters of the eclipsed and eclipser, minus the latitude, is called the "covered" portion. The "covered" portion minus the diameter of the eclipsed body is the "clear-covered" portion, which occurs in total eclipses. ॥ 9 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa:** — Half the sum of the diameters of the eclipsed body and the eclipser, minus the latitude <term>śara</term>, is the magnitude of the eclipse <term>grāsa</term>. If the latitude is greater than this half-sum, no eclipse occurs. The "clear-covered" portion <term>khachanna</term> refers to the amount of the eclipser that extends beyond the eclipsed body in a total eclipse. For example: the Moon’s diameter is 11;12 and the shadow's diameter is 28;22. Half their sum is 19;47. If there were no latitude, the maximum magnitude would be about 20 digits. With a latitude of 2;57, the magnitude <term>sthagita</term> is 16;50. Subtracting the Moon's diameter (11;12) gives a "clear-covered" value of 5;38.
There is a supplementary verse for specific calculations:
-> “Multiply the eclipsed portion by twenty and divide by the diameter of the eclipsed body.” <-
Taking the eclipsed portion of 16;50, multiplied by 20 (336;40) and divided by the Moon's diameter (11;12), the result is 30;3 "twentieths" <term>viṃśopaka</term>. The maximum possible value is approximately 40. ॥ 9 ॥

**Sudhākara:** — The meaning is clear. The derivation is based on finding the diameter in minutes and then dividing by three to get digits. For the Sun, the diameter in minutes is approximately $\frac{11 \times \text{Sun's motion}}{60}$, which simplified is...

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivations for a lunar eclipse. Having previously established the diameters of the Sun, Moon, and the Earth's shadow, the text now calculates the specific timings of the eclipse phases, including the total duration and the period of totality.</meta>

# In the Karaṇakutūhala

Regarding the calculation of the Earth's shadow <term>bhūbhā</term>: the motion of the Sun multiplied by the latitude and divided by twelve, and so forth. By using the digit-based calculation <note>original: "aṅgula-karaṇena"</note>:

Earth's shadow = (2 × Moon's motion / 15 × 3) — (5 × Sun's motion / 12 × 3)

= (2 × 3 × Moon's motion / 135) — (5 × Sun's motion / 36)

= (6 × Moon's motion / 135) — (5 × Sun's motion / 36) = (3 × Moon's motion / 67) — (Sun's motion / 7)

Thus, the calculation of the celestial disks <term>bimba</term> is established; the remaining proof is clear.

### Calculation of the Duration and Totality in Ghatis—

> Take twice the latitude, add the obscured portion, and multiply the sum by that same obscured portion. Take the square root of this, multiply it by 180, and divide by the difference between the speeds of the two luminaries. This gives the half-duration <term>sthiti</term> in units of time <term>ghaṭikās</term>. The duration of totality <term>vimarda</term> is found similarly using the "remainder of the obscuration" <term>khacchanna</term>. ॥ 10 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa:** — The latitude <term>śara</term> calculated previously should be multiplied by two, added to the obscured portion of the Moon <term>channa</term>, and then multiplied again by that same obscured portion. One must take the square root <note>original: "mūla"</note> of this result. That root is multiplied by 180 <note>original: "khāṣṭendu", literally 'space-eight-moon'</note> and divided by the difference between the true daily motions <term>sphuṭa-bhukti</term> of the Moon and Sun. The resulting value represents the half-duration of the eclipse in units of <term>ghaṭikās</term> (units of 24 minutes). The duration of totality is found by the same process, but using the "void-obscuration" <note>original: "khacchanna", the difference between the shadow's radius and the sum of the latitude and Moon's radius</note>. Because the Earth's shadow moves eastward following the Sun's path, the Sun's motion is used in the calculation.

**Example:** If the latitude is 2;57, doubled it becomes 5;54. Added to the obscured portion of 16;52, it becomes 22;44. Multiplied by the obscured portion (16;52) itself, the result is 382;40. The square root is 19;34. Multiplied by 180, we get 3522;0. Divided by the difference in motion of 768;14, the result is 4;35 <term>ghaṭikās</term> for the mean half-duration. The maximum half-duration is nearly five <term>ghaṭikās</term>. Similarly, for the duration of totality: the doubled latitude 5;54 is added to the "void-obscuration" 5;38, totaling 11;32. Multiplied by the "void-obscuration," the result is 64;58. Its square root is 8. Multiplied by 180, it is 1440. Divided by the motion difference of 768;14, the result is 1;55 <term>ghaṭikās</term> for the half-duration of totality. The maximum totality is nearly two <term>ghaṭikās</term>. ॥ 10 ॥

### Calculation of the Five Moments—

> Divide the latitude by forty-eight <note>original: "nāgayugaiḥ", literally 'serpents-ages'</note>. The resulting time in units of <term>nāḍīs</term> (time-units) should be applied to the half-duration in two places: added to one and subtracted from the other. These give, in order, the durations for the end and the beginning of the eclipse phases. ॥ 11 ॥
> If the Moon, combined with its ascending node <term>pāta</term>, is in an odd quadrant, use this method. If in an even quadrant, the reverse applies to the durations of the eclipse and totality. At the exact moment the lunar month ends <term>parva-virāma</term>, the middle of the eclipse occurs, calculated from sunrise or sunset. ॥ 12 ॥

<column-break/>

<page-num>५५</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Lunar Eclipses — 4</header>

> When the time of the end of the lunar month is decreased by the half-duration and the half-totality, you find the moments of first contact <term>sparśa</term> and immersion <term>sammelana</term> respectively. When increased by their respective values, you find the moments of last contact <term>mukti</term> and emersion <term>unmīlana</term>. ॥ 13 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa:** — From the latitude <term>śara</term>, which has certain characteristics, divide by 48 <note>original: "nāgayugaiḥ"</note>. The resulting time <term>phala</term> in <term>nāḍīs</term> is used with the half-duration <term>sthiti</term> placed in two spots: added in one and subtracted in the other. This provides the specific durations for the beginning (contact) and end (release) of the eclipse. This applies specifically to lunar eclipses, not solar ones. If the Moon with its node is in an odd quadrant <term>ojapada</term>, this is the rule. If the Moon with its node is in an even quadrant <term>yugmapada</term>, then adding the result gives the duration of release <term>mokṣa</term> and subtracting it gives the duration of contact <term>sparśa</term>. The same is done for the duration of totality <term>vimarda</term>.

In the commentary <term>bhāṣya</term>, this operation is calculated from the latitude at the moments of contact and release. The instruction "from the latitude" does not mean the mean latitude, but rather the specific latitude at those moments. It is improper to use the mean latitude because the distance between the two half-durations is not equal. Because the latitude changes direction, one must use the specific latitudes for the release.

I will illustrate this following the tradition of the elders. For example: the mean latitude 2;57 divided by 48 gives a result of 0;3. The mean half-duration 4;35 is placed in two spots. Since the Moon with its node is in an odd quadrant, adding 0;3 gives a contact duration of 4;38, and subtracting it gives a release duration of 4;32. Similarly, with the result 0;3, the mean half-totality 1;55 becomes 1;58 for immersion <term>sparśa-vimarda</term> and 1;52 for emersion <term>mokṣa-marda</term>.

Regarding the phrase "from sunrise": In a solar eclipse, calculations start from sunrise; in a lunar eclipse, from sunset. At the end of the lunar fortnight <term>parvānta</term>, the true and mean positions of the planets are calculated. In a lunar eclipse, this "middle of the eclipse" is the end of the Full Moon <term>pūrṇimāntas</term>. In a solar eclipse, it is the end of the New Moon <term>darśānta</term> corrected by parallax <term>lambana</term>. 

When the time of the end of the lunar month <term>tithyanta</term> is decreased by the specific half-duration and half-totality, one finds the moments of first contact <term>sparśa</term> and immersion <term>sammelana</term> (when the Moon is fully covered). When that same end-time is increased by the respective release-durations, one finds the moments of release <term>mokṣa</term> and emersion <term>unmīlana</term>. 

"Immersion" <term>sammelana</term> refers to the meeting of the two disks—the obscured and the obscurer. It means the entire disk is swallowed by the shadow circle and becomes invisible. This lasts until the middle of the eclipse. The term "obscured" <term>channa</term> refers to the extent of the covering measured in digits of latitude. After the middle of the eclipse, the disk is covered no further; this is "emersion" <term>unmīlana</term>. When the obscured disk is just touching as it leaves the shadow, it is "release" <term>mokṣa</term>, meaning the two disks have separated.

**Example Calculation:**
End of the lunar month <term>tithyanta</term>: 11;38
Contact duration: 4;38 (Subtract from end-time) → Moment of Contact <term>sparśa-kāla</term>: 7;0
Immersion duration: 1;58 (Subtract from end-time) → Moment of Immersion <term>sammelana-kāla</term>: 9;40
Release duration: 4;32 (Add to end-time) → Moment of Release <term>mokṣa-kāla</term>: 16;10

<meta>This page continues the calculation of eclipse timings from the previous page, moving from the basic duration of the eclipse to the specific moments of contact, emersion, and totality. It then transitions into the calculation of 'valana' (deflection or orientation), which describes the angular position of the eclipse on the celestial disk.</meta>

# In the Karaṇakutūhala

The time of the end of the conjunction <term>parvānta-kāla</term> is 11;38. When the half-duration of totality at egress <term>mokṣa-marda</term> of 15;2 is added, it becomes the time of emersion <term>unmīlana-kāla</term> at 13;30. The time of first contact <term>sparśa-kāla</term> is 7;0. The time of final separation <term>mokṣa-kāla</term> is 16;10. The difference between these two is 9;10. This is the duration between contact and separation <note>the total duration of the eclipse</note> in units of ghatis <term>ghaṭikā</term> and so forth, representing the sacred time and the planetary state during the eclipse. ॥ 11-13 ॥

**Sudhākara:** — The meaning is clear. 
The proof here is as follows. There, the mean motion of the latitude <term>śara-gati</term> in one ghati is:
-> = $\frac{793 \times 2 \times 270}{60 \times 120 \times 60}$ = 1' <-
And the difference in the mean motions of the Sun and Moon per ghati is:
-> $\frac{733}{60}$ = 12' <-

Now, let $y$ = the half-duration <term>sthityardha</term> in units of ghatis. At the odd quadrant <term>ojapada</term>, the latitude at contact for the Moon with its node is:
3 × mean latitude - $y$. Then, following the rule "from the squares of the sum and difference of the half-diameters" <note>referring to a standard geometric rule in Indian astronomy</note>, the square of the units of the half-duration is:
9 × (half-diameter sum)$^2$ - (3 × mean latitude - $y$)$^2$ = 9 (half-diameter sum$^2$ - mean latitude$^2$) + 6 × mean latitude $\cdot$ $y$ - $y^2$. Then, dividing by the square of the difference in motions, we obtain the value of the square of the half-duration in ghatis:
-> = $\frac{9(mā^2 - m \check{s}^2) + 6 m \check{s} \cdot y - y^2}{144}$ = $y^2$ <-
<note>In these equations, 'mā' refers to the sum of half-diameters and 'm š' refers to the mean latitude.</note>

Or, $145 y^2 - 6 m \check{s} \cdot y = 9(mā^2 - m \check{s}^2)$
= 9(mā + m š)(mā - m š)
= 9(6 + 2 m š) × 6. Taking the square roots of both:
$y - \frac{6 m \check{s}}{145 \times 2}$ = $\frac{3 \sqrt{m\bar{u}}}{12}$ <note>approximate calculation</note>
-> $\therefore y = \frac{6 m \check{s}}{145 \times 2} + \frac{3 \sqrt{m\bar{u}}}{12} = \frac{m \check{s}}{48} + \frac{180 \sqrt{m\bar{u}}}{720}$ <-

This procedure is the same for the egress <term>maukṣika</term> phase by reversing the addition and subtraction. It is calculated similarly for the half-duration of totality <term>vimardārdha</term>. In an even quadrant <term>yugmapada</term>, with the Moon at its node, the operation is reversed because the latitude <term>śara</term> is decreasing. The rest is clear.

### Calculation of Deflection (Valana)—

> Multiply the hour angle <term>nata</term> at contact and separation by ninety <note>original: 'khāṅka', space-nine</note> and divide by half the length of its own day. The result is the degrees of the hour angle. Multiply the sine of that by the sine of the local latitude and divide by the radius <term>tri-jyā</term>. This gives the latitudinal deflection <term>akṣa-valana</term> for the east and west. ॥ 14 ॥

---

# Chapter 4: On Lunar Eclipses
### Page 57

> The deflection <term>valana</term> of the planet is toward the north or south. For the precessional deflection <term>āyana-valana</term>, take the cosine <term>koṭi-jīvā</term> of the planet's position including precession <term>ayanāṃśa</term>. Divide this by five <note>original: 'bāṇa', arrows</note> to get the degrees. These two deflections are in the same or different directions. ॥ 15 ॥
> Multiply the sum of the half-diameters by the sine of the sum or difference of these two deflections and divide by the radius to get the result in digits <term>aṅgula</term>. This is the clear deflection <term>sphuṭa-valana</term> of the Sun or Moon. At the beginning of the eclipse <term>grāsa</term> and at the end <term>mokṣa</term>, the directions are reversed. ॥ 16 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa:** — At the time of contact <term>sparśa</term> and separation <term>mokṣa</term>, multiply the hour angle <term>nata</term> by ninety (90). In a lunar eclipse, divide this by the half-night; in a solar eclipse, by the half-day. This gives the degrees of the hour angle. The sine <term>jyā</term> of these degrees is multiplied by the degrees of the local latitude <term>akṣāṃśa</term> and divided by the radius <term>tri-jyā</term> of one hundred and twenty (120). The result is the latitudinal deflection <term>akṣa-valana</term> at contact and separation. In the eastern hemisphere <term>pūrva-nata</term>, the latitudinal deflection is northern <term>saumya</term>. In the western hemisphere <term>apara-nata</term>, the latitudinal deflection produced by the earth's latitude is southern <term>yāmya</term>. 

For the precessional deflection <term>āyana-valana</term>, take the cosine <term>koṭi-jyā</term> of the sun or moon at that time, including the degrees of precession <term>ayanāṃśa</term>. Divide this by five (5) to obtain the result in degrees. Its direction follows the current solstice <term>ayana</term>; if the planet with precession is in the northern solstice, it is northern; if in the southern, it is southern. If these two—the latitudinal and precessional deflections—are in the same direction, add them; if in different directions, subtract them. Multiply the sine of that sum or difference by the sum of the half-diameters of the eclipsed and eclipsing bodies, and divide by the radius to get the clear deflection in digits <term>aṅgula</term>. In the case of addition, it retains that direction; in the case of subtraction, it takes the direction of the larger value. 

That deflection for the Sun and Moon at the start of the eclipse and at separation has reversed directions. Specifically, the contact deflection in a solar eclipse should be applied in the opposite direction: if southern, it is known as northern; if northern, it is known as southern. In a lunar eclipse, the separation deflection is reversed: if southern, it is northern; if northern, it is southern. Furthermore, there is no latitudinal deflection at the zenith <term>kha-madhya</term> or at the nadir <term>pātāla</term>. The maximum deflection is twenty-four degrees. It is always said that there is no deflection at the beginning of Cancer <term>karka</term> or Capricorn <term>makara</term>. The maximum clear deflection is approximately fifteen digits. Where the latitude of the place is sixty-six degrees, the maximum clear deflection becomes twenty digits according to the method described. 

Here, the prime vertical <term>sama-maṇḍala</term> is the observer's East. The deflection produced by the hemisphere at the desired time of the hour angle from the prime vertical to the intersection of the celestial equator and the ecliptic East is to be known. Then, the extent to which the East of the ecliptic moves from the East of the celestial equator is the precessional deflection <term>āyana</term>. From their sum or difference, the clear deflection is found. The amount the ecliptic moves from the prime vertical is the clear deflection. Now, for an eclipse that begins or ends during sunrise or sunset, the hour angle is calculated as in a birth chart, multiplied by ninety, divided by the half-day to get the degrees of zenith distance; from their base <term>bhuja</term>, the sine is calculated, and then the latitudinal deflection is found as before. As stated in the *Pūrva-mālā*: —

<summary>This section details the mathematical derivation for the timing of eclipse phases (contact, emersion, and separation) and explains the calculation of 'valana' (deflection). Valana is a critical adjustment used to orient the eclipse on the lunar or solar disk for an observer on Earth, combining effects of terrestrial latitude and the ecliptic's tilt.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical calculations for a lunar eclipse. Having established the timings of the eclipse on the previous page, the text now moves to calculating "valana" (the deflection or angular orientation of the eclipse) and "śara" (the celestial latitude of the Moon). These values are essential for a priest or astronomer to visually represent or "draw" the eclipse as it would appear in the sky.</meta>

# In the Karaṇakutūhala

> "The remaining ghatis <note>original: 'nāḍyo', a unit of 24 minutes</note> of one’s own night, and the half-day at the times of contact and separation, [determine the hour angle]. The hour angle <term>natam</term> is calculated as before; the ghatis multiplied by six become degrees. The sine of that hour angle, multiplied by the terrestrial latitude and divided by the radius, gives the deflection <term>valana</term> in degrees. It shall be north or south depending on whether the eclipse is in the east or west." ॥

It is further stated,
> Half the day added to the remaining ghatis of the night is the Sun’s eastern hour angle. Half the day added to the elapsed ghatis of the night is the Sun’s western hour angle. Half the night added to the remaining ghatis of the day is the eastern hour angle. For the Moon, the hour angle from sunrise to the half-night is considered western.

Then, calculations such as "multiplied by ninety" and so forth are performed. In the *Brahmate-tulya* commentary, however, for an eclipse that begins before moonrise <term>grastodaya</term>, the hour angle at contact is used; for an eclipse that ends after moonset <term>grastāste</term>, the hour angle at separation is used. One should calculate the degrees by which the time exceeds the half-day, subtract them from 90, find the sine, multiply by the terrestrial latitude, and divide by the radius to find the deflection due to terrestrial latitude <term>akṣaja-valana</term>. At an eclipsed rising, it is north; at an eclipsed setting, it is south. We shall demonstrate this example in its proper place.

The time of contact <term>sparśa-ghaṭī</term> is 7;0. The Moon’s half-day <term>candra-dinārdha</term> is 16;49. The difference between these is the hour angle at contact: 9;49. This is in the east. Multiplied by ninety, it is 883;30. Divided by the half-day 16;49, the resulting degrees of hour angle are 52;32;13. Their sine <term>jyā</term> is 59;2. This is multiplied by the terrestrial latitude 24;35;9, giving 2336;28;58. Divided by the radius <term>triJyā</term> of 120, the resulting terrestrial deflection <term>akṣaja-valana</term> in degrees and minutes is 19;28;54. Because it is an eastern hour angle, the direction is North. Now for the ecliptic deflection <term>āyana-valana</term>: at the time of contact (7;0), using the rule "the remaining ghatis..." the Moon with the precession of the equinoxes <term>sāyana-candra</term> is 2 signs, 17 degrees, 19 minutes, 20 seconds. The cosine <term>koṭi</term> is 0;12;4;40 and its sine is 26;1. Divided by five <note>original: 'bāṇa' (arrows), the number 5</note>, the resulting ecliptic deflection is 5;13. Because the sun is in the northern path <term>saumyāyana</term>, this is North. Since the terrestrial and ecliptic deflections are in the same direction, their sum is 24;40;40. Its sine is 49;52. This is multiplied by the half-sum of the diameters of the Moon and the Earth’s shadow 19;47, giving 986;31;44. Divided by the radius 120, the resulting "clear" deflection <term>spaṣṭa-valana</term> in digits <note>original: 'angula', roughly 3/4 of an inch</note> is 8;13;9.

At the time of separation <term>mokṣa-kāla</term> (16;10), the difference between the time and the Moon's half-day is the eastern hour angle: 0;39. Multiplied by 90, it is 58;30. Divided by the Moon's half-day 16;49, the degrees of hour angle are 3;28;43. Their sine is 7;18. Multiplied by the terrestrial latitude 24;35;9, it is 179;28. Divided by the radius, the result is 1;29;44. Because it is eastern, it is North. Now for the ecliptic deflection: at the time of separation (16;10), the Moon is 2 signs, 19 degrees, 18 minutes, 44 seconds. With precession, it is 2 signs, 19 degrees, 12 minutes, 26 seconds. The cosine is 0;17;33;34. Its sine is

<column-break/>

<page-num>५९</page-num>
<header>Chapter 4: Lunar Eclipses</header>

21;47. Divided by five, the result is 4;21;24. Because the sun is in the northern path, this is North. Since the terrestrial and ecliptic deflections are in the same direction, their sum is 5;51;8. Its sine is 12;17. Multiplied by the half-sum of the diameters 19;47, the result is 246;11. Divided by the radius 120, the "clear" deflection <term>sphuṭa-valana</term> is 2;8;5 North. ॥ 14-16 ॥

### Calculation of the Latitude at Contact and Separation—

> **"If the mean latitude <term>madhya-śara</term> arises from an odd quadrant, it is decreased and increased by one-third of the duration <term>sthityardha</term>. If from an even quadrant, [it is the reverse]. For a lunar eclipse, the latitudes <term>śara</term> are considered to be in the opposite directions [for drawing]." ॥ 17 ॥**

**Sumatiharṣa:** — The meaning is that if the mean latitude at the time of the middle of the eclipse is produced from the Moon and its Node being in an odd quadrant <term>ojapada</term>, then when decreased by one-third of the half-duration, it becomes the latitude at the moment of contact <term>spārśika-śara</term>. When increased by one-third of the half-duration, the mean latitude becomes the latitude at the moment of separation <term>maukṣika-śara</term>. If it arises from an even quadrant <term>yugmapada</term>, then adding one-third gives the contact latitude and subtracting gives the separation latitude. This rule applies to lunar eclipses, not solar. Alternatively, the latitudes at contact and separation can be calculated directly from the Moon and Node at those specific times. In a lunar eclipse, these latitudes must be understood to have reversed directions when performing the graphical projection <term>parilekha</term>, but not elsewhere. As it is said: "The latitudes of the Sun are always taken as they are in drawing; those of the Moon are reversed."

For example: the half-duration is 4;35. One-third of it is 1;31. Because the Moon is in an odd quadrant, the mean latitude 2;57 decreased [by one-third] becomes the contact latitude: 1;26. The mean latitude 2;57 increased by one-third (1;31) becomes the separation latitude: 4;28. This calculation is for the mean latitude itself. Now, by the other method: the Moon at contact is 9 signs, 29 degrees, 11 minutes, 38 seconds. Added to the Node at that time (4;13;5;53), it is 6 signs, 10 degrees, 47 minutes, 31 seconds. The sine is 139. Multiplied by three (417) and divided [by the radius factor], the latitude at contact is 1;14 South. For the time of separation, the Node is 4;13;6;21. Added to the Moon (2;19;18;44), it is 6 signs, 2 degrees, 55 minutes, 5 seconds. The sine is 67. Multiplied by three (202;1) and divided, the latitude at separation is 4;35 South. There is a small difference between these two methods. ॥ 17 ॥

**Sudhākara:** — The meaning is clear. The derivation is also easy, based on the ratio of the degrees of terrestrial and ecliptic deflection, as follows:
The terrestrial deflection in degrees = (Sine of the hour angle × Terrestrial latitude) / Radius.

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on calculating the deflection (valana) and latitude (śara) of the Moon. Having determined these mathematical values, the author now provides the specific geometric instructions for "projecting" or drawing the eclipse diagram (parilekha). This allows the astronomer to visually represent how the eclipse will progress across the celestial disk.</meta>

Latitude Deflection = --- 24 × Sine of Zenith Distance --- / 120 = --- Sine of Zenith Distance --- / 5 <note>original: 'khako-jyā', meaning the sine of the zenith distance. This formula simplifies the calculation of the equatorial deflection.</note>

Now, for the latitude at the moments of contact and separation, a proportion is used: if in one ghaṭī <note>a unit of 24 minutes</note> the latitude is one minute, then for the ghatis of the half-duration, what will it be? To convert these latitude minutes into digits <term>aṅgulas</term>, they are divided by three. The addition or subtraction of these values is easy to understand based on whether the directions are the same or opposite. Everything else is very simple.

## Explanation of the Projection (Diagram)—

**With a radius equal to half the eclipsed body's diameter, draw a circle; then, draw another with half the sum of the diameters to establish the directions.**
**On this outer circle, mark the deflection according to its direction—at the east for contact and at the west for separation. ॥ 18 ॥**

**From the western and eastern sides of the Sun, the two latitudes should be marked like a sine from the ends of the deflection marks.**
**Having constructed a "fish" <term>matsya</term> from the two ends of the deflection, the middle latitude is found along the line connecting its "mouth" and "tail." ॥ 19 ॥**

**Sumatiharṣa:** — On level ground, or at the feet of the observer, first take half the measure of the digits of the disk of the eclipsed body—the Moon in a lunar eclipse or the Sun in a solar eclipse. From a point chosen as the center, draw a circle. Then, with a compass <term>karkaṭaka</term> or a string, draw another circle from that same center using the measure of half the sum of the eclipsed and eclipsing bodies' diameters. From that center point, two lines should be drawn: one running East-West and another North-South.

Once the directions are established, the deflection <term>valana</term> must be marked on the outer circle. This is done according to its direction: if southern, toward the south; if northern, toward the north. As previously explained, the directions are reversed for the beginning (contact) and the end (separation). In a lunar eclipse, the deflection for contact is marked from the eastern point, and for separation from the western point. In a solar eclipse, contact is from the west and separation is from the east.

Then, since the latitudes <term>śara</term> for the Moon are in the opposite directions, as previously explained, one should mark the latitudes at contact and separation. These are measured in digits and placed like a sine cord, starting from the marks made for deflection and pointing toward their respective directions. The contact latitude is marked from the end of the contact deflection; the separation latitude is marked from the end of the separation deflection. Having thus marked the ends of the two latitudes on the area of the outer circle, the author explains how to find the middle latitude:

"Having constructed..." — Placing one end of a string (equal to the distance between the two deflection points) on the contact deflection mark, one should draw a half-arc; then do the same from the separation deflection mark. Where these two arcs intersect, designate them as the "mouth" and "tail" of the "fish." <note>The 'matsya' or 'fish' is a traditional Indian geometric technique for finding the perpendicular bisector or the center line between two points.</note> Drawing a line from the mouth through the center to the tail creates the required axis. ॥ 18-19 ॥

<column-break/>

# 61
### Chapter on Lunar Eclipses - 4

## Description of the Points of Contact, Middle, and Separation—

**From the center, according to the directions, draw circles from the ends of the latitudes using half the diameter of the eclipsing body.**
**These will show the positions of contact, mid-eclipse, and separation. Then, mark the point from the end of the middle latitude. ॥ 20 ॥**

**Sumatiharṣa:** — "From the center..." — From that central point, the middle latitude should be marked toward its proper direction. After marking the ends of the three latitudes (contact, middle, and separation), use a compass set to half the diameter of the eclipsing body (the Moon in a solar eclipse or the Earth's shadow in a lunar eclipse). Draw a circle centered at the mark for the contact latitude. Where this circle touches the inner circle of the eclipsed body is the point of contact (the start). Similarly, a circle drawn from the mark of the separation latitude shows the point of separation (the end). A circle from the mark of the middle latitude shows the state of the eclipse at its peak.

If, at the middle of the eclipse, the eclipsing body completely covers and passes beyond the eclipsed disk, it is a total eclipse <term>sarva-grahaṇa</term>. If it only covers a portion of the eclipsed disk, it is a partial eclipse <term>khaṇḍa-grahaṇa</term>. If it does not touch the eclipsed disk at all, there is no eclipse. ॥ 20 ॥

## Description of the Desired Obscuration—

**The lines passing through the ends of the first and last latitudes are known as the paths of immersion and emersion.**
**Draw a circle at the center with half the difference of the diameters, and also at the two points where it meets those paths. ॥ 21 ॥**

**By drawing a circle with the radius of the Earth’s shadow, the moments of total immersion and total emersion can be known.**
**First calculate the measure of the path, then multiply the desired time by the path in digits and divide by the total duration. ॥ 22 ॥**

**The resulting digits should be marked along the path according to the desired time. A circle drawn there with the radius of the eclipsing body shows the state of the eclipse at that desired time. ॥ 23 ॥**

**Sumatiharṣa:** — Now, draw a line from the point of the middle latitude to the point of contact; this is the path of the eclipsing body's entry. Then draw a line from the point of the middle latitude to the point of separation; this is the path of its exit. These lines, touching the three latitude points, form an arc.

"With half the difference..." — Take a compass set to half the difference between the diameters of the eclipsed and eclipsing bodies and draw a circle at the center. Where this circle intersects the previously drawn paths of the eclipse, draw another circle using the radius of the Earth's shadow. Where this touches the outer circle, that point is the total immersion <term>sammīlana</term> (the start of totality). Where the circle of the Earth's shadow meets the exit path, that point is the total emersion <term>unmīlana</term> (the end of totality). It should be noted that because the text specifically mentions the "Earth's shadow" <term>bhūbhā</term>, these points of total immersion and emersion do not occur in a solar eclipse <note>In this specific context, the commentary implies that solar totality is treated differently or that the Moon is not usually larger than the Sun in these calculations.</note>

<vocab>Karaṇakutūhala, Parilekha (Projection/Diagram), Valana (Deflection), Śara (Latitude), Sparśa (Contact/Beginning), Mokṣa (Separation/End), Sumatiharṣa (the commentator), Chandra-grahaṇa (Lunar Eclipse), Iṣṭa-grāsa (Eclipse at a desired time), Bhūbhā (Earth's shadow), Mānaikya-khaṇḍa (Half the sum of diameters), Bimbārdha (Half the disk/radius), Sammīlana (Total immersion), Unmīlana (Total emersion)</vocab>
