# In the Computation of Wonders

## Chapter 4: On the Lunar Eclipse

...should be known. Sometimes, a very small difference is mentioned, yet it still results in an alignment of the disks; however, this is an occultation <note>original: "khacchannam", referring to when one body completely hides another</note>, because the two—the eclipser and the eclipsed—have similar disk sizes. Now, the magnitude of the eclipse at a desired time and at the moment of release.

**Regarding the path:** Someone asks, "Between the time of first contact and the desired time, or before the time of release, how much of the eclipse has occurred?" At that point, one should measure the line of the eclipse's path and the line of the release path in digits <term>aṅgulas; astronomical units of measurement</term>. Multiply the desired time by those path-digits, then divide sequentially by the duration of the contact and release in ghaṭīs <note>one ghaṭī equals 24 minutes</note>. The resulting value gives the desired digits. These digits should be marked on the path as required. By marking the desired digits from the latitude of contact or the latitude of release, and by using a circle with the radius of the eclipsing body centered on that mark, one can know the appearance of the eclipse at that specific time, showing how much of the eclipsed body is covered. For example: if the desired time from contact is 1 ghaṭī, multiplied by 19 digits (the measure of contact) and divided by the duration of contact 4;38, the result is 4;06 desired digits.

Now, the knowledge of contact, middle, and release without a diagram is explained: "If the latitude is in the northern portion and the lunar eclipse begins in the fire direction (South-East) in the East, the first covering occurs there; then the release is in the direction of Death (South). If the latitude is Southern, the contact is in the direction of Indra (East) and the covering is in the direction of the Destroyer of Cities (North-East); the release occurs in the direction of Fire (South-East) in sequence."

Where the half-diameter of the eclipsed body is 5;36 and the half-sum of the diameters is 19;46. The clear deflection <term>valana</term> at contact is North 8;29;3, and the clear deflection at release is North 2;08. Furthermore, the southern latitude at contact is 1;16, the middle latitude is 2;27, and the release latitude is 4;48. The half-diameter of the Earth's shadow to the North is 14;11, and the half-diameter [of the Moon] is 8;35. Thus, a clever person should contemplate these values and then construct the diagram.

Now, an example of the Moon's contact and zenith distance at the time of an eclipsed moonrise: In the Shaka year 1528 <note>1606 CE</note>, on the full moon of the month of Bhadrapada, on Saturday, the elapsed years were 423, intercalary months 157, omitted lunar days 2459, and the total day-count (*Ahargaṇa*) was 1,546,950. At sunrise, the mean Sun was 5s 07° 59', the Moon was 10s 25° 03' 34", the lunar apogee was 2s 22° 36' 44", and the node was 6s 26° 03' 54"—these were for the local observer's sunrise. The "Rama" correction for the Moon was 0° 15' 00". The Sun’s equation of center was subtractive 2° 08' 12", and the Moon’s equation of center was additive 4° 27' 34". The true Sun was 5s 04° 57' 00", the Moon 10s 12° 09' 27" 05'''. The precession of the equinoxes was 18° 03' 37". The ascensional difference in minutes was 12. The Sun's daily motion was 58' 35", the Moon's 819' 00". Half the day length corrected for the ascensional difference was 15;12 ghaṭīs, and half the night was 14;48 ghaṭīs. The remaining time of the lunar day was 26;12 ghaṭīs. Because the full moon occurred during the day, for the purpose of finding the zenith distance... the calculation of the zenith distance: the remaining day ghaṭīs are added... the remaining day is 4;22 and half the night is 14;48. The sum of these is

<column-break/>

19;00, which is the zenith distance <term>nata</term> to the East. With this, the Sun's zenith distance is the same as the Moon's zenith distance, calculated according to the method for determining the Sun's position at noon. From this, by applying the rules for the zenith result, the Sun's result is additive 0;02;55 and the Moon's zenith result is subtractive 0;06;46. The time of the middle of the eclipse, corrected by these results, is when they are at the same longitude. By the method of the node and the specific moment, the latitude in digits is 2;24. In the subtraction, the Moon's disk is 11;08, the Earth's shadow is 28;00, and the eclipsed portion is 17;18. The duration of the eclipse is 4;37 subtractive. Now, for the zenith distance at contact, the time elapsed at moonrise is 22;21. By that, the remaining day is 8;30. Just as for the Moon's remaining night, the remaining night ghaṭīs are 8;03. Half the Moon's day is 14;48. Their sum is 22;51, the zenith distance at contact to the East. Multiplied by 90 and divided by the half-day 14;48, the degrees of zenith distance are 138° 57' 09". In signs, this is 4s 18° 57' 09". The base <term>bhuja</term> is 1s 11° 02' 51". The sine <term>jyā</term> is 78;34. The latitude of the place is 24° 35' 09". When multiplied, the result is 1939;10;37. Divided by the radius 120, the obtained terrestrial deflection is North 16;05. In the divisible method, from the zenith distance of 22;51, half the day 14;48 is subtracted, leaving 8;03. Multiplied by 90 and divided by the Moon's half-day, the zenith degrees are 48;57. Subtracting from 90 gives 41;30. Because this is similar to the previous base-degrees, the deflection is as before. Other operations should be performed as previously described. This has been shown to explain the deflection because of its specific importance; there is nothing here that a person of good intellect cannot grasp. Similarly, for an eclipse at moonset, the direction of the zenith distance should be inferred by oneself. In a solar eclipse as well, at the time of an eclipsed sunrise or eclipsed sunset, the method for finding the direction should be stated accordingly.

->Thus ends the fourth chapter on Lunar Eclipses in the commentary on the *Computation of Wonders* (Karaṇa-kutūhala). ॥ 4 ॥<-

## Conclusion—

> Thus, in this *Computation of Wonders* (Kutūhala), a manual on the planets spoken by Bhāskara,
> Which is dear to the intellects of the learned, the means for calculating the Moon and others is complete. ॥ 4 ॥

**Sudhākara:** — The meaning is clear. The explanation is as simple as the statements in the Siddhāntas <term>foundational astronomical treatises</term>.

> In this excellent ornament of explanation composed by the son of the compassionate Shri Kripalu,
> The explanation of the calculation of the lunar eclipse is presented here, accompanied by good sayings and logic.

->Thus ends the section on Lunar Eclipses in the *Ornament of Explanation* (Vāsanā-vibhūṣaṇa) on the *Computation of Wonders* (Karaṇa-kutūhala). ॥ 4 ॥<-

<header>Chapter 5: On the Calculation of Solar Eclipses</header>

# The Nature of Zenith Distance and Altitude—

> At the moment of the New Moon's end <note>original: "darśantakāle"</note>, the ascendant <term>Lagna; the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term> should be reduced by three signs. One should then find the sum or difference of its declination and the local latitude.
> If the directions are different, the result is the zenith distance <term>natabhāgakāḥ; the angular distance from the point directly overhead</term>; when these are subtracted from ninety, the remainder is the altitude <term>unnatāṃśāḥ; the height above the horizon</term>. ॥ 1 ॥

## Sumatiharṣa—Here the procedure is written first—

In the year Samvat 1657 <note>approximately 1600 CE</note>, in the month of Ashadha, during the dark fortnight, while Shaka 1422 was current, on the day of the New Moon <note>Amavasya</note> in the month of Shravana according to local custom, the Moon was at 28;46 degrees. On this day, to observe the solar eclipse <note>original: "sūryaparva"</note>, the elapsed years were 417, the intercalary months were 154, and the omitted lunar days <note>original: "avamāni"</note> were 2,423. The civil day-count from sunrise <term>ahargaṇa</term> was 1,524,36. After correcting for the "Rama-seed" <note>a specific astronomical correction factor</note> and the longitudinal difference between cities, the mean positions are: Sun 3s 0° 36' 32"; Moon 2s 29° 40' 23"; Higher Apogee 6s 11° 21' 56"; Node 2s 26° 20'. For the Moon, the "Rama-seed" correction is 15 minutes. The Sun’s equation of center <term>mandaphala</term> is negative 0° 28' 36". Thus, the true Sun is 3s 0° 6' 34". The Moon’s equation of center is positive 4° 41' 50", making the true Moon 2s 24° 51' 57". The Node is 2s 26° 55'. The precession of the equinoxes <term>ayanāṃśa</term> is 17° 57' 20". The ascensional difference <term>carapala</term> is 103. The remaining time of the New Moon to occur is 28;46 ghaṭīs <note>one ghaṭī is 24 minutes</note>. Midday is at 16;43 ghaṭīs. The total day length is 33;26 ghaṭīs. Midnight is at 13;17 ghaṭīs and the total night is 26;34 ghaṭīs. The difference between midday (16;43) and the elapsed time of the New Moon (28;46) is the westward zenith distance in time: 12;3 ghaṭīs. The Moon’s proximity is calculated similarly. When the New Moon ends during the night, the zenith distance should be calculated by adding the remaining night hours. Now, for the parallax calculation: the zenith distance 12;3 is subtracted from the multiplier 60, resulting in 17;57. This is multiplied by the zenith distance 12;3, giving 216. Dividing the constant 119,250 by this gives 52.5. Subtracting 10 results in the "Sun-divisor" <term>ravihara</term> of 42.5. This value (42.5) reduced by its own tenth part (4.12) becomes the "Moon-divisor" <term>candrahara</term> of 37;52. Dividing the results gives the Sun’s zenith parallax of 0' 40" and the Moon’s zenith parallax of 7' 22". Since the zenith distance is westward, this is added to the Sun, making the corrected Sun 3s 0° 7' 14". For the Moon, it is subtracted, making the corrected Moon 2s 24° 51' 45". Now, he explains the parallax in velocity: the Sun's equation of velocity 2' 55" divided by the divisor 42.5 gives 3 seconds. Applying this to the daily motion, the Sun's velocity becomes 56' 58". The Moon's equation of velocity 29' 15" (positive) divided by its divisor gives 0' 46". Since it is the western hemisphere, this is subtracted from the daily motion 819' 50", resulting in the corrected

<meta>translator note: the catchword "candrasya" (of the Moon) indicates the start of the next column.</meta>

<column-break/>

<header>Chapter 5: On the Calculation of Solar Eclipses   65</header>

Moon's velocity of 818' 4". Therefore, the true time of the New Moon still to come is 29;24 ghaṭīs. The positions at this time are calculated by multiplying the elapsed or remaining time by the daily motion. Thus, the wise should understand this everywhere.

Regarding the verse "At the moment of the New Moon's end": One calculates the ascendant for the moment the New Moon ends and subtracts three signs <note>90 degrees</note>. If the resulting point (the *Vitribha Lagna*) is equal to the Sun's position at that moment, there is no parallax <term>lambana; the apparent shift in position due to the observer's location on Earth</term>. If the Sun is more or less than this point, parallax occurs. Specifically, the *Vitribha Lagna* refers here to the 10th house <term>daśamaṃ lagnam; the meridian</term>, as that produces the correct "deflection" <term>valana</term>. In texts like the *Sūrya Siddhānta*, calculations are made from the 10th house because at midday, when the New Moon ends, there is no zenith distance and the Sun itself is at the 10th house, meaning there is no parallax. Sometimes a solar eclipse near midday might not align perfectly if calculated from the 10th house; in those cases, one should calculate from the 10th house for accuracy, though the author has occasionally accepted a coarser method for ease. If three signs cannot be subtracted from the ascendant, one should add a full circle (12 signs) and then subtract. As it is said:

> "If three signs cannot be subtracted, add a circle and then subtract. Even if the result is greater, the *Vitribha* point is said to be less than the Sun."

Now, the declination of this *Vitribha Lagna* (including the precession) should be calculated in degrees. If this declination and the local latitude have different directions, find their difference; if they have the same direction, find their sum. The resulting direction belongs to the larger value. These are the zenith distance degrees <term>natāṃśāḥ</term>. Subtracting these from 90 gives the altitude degrees <term>unnatāṃśāḥ</term>. Thus, the true Sun is 3s 0° 35' 8" and the end of the New Moon is 29;24 ghaṭīs. For this, the calculated ascendant with precession is 8s 25° 14' 58". The *Vitribha* point is 5s 25° 14' 58". From this, the declination is 15° 4' 58" North. The latitude for Sirohi <note>a city in Rajasthan, India</note> is 24° 35' 9" South. Because the directions are different, the difference is the zenith distance: 22° 40' 32" South (since the latitude remains South). Subtracting from 90, the altitude is 67° 19' 28". The sine of the altitude is 11;135 and the sine of the zenith distance is 46;5. ॥ 1 ॥

# The Method for Calculating Parallax in Longitude and Latitude—

> The sine of the difference between the Sun and the *Vitribha Lagna*, divided by 130 <note>original: "khadhāma", referring to 30 multiplied by something, here used as a divisor</note>, gives the parallax in time <term>lambana</term> in ghaṭīs.
> Multiply that by the sine of the altitude, and divide by 120 <note>original: "nakhendu"</note>; this should be added to or subtracted from the lunar day <term>tithi</term> in order. ॥ 2 ॥
> Depending on whether the Sun is greater or less than the *Vitribha Lagna*, one must repeatedly calculate the ascendant and parallax.
> The sine of the zenith distance, added to twelve and divided by eight, gives the parallax in latitude <term>nati</term> in digits <term>aṅgulas</term>, moving in the direction of the zenith distance. ॥ 3 ॥

<summary>This page details the mathematical steps to calculate the zenith distance and altitude for a solar eclipse, using a specific historical example from 1600 CE in Sirohi. It explains how to adjust the Sun and Moon's positions for parallax, which is the apparent shift in their coordinates based on the observer's specific location on Earth.</summary>
<keywords>Solar eclipse, Parallax (Lambana), Zenith distance (Natamsha), Altitude (Unnatamsha), Vitribha Lagna, Sirohi, Sumatiharsha, Sanskrit astronomy, 1600 CE</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical calculation of solar eclipse parameters from the previous page, specifically focusing on the corrections for parallax in longitude (lambana) and latitude (nati) according to the commentary of Sumatiharṣa and Sudhākara.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>66 In the Karana-kutuhala</header>

**Sumatiharṣa—** Take the sine <term>jyā; a trigonometric function similar to the modern sine but based on a specific radius</term> of the difference between the Nonagesimal <term>viri-bha-lagna; the point on the ecliptic exactly 90 degrees behind the rising point</term> and the Sun at the moment of the New Moon's end. When this is divided by thirty (30), the result is the mean parallax <term>lambana</term> in units of *ghatīs* <term>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>. When that result is multiplied by the sine of the zenith distance of the Nonagesimal and divided by one hundred and twenty (120), the "clear" or true parallax is obtained. 

If the Nonagesimal is greater than the Sun's position at the end of the New Moon, the parallax is added; if the Sun is greater, it is subtracted. This determines whether the eclipse occurs in the eastern or western hemisphere <note>original: "pūrvakapāle" and "paścimakapāle"</note>. Experts should understand that in the eastern hemisphere, it is subtracted, and in the western, it is added. The rule that "it is added when the Nonagesimal is greater" might occasionally result in an addition even in the eastern hemisphere or a subtraction in the western, therefore this specific reasoning is not always helpful on its own. 

> "When the Sun is at the mid-heaven, there is no horizontal parallax" <note>original: "madhyalagnasame bhānau harijasya na sambhava"</note>

This reasoning is seen as logical. However, whatever was accepted by the Great Teachers in their verses must be explained by our own masters in this way. By repeatedly applying this correction, the end-time of the New Moon is refined until the parallax becomes stable. For example, the tropical Nonagesimal is 5s 25° 14' 58" and the tropical Sun is 3s 18° 32' 28". Their difference is 2s 6° 43' 30". The sine of this difference <note>bhujajyā</note> is 110;12. Divided by thirty, we get 3;40 *ghatīs*. This is the mean parallax. Multiplied by the sine of the zenith distance (110;35) and divided by 120, the resulting parallax is 3;22. Since the Nonagesimal is greater than the Sun and it is in the western hemisphere, this is added to the New Moon end-time of 29;24, resulting in 32;46. 

By repeating this process <note>the iterative method used in Indian astronomy to find precise moments</note>, the solar correction is 3;16. This is added to the sunrise position of the Sun. Alternatively, multiplying by the parallax and dividing by sixty, the resulting minutes are added to the Sun's mean position if the parallax is positive, and subtracted if negative. The instantaneous Sun is then 3s 10° 38' 20", and tropical is 3s 18° 35' 40". The New Moon end is 32;46. From this, the ascendant is calculated as 9s 14° 39' 37", and the Nonagesimal as 6s 14° 39' 37". From this, the declination is 5° 53' 48" South, the zenith distance is 30° 28' 57", and the altitude is 59° 31' 3". Their sine is 103;25. The difference between the tropical Nonagesimal and the Sun is 2s 26° 13' 57". Its sine is 129;22. Divided by 30, the mean parallax is 3;59 *ghatīs*. As before, the true parallax is 3;25. Due to the western hemisphere, this is added to the time 29;24 to get 32;49. For the north, the instantaneous Sun is 3s 10° 38' 22", tropical is 3s 18° 35' 42". The tropical ascendant is 9s 14° 57' 21", Nonagesimal is 6s 14° 57' 21". Declination is 6° 0' 56" South. Zenith distance is 30° 36' 5" South. Altitude is 59° 23' 55". Sine of zenith distance is 61;9. Sine of altitude is 103;16. Difference between Nonagesimal and Sun is 2s 26° 21' 39". Its sine is 111;16. Divided by 30, the mean parallax is 3;58. The true parallax becomes stable at 3;25. Adding this to the New Moon end 29;24 gives the stable end-time of 32;49. This is the time of the middle of the eclipse.

<column-break/>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>Chapter 5: On Solar Eclipses — 67</header>

Now, the calculation of the Parallax in Latitude <term>nati</term>. The sine of the zenith distance <note>natāṃśajīvā</note> should be increased by its tenth part and then divided by eight. The direction of the resulting latitude parallax is the same as the direction of the zenith distance. Just as the calculation for stable parallax was done, this gives the parallax in latitude in digits <term>aṅgulas</term>. The zenith distance is 30° 36' 5". Its sine is 61;9. This, plus its tenth part (5;5), is 66;6. Divided by eight, the result is 8;15. This is the latitude parallax in digits, and it is always South in this case. At sunrise or sunset, the maximum parallax in longitude is 4 *ghatīs*, and the maximum parallax in latitude is 16;55 digits. ॥ 2, 3 ॥

**Sudhākara—** For the sake of clarity:
In this context, the proof is that we assume the zenith distance of the Nonagesimal is equal to the desired zenith distance. With a maximum parallax in longitude of four *ghatīs*, the operations mentioned in the original text for calculating parallax are derived.

Calculation of Parallax in Latitude—The proof here is by proportion: if for the Radius <term>trijyā</term> we get the maximum latitude parallax, then what do we get for the sine of the zenith distance <note>dorjyaka</note>? Then, to convert this into digits, it is divided by three. Its form is:

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;48 × Drk-kshepa&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;16 × Drk-kshepa&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;13 × Drk-kshepa
<br/>= ——————— = ——————— = ——————— Therefore, it is proven.
<br/>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;120 × 3&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;15 × 8&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;12 × 8
<note>Drk-kshepa refers to the sine of the zenith distance of the nonagesimal</note>

An alternative method for calculating parallax:

The numerical factors are: seven and mountains (77), earth and man (41), eight and shakti (178), nine, moon, and teeth (219), arrows, three, and pairs (235), sky, birth, and ashvins (240), six, three, and ashvins (236), jin and pairs (224), and two hundred (200). These are the table values based on the difference between the Nonagesimal and the Sun. ॥ 4 ॥
The current table value, multiplied by the remaining degrees and divided by the interval, should be subtracted from or added to the previous value. Dividing by the "sky-arrow" (50) gives the parallax. As before, the clear parallax and the latitude parallax are found from the other ascendant. ॥ 5 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa—** "Seven..." etc. The values 77, 41, 178, 219, 235, 240, 236, 224, 200 are the nine "parallax-blobs" <term>pindas; table entries</term> for every eleven degrees of difference between the Nonagesimal and the Sun. They represent the parallax in units of *pala* <term>1/60th of a ghati</term>. When the difference between the Nonagesimal and the Sun is 66 degrees, the maximum parallax is 4 *ghatīs*. From this maximum, divided by eleven degrees, eight segments are formed, with the ninth table value representing ninety degrees. Now, from the difference between the Nonagesimal and the Sun calculated at the end of the New Moon, we divide by eleven... as stated in the *Karana-prakasha* <note>a famous astronomical treatise by Brahmadeva, 1092 CE</note>—

<summary>This page details the mathematical procedures for calculating 'lambana' (parallax in longitude) and 'nati' (parallax in latitude) during a solar eclipse. It provides specific numerical examples, iterative calculation methods to find the stable eclipse middle, and introduces a tabular method for faster calculation.</summary>
<keywords>lambana, nati, parallax, Nonagesimal, vitribhalagna, zenith distance, solar eclipse, Sumatiharṣa, Sudhākara, Karana-kutuhala, Hindu astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating a solar eclipse. The previous page discussed the initial calculation of parallax in longitude (lambana). This page refines those calculations and introduces parallax in latitude (nati), the diameters of the celestial bodies (bimba), and the duration of the eclipse (sthiti).</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
<header>In the Karaṇakutūhala</header>

> "The parallax in *ghatīs* <term>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term> is made clear once, as before, by dividing the Earth's radius—multiplied by the sine of the difference between the Nonagesimal <term>viri-bha-lagna; the point on the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the rising point</term> and the Sun—by eighty-eight." <note>original: "vittribhalagnāntara..."</note>

Now, take the difference between the past and future tabular values and multiply it by the remainder; divide this by eleven. Add this to the past value if the future value is greater, or subtract it if the future value is less. When the corrected past value is divided by sixty, the result is the mean parallax <term>lambana</term> as described before. This is then multiplied by the sine of the zenith distance and divided by one hundred and twenty-one <note>original: "nakhendu"; literally "fingernails" (20) and "moon" (1), representing 121</note>. This process can be done even once to be effective. 

Thus, the parallax in latitude <term>nati</term> must be calculated using the zenith distance and other factors at the moment the Nonagesimal is corrected by parallax. For example, at the end of the New Moon, the difference between the Nonagesimal and the Sun is 2 | 6 | 42 | 30. The "arm" degrees <term>bhuja; the angular distance used for sine calculations</term> are 66. Divided by eleven, the result is 6. The 6th past value is 2 | 40 and the future is 2 | 36. Multiplying the difference between these values by the remainder 0 | 42 | 27 and dividing by eleven, we get 0 | 15 | 27. Since the future value is less, we subtract this from the past value to get 2 | 39 | 44 | 33. Divided by sixty, the mean parallax is 3 | 59. Multiplied by the sine of the altitude of the Nonagesimal (11 | 135) and divided by 121, the resulting parallax in time is 3 | 40. Because the Nonagesimal is greater than the Sun, this is added to the New Moon time (29 | 24), resulting in the fixed middle of the eclipse: 33 | 4. To find the parallax in latitude <term>nati</term> from the other coordinates: the Sun is at 3 | 0 | 38 | 37, the Ascendant is 9 | 16 | 26 | 7, and the Nonagesimal is 6 | 16 | 26 | 7. The declination is 6 | 46 | 37. The southern zenith distance is 3 | 19 | 40. Its sine is 61 | 58. Adding its own twelfth part (5 | 9), we get 67 | 7. Dividing by eight, the result is 8 | 23. This is the parallax in latitude <term>nati</term>, which should be known as useful when applying the single-step method for parallax. // 5 //

**Sudhākara—** Whatever parallax is derived from the tabular values should be made "clear" (refined) as before. This means multiplying by the sine of the altitude and dividing by 120. The rest is for the sake of clarity.

The rationale here is that even though the tabular values are approximate, they are provided to simplify the process and avoid repetitive calculations.

### Calculation of Duration and Other Factors—

> "In this calculation, the Moon's latitude <term>śara; literally 'arrow', the angular distance from the ecliptic</term> is corrected by the parallax in latitude. From this, the magnitude of the eclipse is found as before, and then the duration. The parallax is calculated separately for the beginning and end of the New Moon, as determined by the calculation." // 6 //

> "By applying the positive or negative correction to that, the corrected latitude and duration for that specific time are calculated. These are applied again to the calculated time of the New Moon until the results are consistent." // 7 //

<column-break/>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Solar Eclipses—5</header>

**Sumatiharṣa—** "Corrected" <note>original: "spaṣṭa"</note> means the Moon's latitude is calculated from the position of the Moon and its Node at the end of the New Moon (corrected by the fixed parallax). If the latitude and the parallax in latitude <term>nati</term> are in the same direction, they are added; if in different directions, the difference is the corrected latitude. Using this corrected latitude, the magnitude of the eclipse <term>channa</term> and the duration <term>sthiti</term> are calculated. For example, by the single-step method, the end of the New Moon is 32 | 49. At that time, the Moon is 3 | 12 | 4 | 46 and the Node is 2 | 26 | 21 | 38. Their sum is 5 | 27 | 43 | 24. The "arm" <term>bhuja</term> is 0 | 2 | 16 | 36. The sine is 4 | 46. Multiplied by three (14 | 18) and divided by 4, the latitude is 3 | 34 North. The parallax in latitude <term>nati</term> is 8 | 55 South. Since the directions are different, their difference is the corrected latitude: 4 | 41 South.

### Now, the Calculation of the Disks.

The Moon's disk is found by taking the Moon's daily motion (8 | 19 | 4) and dividing by seventy-four, resulting in 11 | 4 digits <term>aṅgula; a traditional unit of measure</term>. The Sun's daily motion (56 | 58) is multiplied by two (113 | 56) and divided by eleven, giving the Sun's disk as 10 | 21. In a solar eclipse, the Sun (10 | 22) is the object to be covered <term>chādya</term> and the Moon (11 | 4) is the coverer <term>chādaka</term>. Half the sum of their diameters is 10 | 43. Subtracting the latitude (4 | 41), we get 6 | 1. This is the magnitude of the eclipse <term>channa</term>. To find the "points" of the eclipse <term>viśvās</term>, multiply the magnitude (6 | 1) by twenty (120 | 20) and divide by the Sun's disk (10 | 21), resulting in 11 | 37 points. Now for the duration: multiply the magnitude by two (9 | 22) and add the magnitude (6 | 1), which gives 15 | 23. Multiply these and take the square root: 9 | 37. Multiply by 180 | 17 | 31 and divide by the difference in the speeds of the Moon and Sun (762 | 6) to get the half-duration in *ghatīs*: 2 | 16. Generally, in solar eclipses, there is no total obscuration <term>vimarda</term>. // 7 //

### Calculation of Contact and Release—

> "The times of contact <term>pragraha</term> and release <term>mukti</term> are made clear. When the parallax is calculated once, they occur once. The duration between the middle of the eclipse and these points is clarified; the remaining rules are as stated in the section on lunar eclipses." // 8 //

**Sumatiharṣa—** The time of the New Moon's end is calculated as before. To find the time of contact, subtract the duration; to find the release, add the duration. For these times, the parallax must be recalculated. This is done repeatedly <term>asakṛt</term> until the values for the corrected latitude and duration no longer change. Thus, the exact moments of contact and release are determined. If the parallax is only calculated once, the times of contact and release are only approximate.

<vocab>Karaṇakutūhala, lambana, nati, vittribhalagna, sūryagrahaṇa, bimbanayana, sparśa, mokṣa, Sudhākara, Sumatiharṣa</vocab>

# In the Karaṇakutūhala

## Chapter 5: On Solar Eclipses

The differences between the time of contact <term>sparśa; the beginning of the eclipse</term>, the time of release <term>mukti/mokṣa; the end of the eclipse</term>, and the time of the middle of the eclipse <term>madhyagrahaṇa</term> are the true durations of the first and second halves of the eclipse. Specifically, the difference between the time of contact and the middle is the duration of the first half <term>sparśa-sthiti</term>, and the difference between the middle and the time of release is the duration of the second half <term>mokṣa-sthiti</term>.

For example, if the mean duration <term>madhya-sthiti</term> is 2;16 <note>Values are often given in ghaṭīs and vighaṭīs, where 1 ghaṭī = 24 minutes</note> and the calculated end of the lunar day <term>tithy-anta</term> is 29;24, then subtracting the duration for the contact phase gives 27;8. At this time, the Sun's position is 3;0;32;19. At the desired time of 27;8, the tropical <term>sāyana; adjusted for precession</term> Sun is 3;18;30;19. For convenience, the tropical nonagesimal <term>vittribha; the point 90 degrees behind the rising sign</term> is 8;13;17;5. The nonagesimal is 5;13;17;5. The declination <term>krānti</term> is 6;40;58. The zenith distance <term>natāṃśa</term> is 27;54;11. The altitude <term>unnatāṃśa</term> is 62;5;49. Its sine <term>jyā</term> is 114;2. The difference between the nonagesimal and the Sun is 1;24;46;46. Its sine is 97;44. Divided by eighty-eight <note>original: "kharāma"; a code for 88</note>, the mean parallax is 3;15. The true parallax <term>lambana</term> is 3;15, which is added. Thus, the parallax added to the calculated time of 27;8 results in 30;13. To find the duration, at this time (30;13), the Sun is 3;0;35;55. To find the parallax in latitude <term>nati</term>, the tropical nonagesimal is 5;29;33;37. The zenith distance is 24;24;33. The sine of the zenith distance of the meridian is 49;22. The sine of the parallax in latitude is 6;41. At this time (30;13), the year is 3;0;46;14. The ascending node <term>pāta</term> is 2;26;21;31. Their sum is 5;27;7;45. The celestial latitude <term>śara</term> is 4;31 North. When corrected for the parallax in latitude, the true celestial latitude is 2;10 South. Half the sum of the diameters <term>mānayogārdha</term> is 10;42. Subtracting the latitude (2;10) gives 8;32, which is the magnitude <term>channa; the portion of the Sun obscured</term>. From the latitude (2;10) multiplied by two (4;20), added to the magnitude (8;32), we get 12;52. From this, the square root <note>The text describes a geometric formula to find the duration based on the radii and latitude</note> is 10;14, 7... the root is 10;29. The duration is 2;28. By this, the calculated end of the lunar day (29;24) minus the contact duration is 26;56. Adding the separately calculated parallax of 3;15, the rough time of contact is 30;12. To refine this further through repeated calculation <term>asakṛt-karaṇa</term>, at that time the Sun is 3;0;35;44. The tropical Sun is 3;18;33;40. The tropical nonagesimal is 5;28;30;16. The North declination is 0;36;5. The zenith distance is 23;59;4. The altitude is 66;0;56. Its sine is 109;24. The difference between the nonagesimal and the Sun is 2;9;57. Its sine is 91;57. The parallax is 3;45. Adding this to the mean tropical time (27;8) gives 30;33. For the duration at 30;33, the Sun is 3;0;36;14 and the Moon is 3;0;57;47. The node is 2;26;21;32. The tropical Sun is 3;18;33;34. The tropical nonagesimal is 6;1;32;35. The South zenith distance is 25;12;23. The altitude is 64;47;37. The parallax in latitude is 6;53. The Moon with its node is 5;27;12;19. The North latitude is 4;24. The true latitude corrected for parallax is 2;29. The magnitude is 8;13. The duration is 2;28. At the end of the lunar day (26;56), the previously obtained parallax was 3;25. The addition is 3;21. Now, for the purpose of repeated calculation, at 30;21, the Sun is 3;0;26;12. The nonagesimal is 6;0;21;34. The South zenith distance is 24;43;49. The altitude is 65;16;11.

The sine is 108;44. The difference between the nonagesimal and the Sun is 2;11;48;52. Its sine is 113;54. The parallax is 3;47. The fixed parallax is 3;25. 31;22 <note>Values are given in ghaṭīs and vighaṭīs; 1 ghaṭī = 24 minutes</note>. In this, the parallax <term>lambana; parallax in longitude</term> which was set aside separately, being 3;27, is added. The result is 34;49. This is the rough time of release <term>mokṣa-kāla; the end of the eclipse</term>. For the sake of iterative calculation <term>asakṛt-karma</term>, the Sun's position at this time (34;49) is 3;18;37;37. The elapsed time of night is 1;23. The tropical nonagesimal <term>sāyana-vittribha; the point on the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the rising sign, adjusted for precession</term> at this time is 6;26;47;27. Using the two degrees of declination <term>krānti</term> (10;30;20), the zenith distance <term>natāṃśa</term> is 35;5;11 and the altitude <term>unnatāṃśa</term> is 54;54;49. The sine <term>jyā</term> of these is 97;53. The difference between the nonagesimal and the Sun is 8;9;50. In this location, the release occurs at night; therefore, the time of the release's visibility for others must be calculated. Now, for the parallax, the sine of the difference between the nonagesimal and the Sun (3;8;19;50) is the base <term>bhuja</term> (2;29;50;10). Here, even if not explicitly stated, because a sine cannot exist without a base and a perpendicular <term>koṭi</term>, it is said in other texts: "Wherever a sine is prescribed, one must first determine the base and then calculate the sine." The value is 128;22. Divided by thirty <note>original: "kharāma"; a word-numeral where kha=0 and rāma=3</note>, the parallax is 3;56. The previous parallax was 3;13. Since the nonagesimal is greater than the Sun's position, this is additive <term>dhana</term>. This is added to the end of the lunar day <term>tithy-anta</term> (30;40) plus the mean duration, resulting in 34;53. Now, for the duration <term>sthiti</term> at 34;53: the Sun is 3;0;40;21, the Moon is 3;9;49;56, and the Node <term>pāta; Rahu</term> is 2;26;21;57. The Moon's latitude <term>śara</term> in digits <term>aṅgula</term> is 2;50. For the parallax in latitude <term>nati</term>, the tropical nonagesimal at this time is 6;27;11;7. With the declination (10;39;0), the southern zenith distance is 35;14;9 and the altitude is 54;45;51. The sine of the zenith distance is 68;54. The parallax in latitude <term>nati</term> is 9;19. The latitude adjusted by the parallax <term>nati-saṃskṛta-śara</term> is 6;29. The "shadow" <term>chhatra</term> is 4;13. From twice the latitude (12;58), adding the shadow and dividing, the duration is 1;58. This is added to the calculated end of the lunar day (29;20), totaling 31;22. To this, the separate parallax (3;12) is added, resulting in 34;35. Because this must be calculated iteratively, one should find the nonagesimal from this time and calculate the parallax as before. One should apply the parallax to the end of the lunar day plus the mean duration. If it is more, it is additive; if less, subtractive. Thus, having brought the duration from the time adjusted by parallax, and adding it to the end of the lunar day, the parallax should be applied again. The difference between this fixed time of release and the fixed middle of the eclipse is the true duration of the second half. 

Now, for the "once-off" <term>sakṛt; simplified</term> method: The calculated end of the lunar day plus mean duration is 31;40. At this time, the difference between the nonagesimal and the Sun is 2;19;34;26. The degrees are 79. Divided by eleven <note>original: "bhava"; representing the 11 Rudras</note>, the quotient is 7. The index <term>piṇḍa</term> is 236, with a remainder of 2;34;26. The parallax is 3;53. The sine of the altitude is 105;55. Multiplying by 120 <note>original: "nakhendu"; nakha=20, indu=1</note> and dividing, we get 3;25. As before, this is added to 31;40, giving 35;5. This is the fixed time of release by the once-off method. From this, the parallax in latitude and the Moon's latitude are to be calculated. Now, for the parallax at the time of contact <term>sparśa</term>: the elapsed time from sunrise is 30;21 ghaṭīs. At this time, the contact occurs. Following the rule "of the ghaṭīs elapsed since midday," the hour angle <term>nata</term> is found as follows: midday is...

Dividing by the radius (120), the resulting true deflection for the release <term>mokṣa-valana</term> in digits is 5;40 southern. || 8 ||

### Knowledge of the Colors of the Sun and Moon in an Eclipse

> An eclipse of the Sun equal to one-twelfth of its disk, or an eclipse of the Moon equal to one-sixteenth of its disk, should not even be predicted, though it be a partial one. ||
> In an eclipse less than half, the Moon appears smoky; if half, it is black; if more than half, it is variegated (brownish-red); but the Sun is always black in all total eclipses. || 9 ||

**Sumatiharṣa's Commentary:** An eclipse of the Sun equal to one-twelfth of the solar disk's measure should not be predicted. An eclipse of the Moon equal to one-sixteenth of the lunar disk's measure should not be spoken of <note>because they are too small to be easily seen by the naked eye</note>. In a minor eclipse, the Moon appears smoky in color; in a half-eclipse, it is black; in a total eclipse, it is reddish-brown <term>piśaṅga</term>. The Sun, however, is always black during any total eclipse. A solar eclipse of more than twenty "biswas" <note>a unit of measure</note> does not...

# Chapter 6: Rising and Setting

**Calculation for the Rising and Setting of Jupiter —**

> The desired count of days <term>ahargaṇa; the total number of days elapsed from a fixed epoch</term> is increased by one-tenth of the years elapsed since the epoch, and decreased by one hundred and five. This result is divided by three hundred and ninety-nine <note>original: "nandanavāgnibhiḥ," where Nanda=9, Navā=9, Agni=3, read right-to-left as 399, the synodic period of Jupiter in days</note>. If the remainder is fifteen, it is the rising <term>udgama; helical rising</term> of Jupiter. || 1 ||
> If the remainder is three hundred and eighty-four, it is the setting <term>asta; helical setting</term>. If the remainder is greater or less than these values, the days passed or remaining [until the event] are found. The correction for the Sun's position <term>arkaghaṭīphala</term> at that time is multiplied by twelve <note>original: "sūryāhataṃ," literally "struck by the Sun," where Sun refers to 12</note> and applied to the remainder, just as with lunar days. || 2 ||

> For the rising, two signs are added to the Sun; for the setting, three signs are added. The correction for the Sun's position is then calculated as before, using thirty <note>original: "khaguṇaiḥ"</note> to find the precise positive or negative adjustment. From the transition <term>saṅkrānti; the entry of the Sun into a new zodiac sign</term>, the rising-time is subtracted from three hundred and thirty. Multiplied by fifteen and divided by its own rising, the result is added for Jupiter's rising and subtracted for its setting. || 2 ||

**Sumatiharṣa** — The desired total count of days is increased by one-tenth of the years elapsed in the manual's epoch. It is then decreased by one hundred and five (105). This is divided by three hundred and ninety-nine (399). The quotient is discarded as it serves no purpose; if the remainder is fifteen (15), the rising of Jupiter is known. If the remainder is three hundred and eighty-four (384) or more, the rising or setting has passed; if less, they are yet to come. Now, regarding the clarification of the remainder: the "Sun at that time" <term>tātkālārka</term> refers to the sign and degree the Sun occupies at that moment. Its time-correction <term>ghaṭīphala</term> is multiplied by twelve (12) and divided by sixty to convert it into days, which are then added to or subtracted from the remainder. In the various transitions <term>saṅkrāntis</term>, the correction values for the two fortnights are: "In Cancer and Capricorn, it is three and six (3, 6); in Leo and Aquarius, eight and ten (8, 10); in Virgo and Pisces, eleven and eleven (11, 11); in Libra and Aries, eleven and ten (11, 10); in Taurus and Scorpio, eight and six (8, 6); in Sagittarius and Gemini, three and zero (3, 0). These solar minutes are subtracted starting from Cancer and added starting from Capricorn."

The Sun's fifteen degrees should be converted; these are the minutes of the equation of the center <term>mandaphala</term>. These are the established minutes <term>ghaṭikās</term>. Here, the minutes doubled and divided by nine become degrees. These are divided by the difference in mean motion... for the sake of simplicity here, the minutes are multiplied by twelve (12) and whatever is obtained by [dividing by] sixty are the days. By the compassion of the wise, for the sake of ease, the "Sun's time-correction" is stated repeatedly: in the first half of Cancer and Capricorn, it is three minutes (3)... thus, it is subtracted in Cancer and added in Capricorn. Because the clarification of the remainder at the rising...

<meta>This page continues the specific mathematical procedures for determining the visibility of Jupiter (rising and setting), building upon the "day-count" (ahargaṇa) method introduced on the previous page. It provides a worked example from the year 1543 of the Śaka era.</meta>

# 76
# In the Karaṇakutūhala

Once the value is determined, a correction factor <note>original: "ghaṭī-phalam"</note> for the Sun is calculated from the position of the Sun increased by two signs <term>rāśi; a zodiac sign of 30 degrees</term>, or from the Sun's position at setting increased by three signs, as taught by the Teacher Brahma <note>Likely referring to the 7th-century astronomer Brahmagupta</note>. This value is then multiplied by thirty <note>original: "kha-guṇaiḥ"</note>. This correction is added or subtracted: it is negative when the Sun is in the southern hemisphere and positive when in the northern hemisphere.

**Further Clarification —** Now, regarding the rising at the time of the solar transit <term>saṅkrānti; the entry of the Sun into a new zodiac sign</term>: from the rising value of the Sun's sign of transit (determined by the "Three Problems" <note>Referring to the chapter on time, place, and direction</note>), subtract three hundred <note>original: "kha-kha-agni"</note> and thirty. Multiply the result by fifteen <note>representing the lunar days or "tithis"</note>. Divide this by the rising time of the sign where the transit occurs. The quotient obtained should be added to the remainder for the rising of Jupiter.

**Calculation of the Setting —** Regarding the setting, the same calculation is performed using the rising of the seventh sign from the Sun's transit sign, but subtracting three hundred. The resulting value is treated as a negative correction to the remainder. If the clear remainder is greater than fifteen, the rising is "yet to come" <term>gamya; future</term>; if less, the rising has "already occurred" <term>gata; past</term>. The setting is understood similarly by comparing with three hundred and eighty-four <note>original: "veda-gaja-agnibhiḥ"</note>.

**Example Calculation —** In the Śaka year 1543 <note>approximately 1621 CE</note>, on Tuesday, the 4th day of the dark fortnight of the month of Āṣāḍha:
The years elapsed are 438. The intercalary months <term>adhikamāsa</term> are 162. The total day-count <term>ahargaṇa</term> is 160,074.
To this, we add one-tenth of the years (43 and 48 minutes), totaling 160,117 and 48 minutes.
Subtracting one hundred and five <note>original: "pañcābhra-bhuvi"</note>, we get 160,012 and 48 minutes.
Dividing this by three hundred and ninety-nine <note>original: "nandana-v-agnibhiḥ"</note>, the quotient is not needed, but the remainder is 13 and 48 minutes. Since this is less than fifteen, the rising is "future" <note>meaning it hasn't happened yet</note>.

**Clarification of the Remainder —** Now, with the Sun in the second half of Taurus <note>Vṛṣa</note>, the solar correction (6) is multiplied by twelve, giving 72. Dividing by sixty, we get 1 day and 12 minutes. Since it is calculated from Capricorn <note>Makara</note>, the remainder (13;48) becomes 15;10 when added. Further, the Sun is at 11 signs and 29 degrees. For the rising, adding 2 signs gives 3 signs and 29 degrees. Its solar correction (6) multiplied by thirty is 1800. Dividing by sixty gives 30. Because it is calculated from Cancer <note>Karka</note>, the result is subtracted from the remainder 15;10, leaving 12.

Again, the rising of the transit sign is 256. Subtracting three hundred is not possible here, so the subtraction is performed inversely. 300 minus 256 equals 44. Multiplied by 15, we get 660. Dividing by the Taurus rising time (256 pulses <term>pala; a unit of time equal to 24 seconds</term>), the result is 2 days, 34 minutes, and 41 seconds. Because it is for Jupiter's rising, this is added to the remainder, but since the subtraction was inverse, the rule is reversed. The remainder 15;10 minus 2;34;41 results in a clear remainder of 9;25;19. The difference between this and fifteen is 5;34;41. Because the remainder is less than fifteen, the event is "future" <term>agamya</term>. The same process applies to the setting as described in the verse. || 2 ||

---

**Sudhākara —** The "solar correction for that time" refers to the value spoken by Bavva <note>An earlier commentator or astronomer</note>. Note that if the solar correction is multiplied by twelve before being applied, the result is precise <term>sūkṣma</term>; otherwise, it is approximate <term>sthūla</term>. The values spoken by Bavva are:

<column-break/>

# 77
# Chapter 6: Rising and Setting

->In Cancer and Capricorn, the value is three; in Leo and Aquarius, eight and ten;<-
->In Virgo and Pisces, eleven; in Libra and Aries, eleven and ten;<-
->In Taurus and Scorpio, eight and six; in Sagittarius and Gemini, three and zero.<-
->These solar corrections are positive from Capricorn onwards and negative from Cancer onwards.<-

Treating this correction as a fixed "equation of center" <term>mandocca; apogee/correction factor</term>, one should calculate the correction <term>mandaphala</term> for the Sun in each sign. Convert those minutes into hours/minutes <term>ghaṭikā</term> by dividing by twelve and place them in the Sun's position. For example, if the Sun is at 10 signs (Capricorn) and the anomaly <term>kendra</term> is 5, the correction is positive. By the previous method, the correction in minutes (65) divided by twelve gives 6. This is the method for all signs; the rest is for clarity.

**Rationale —** In a great cycle <term>kalpa</term>, the number of conjunctions of Jupiter and the Sun is equal to the difference in their revolutions. Therefore, by proportion: if the difference in revolutions corresponds to the total Earth-days, what time corresponds to a single conjunction? This gives the period of conjunction <gloss>Total Earth-days / Difference in Revolutions</gloss>.

<note>The image shows a continued fraction used to approximate the synodic period of Jupiter. It calculates the ratio to be approximately 398 and a fraction, eventually simplified to 399 days.</note>

The approximate values for this are: 399, 398 and 8/9, or 398 and 161/181, and so on. Here, the Teacher has accepted 399. Because of this, over ten years, the difference between the actual value and the approximation results in a shift of 62 or 61 remainder days; thus, one extra day must be added every ten years. Since the start of this text, the conjunction period has been calculated and the count adjusted accordingly. If the remainder in a 399-day cycle is fifteen, it is Jupiter's rising, because the "time-degrees" <term>kālāṃśa; degrees of separation from the Sun required for visibility</term> for Jupiter are fifteen. Thus, fifteen days before the conjunction, Jupiter sets, or when the remainder equals the conjunction period minus fifteen days. However, this is further adjusted by Jupiter's own correction <term>mandaphala</term>. If the Sun's apogee is at 2 signs 15 degrees and Jupiter's...

# 78
# In the Karaṇakutūhala

The correction factor for time <note>original: "ghaṭī-phalam"</note> is calculated with a one-third adjustment here. For the rising, Jupiter = Sun - 15'. Jupiter's mean position = 6 + 15' - Sun = 8 + 15' - (Sun + 2). Its "arm" <term>bhuja; the distance from the nearest cardinal point in a circle</term> = 2 + 15' - (Sun + 2). Therefore, it should be calculated with the one-third adjustment; then, when the Sun is equal, the correction for Jupiter in time-units <note>original: "ghaṭīs"</note> is produced. This is divided by the Sun's maximum equation of center <term>manda-phala; the correction for the planet's elliptical orbit</term> and multiplied by Jupiter's maximum equation of center. Then, multiplying by twelve gives the Jupiter-correction in minutes of arc and so on.

The formulaic form is: Jupiter's correction = (Sun's correction × 5 × 12) / 2 = 30.
This correction for time, when divided by the speed of the center, gives the days = (30 × Sun's time-correction) / 55.
Multiplying by sixty gives the value in minutes <note>original: "ghaṭī"</note>. This should be applied there. Now, the rising at the time of the solar transit <term>saṅkrānti; the entry of the Sun into a new zodiac sign</term> must be known. The difference between the arc minutes of the sign and the equatorial arc minutes = 6 × Rising - 1800. Thus, by proportion: if this difference corresponds to the rising time in seconds <note>original: "palas"</note>, what is obtained from the seconds of the degrees of time? The resulting difference in minutes of arc, divided by sixty, will be in days and so on. Its form is:
= [6 (Rising - 300) × 15 × 10] / (Rising × 60) = [15 (Rising - 300)] / Rising. 
Because the difference is small, fifteen degrees of time <note>original: "kālāṃśa"</note> are taken. From this, everything else follows simply.

## Calculation of the Rising and Setting of Venus—

> Subtract one hundred and fifteen from the total day-count, and add one-sixteenth of the elapsed years of the era. Divide this by five hundred and eighty-four.
> With the remainders, the rising and setting of Venus in the west occur respectively at thirty-six and two hundred and eighty-seven.
> With the remainders two hundred and ninety-seven and five hundred and forty-eight, the rising and setting in the east occur. At that time, the Sun’s rising value should be decreased by three hundred; for the western setting, the seventh sign from it should be used. // 3 //
> Multiply the remainder by thirty-five, five, five, and thirty-six respectively. Divide by the rising time of the sign. Add the result to the previous remainders for the rising; for the setting, subtract the result. If the subtraction is not possible, perform the subtraction in reverse and flip the signs from positive to negative or negative to positive. // 4 //

**Sumatiharṣa’s Commentary:** The total day-count <term>ahargaṇa; the number of days elapsed since the start of the epoch</term> is decreased by "five and the Lord" <note>original: "pañceśona", where Pañca=5 and Īśa=11, totaling 115</note>. Add to this one-sixteenth <note>original: "bhūpalava"</note> of the years elapsed since the start of the era <note>the year 1183 CE for this text</term>. Divide the sum by "Vedas, eights, and arrows" <note>original: "vedāṣṭabāṇaiḥ", a numerical code where Veda=4, Aṣṭa=8, and Bāṇa=5, representing 584</note>. The quotient is not used. The remainders are used as follows: at 36 <note>original: "ṣat-kṛti"</note>, the western rising occurs; at 287 <note>original: "nagādyugalaiḥ"</note>, the western setting occurs. At 297 <note>original: "nagagoyamaiḥ"</note>, the eastern rising occurs; and at 548 <note>original: "gajayugaprāṇaiḥ"</note>, the eastern setting occurs.

<column-break/>

# 79
# Chapter 6: On Rising and Setting

Now, to clarify the remainders: find the rising time <note>original: "palāni"</note> of the Sun <note>original: "tigmāṃśu", meaning 'the hot-rayed one'</note> in the sign of its current transit. Subtract three hundred <note>original: "kha-kha-agni"</note> from this value. If calculating the western rising or setting, use the rising value of the current sign minus three hundred. Then, in order: for the western rising, multiply by thirty-five <note>original: "pañcaguṇaiḥ"</note>; for the western setting, multiply by five <note>original: "śaraiḥ"</note>; for the eastern rising, multiply by five <note>original: "viṣayaiḥ"</note>; and for the eastern setting, multiply by thirty-six <note>original: "aṅgāgnisaṃkhyaiḥ"</note>. Divide this by the rising time of the sign <note>original: "svodayena"</note>. Add the resulting quotient to the previous remainders to declare the rising <note>original: "udgama"</note>. Subtract the quotient from the remainders to declare the setting. Now, he states a special rule—

"If the value to be subtracted from three hundred <note>original: "kha-kha-agni"</note> for the rising cannot be subtracted, then reverse the subtraction <note>subtract the 300 from the rising value instead</note>. Divide the result by the divisor. Whatever quotient is obtained, apply it to the remainder in reverse: what was positive <term>dhana; additive</term> becomes negative <term>ṛṇa; subtractive</term>, and what was negative becomes positive. This method should be known elsewhere as well. For example: a day-count of 160074. Subtracting 115 gives 159959. Adding 27;22 (which is one-sixteenth of the 438 elapsed years) gives 159986;22. Dividing by 584, the quotient of 273 is ignored. The remainder is 554;22. Because this is greater than 548, the eastern setting has already passed. To clarify the remainder here: since it is an eastern setting, the rising of the sign Taurus is 255. Subtraction from three hundred is not possible. Therefore, 255 is subtracted from 300, leaving 45. Because it is an eastern setting, multiply by 36, giving 1620. Divide by its own rising time, 255, giving a quotient of 6;21. Since it is a setting, this should be subtracted from the remainder 554;22, but because we reversed the subtraction earlier, we add it instead <note>original: "vyastaṃ"</note>. This gives 560;43. The difference between this and the standard value of 548 is 12;43. With this many days more than the standard remainder, the setting has passed from the desired day-count. Other calculations should be performed in their respective places in the same way." // 3, 4 //

**Sudhākara’s Commentary:** "Six-squared" <note>original: "ṣat-kṛti"</note> means thirty-six; the remainder is clear. Here is the mathematical rationale <term>upapatti</term>. The time for a synodic revolution of Venus <note>original: "bhṛgu-kendra-bhagaṇa"</note> in days is:
583 + [1 / (279013328 / (1 + (2423376164)))] ... <note>The commentator is providing a highly precise fraction for the orbital period</note>.
The Teacher has taken 584 here because the difference is very small. In sixteen years, the difference between the real value and the approximate value amounts to about one day; therefore, one day must be added every sixteen years. At the start of this work, the synodic cycles were completed five days before the epoch, so that number was subtracted. Furthermore, thirty-six days before the completion of the cycle, the eastern setting occurs, and after that, the western rising...

It includes specific corrections for the Earth's orbit and a worked example for an observer using a specific historical day-count.</summary>

# 80
## In the Karaṇakutūhala

Thus, five days before the planet reaches its lowest point, it sets in the west; afterwards, it rises in the east. Here too, the calculation is made from the difference between the local celestial degrees and the equatorial degrees. If that difference corresponds to the seconds of the rising time <note>original: "udaya-pala"</note>, then what corresponds to the thirty-six degrees and so on? That result, divided by sixty, gives the value in days. Its mathematical form is:

-> 6 (Rising - 300) × 36 × 10 <-
-> --------------------------- <-
-> Rising Time × 60 <-

-> (Rising - 300) × 36 <-
= ---------------------
-> Rising Time <-

First, in place of thirty-six, thirty-five is taken due to a small difference. For the rising, the value is positive (added); for the setting, it is negative (subtracted). In the case of reverse calculation, the signs are reversed. Everything else is clear.

### Calculating Retrograde Motion from the Degrees of the Fast Anomaly

> By fast anomaly degrees of 163, 145, 125, 167, and 113, 
> the retrograde motion of Mars and the others begins. 
> Direct motion returns when these degrees are subtracted from the full circle. || 5 ||

**Sumatiharsha:** Through repeated calculation, the stable position of Mars’s anomaly is found. If its degrees are 163 <note>original: "trinṛpa," where nṛpa/kings is 16 and tri is 3</note>, it describes the retrogradation <term>vakratā; the appearance of a planet moving backward in the sky</term> of Mars. Likewise, for the stable degrees of the fast anomaly: for Mercury, it is 145 <note>original: "śarendra"</note>; for Jupiter, 125 <note>original: "tattvendu"</note>; for Venus, 167 <note>original: "saptanṛpa"</note>; and for Saturn, 113 <note>original: "trirudra"</note>. Now, when these degrees are subtracted from the 360 degrees of the full circle, the "straight" or direct motion <term>avakratā</term> of each planet begins. 

For Mars, at 197 degrees; for Mercury, at 215 degrees; for Jupiter, at 235 degrees; for Venus, at 193 degrees; and for Saturn, at 247 degrees, the planet abandons its retrograde motion and becomes direct <term>mārgī</term>. There is a specific point in the fast anomaly where the velocity is zero—this is the moment when retrogradation begins or ends. These specific anomaly degrees are recorded in the text. For example, when Saturn is at 113 degrees of anomaly, the "arm sine" <term>bhujajyā</term> is 11018 and the "base sine" <term>koṭijyā</term> is 46142. The resulting correction for the fast anomaly is 11413, the hypotenuse is 119133, and the daily motion of the anomaly is 57' 8". After applying the velocity correction of 59' 8", the planet's fast motion is 39' 8", and when subtracted, the result is 0' 6". This represents the true velocity. The same logic applies at 247 degrees for Saturn, and should be understood for all planets. || 5 ||

### The Rising and Setting Times of Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn

> Mars rises in the east at 28 degrees, Jupiter at 14, and Saturn at 17. 
> These same degrees, subtracted from the full circle, 
> mark their setting in the west. || 6 ||

<column-break/>

# 81
## Chapter 6: On Rising and Setting

**Sumatiharsha:** "In the east" means: Mars rises in the east when the anomaly degrees are at 28. Similarly, Jupiter rises at 14 and Saturn at 17. When these rising degrees are subtracted from the 360 degrees of the circle, the setting of that planet in the west occurs. Thus, Mars sets in the west at 332 degrees; Jupiter at 346 degrees; and Saturn at 343 degrees. As it is said in the *Siddhanta Shiromani*: "A planet with a velocity higher than the Sun rises in the east and sets in the west; for others, it is the opposite." || 6 ||

### Determining the Rising and Setting of Mercury and Venus

> For Mercury and Venus, the western rising is at 50 and 24 degrees, and the western setting is at 155 and 177. 
> The eastern rising is at 205 and 183, and the eastern setting is at 310 and 336. || 7 ||

**Sumatiharsha:** At 50 degrees of the fast anomaly, Mercury rises in the west; at 24 degrees, Venus rises in the west. At 155 degrees, Mercury sets in the west; at 177 degrees, Venus sets in the west. Furthermore, Mercury rises in the east at 205 degrees and Venus at 183. Mercury sets in the east at 310 degrees and Venus at 336. 

Mercury and Venus, while moving directly, rise in the west; then, having attained retrograde motion, they disappear there. Then, rising in the east, they move from retrograde back to direct motion and set there. This is their special behavior. If the difference between the true Sun and the true planet equals the "time-degrees" <term>kālāṃśa; the distance from the Sun required for a planet to become visible</term> to be mentioned later, then the rising or setting will occur. However, when this is calculated using the difference between the mean Sun and the true Sun, the rising and setting are considered only approximate. A planet becomes visible at a certain anomaly degree; that degree is called the indicator of rising/setting. Alternatively, one can calculate the correction for these fast anomaly degrees. The sum of those degrees and the planet's own "time-degrees" determines visibility. These degrees are the same whether the planet is east or west of the Sun. 

For example: if the Sun is at 11s 7° 56' 14" and Jupiter is corrected by its slow anomaly to 10s 23° 56' 14", the fast anomaly is 0s 4°. The "arm sine" is 29, the "base sine" is 116, the hypotenuse is 42' 21", and the fast anomaly correction is 2° 13' 52". Thus, the true Jupiter is at 10s 26° 17' 6". The mean Sun is 11s 7° 56' 14". The difference is 0s 11° 14' 6", which is nearly equal to the 11 "time-degrees" required for Jupiter. Therefore, a rising is possible here. Because the difference is small, it is ignored in this approximate calculation. || 7 ||

### Calculating the Days for Retrograde Motion and Other States

> The degrees specified for direct motion, retrogradation, or setting, 
> subtracted from or added to the current degrees, are converted to minutes. 
> When divided by the daily motion of the fast anomaly, 
> the result is the days past or remaining until the start of direct motion, retrogradation, or visibility. || 8 ||

# 82
## In the Karaṇakutūhala

**Sumatiharsha:** — Regarding the direct <note>original: "mārga"</note> and retrograde <note>original: "vakra"</note> motions mentioned: if the current degrees of the fast anomaly are greater than the prescribed degrees, the difference (converted into minutes/seconds) should be divided by the speed of the fast anomaly. The resulting days and minutes indicate the time that has passed since the planet began that motion. If the current anomaly degrees are less than the prescribed degrees, the resulting days indicate the time remaining until that motion begins. In the present calculation, there is no planet currently in retrograde, so an example for that is not shown.

An example of direct motion: For Mars <note>original: "Bhauma"</note>, the fast anomaly is 6 signs, 22 degrees, 33 minutes, 47 seconds. Its speed is 31 minutes, 7 seconds. The prescribed anomaly for direct motion in this text is 6 signs, 17 degrees, 0 minutes, 0 seconds. The difference between these is 5 degrees, 33 minutes, and 45 seconds, which equals 20,025 seconds. Dividing this by the speed of the fast anomaly (expressed in seconds) gives 10 days, 43 minutes, and 32 seconds. Since the current anomaly is greater than the prescribed value, the direct motion began 10 days ago from the desired time. Thus, on the 3rd day of the dark half of the month of Vaiśākha, a Wednesday, at 16 hours and 28 minutes after sunrise, Mars was in direct motion. This same process should be applied to all planets.

To determine the rising of Mercury in the west: the fast anomaly is 2 signs, 3 degrees, 8 minutes, 43 seconds. The prescribed anomaly is 1 sign, 20 degrees, 0 minutes, 0 seconds. The difference is 13 degrees, 8 minutes, 43 seconds, or 47,323 seconds. Dividing by the speed of 180 minutes and 24 seconds, the result is 4 days, 22 minutes, and 19 seconds. The seconds are negligible and are discarded. Because the current value is greater than the prescribed value, the rising has already occurred. Thus, on the 8th day of the dark half of Vaiśākha, a Tuesday, at 37 hours and 41 minutes after sunrise, the western rising of Mercury took place. The others are calculated similarly. || 8 ||

### Degrees of Visibility and Planetary Nodes —

> The visibility degrees from the Sun are: seventeen, thirteen, eleven, 
> nine, and fifteen. For the Moon, it is twelve. 
> For a retrograde planet, subtract one from these values. 
> The nodes of Mars and the others begin with the sign of Rudra (11).
> The degrees for these nodes are: eight, nine, eight, zero, and seventeen.
> These should be adjusted by the inverse of their own fast anomaly results. || 9 ||

**Sumatiharsha:** — Starting from the Moon, the visibility arcs <term>kālāṃśa; the specific distance in degrees a planet must be from the Sun to become visible to the naked eye</term> are: Moon 12, Mars 17, Mercury 13, Jupiter 11, Venus 9, and Saturn 15. For a retrograde <note>original: "anṛjoḥ" or "vaktriṇaḥ"</note> planet, subtract one <note>original: "kurahitāḥ"</note>. Thus, the visibility arcs for retrograde planets are: Mars 16, Mercury 12, Jupiter 10, Venus 8, and Saturn 14. 

Now, the planetary nodes <term>pāta; the points where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic</term> starting from Mars: The positions are given as signs and degrees. Mars: 11 signs, 18 degrees; Mercury: 11s 9°; Jupiter: 9s 8°; Venus: 10s 0°; Saturn: 8s 17°. These nodes must be adjusted by the inverse of their fast anomaly correction <note>original: "śīghra-phala"</note>: if the correction was positive, subtract it; if negative, add it. This yields the "true" node. For Mercury and Venus, the nodes should be adjusted using their slow anomaly corrections <note>original: "manda-phala"</note> just like the planets themselves. Some people apply the inverse slow correction for these two, but that is incorrect; they do not understand the true rationale <note>original: "vāsanā"</note>. || 9 ||

<column-break/>

# 83
## Chapter 6: Rising and Setting

**Sudhākara:** — "Retrograde" here refers specifically to Venus and Mercury. The nodes for Mars and the others in signs and degrees are: Mars 11s 8°; Mercury 11s 9°; Jupiter 9s 8°; Venus 10s 0°; Saturn 8s 17°. The minutes of latitude <note>original: "śara-kalā"</note> are: Mars 110, Mercury 152, Jupiter 76, Venus 136, Saturn 130. The rest is for the sake of clarity. The logic here follows the established astronomical treatises <note>original: "siddhānta"</note>.

### Description and Calculation of Planetary Latitude —

> Starting from Mars, the maximum latitudes are: 110, 
> 152, 76, 136, and 130 minutes. 
> To find the specific latitude: add the planet's position to its node. 
> For Mercury and Venus, use the position of their fast anomaly. 
> Take the sine of this sum, multiply by the maximum latitude, 
> and divide by the distance (hypotenuse). 
> Divide that by three to find the latitude in digits. || 10 ||

**Sumatiharsha:** — The latitudinal deflection <term>śara; literally 'arrow,' representing the planet's distance north or south of the Sun's path</term> for Mars is 110 minutes; Mercury 152; Jupiter 76; Venus 136; and Saturn 130. 

To calculate the latitude: Add the true position of the planet to its true node. (For Mercury and Venus, add the position of their "fast apex" <note>original: "śīghrocca"</note> to the node). Take the sine <note>original: "bhujajyā"</note> of this sum and multiply it by the planet's specific maximum latitude. Divide the result by the planet's "fast distance" <note>original: "śīghra-karṇa"</note>. This gives the latitude in minutes. To convert this to digits <note>original: "aṅgula"</note>, divide by three. The direction (North or South) depends on the sector of the sum. 

Example for Mercury: True Mercury is 1s 7° 27' 6". Speed is 103' 28". The slow correction is positive: 0s 15' 7". The fast apex is 3s 4° 57' 16". The fast anomaly is 2s 3° 8' 43". The distance <note>original: "karṇa"</note> is 145;15. The node is 11s 9° 0' 0". Adding the positive slow correction (0s 15' 7"), the true node becomes 11s 9° 15' 7". Adding this to the fast apex (3s 4° 57' 16") gives 2s 14° 12' 23". The sine of this is 115;6. Multiplied by the latitude (152), it equals 17485;12. Divided by the distance (145;14), the resulting latitude is 120 minutes and 27 seconds. Since the sum is in the northern hemisphere, the latitude is North. Dividing by three gives 40 digits and 9 seconds of latitude. || 10 ||

### Calculation of Visibility Corrections and Rising/Setting —

> Add or subtract three signs to the planet's position in the east or west. 
> Calculate the degrees of declination and latitude. 
> Subtract these from ninety to find the altitude degrees. 
> Find the sines of these values separately.
> Multiply the latitude by the sine of the zenith distance, 
> then divide by the sine of the altitude.
> The result is added or subtracted from the planet's position 
> based on whether the latitude and declination are in the same or opposite directions. || 11 ||
> In the west, this is done inversely. This is the visibility correction. 
> Take the planet and the Sun; the one with the smaller value 
> is considered the Sun, and the other the "body" (planet). 
> The time between them is calculated in hours (ghaṭīs) as before.

# 84, 85
## In the Karaṇakutūhala, Chapter on Rising and Setting—6

For a planet in the west, add six signs to its position. The resulting degrees, multiplied by six, are the **time-degrees** <note>original: "kālāṃśas"; these are degrees of the celestial equator that determine visibility</note>. If these are greater than the prescribed values, the rising has already occurred; if less, it is yet to happen. ॥ १२ ॥

The process for setting is the reverse. The difference in minutes between the calculated and prescribed values should be multiplied by three hundred <note>original: "khābhra-agnibhiḥ", literally "sky (0), cloud (0), fire (3)"</note>. For the Sun, this is divided by the rising time of the two signs beginning from the fourth sign. The result is the **area-minutes** <note>original: "kṣetrakalās"</note>. Divide this by the difference in daily motion (or the sum if the planet is retrograde). The result gives the days past or remaining until the rising or setting. ॥ १३ ॥

**Sumatiharsha:** — Regarding the instruction "In the east": a planet rises in the east when its position relative to the sun matches the prescribed **time-degrees** <note>such as 12, 14, or 18 degrees depending on the planet</note>. On that day, one should calculate the planet's true position. If the rising or setting occurs in the east, subtract three signs from the planet's position. If in the west, add three signs. Calculate the **declination** <note>original: "krānti"; the angular distance north or south of the celestial equator</note> of this modified position. However, this declination should not be adjusted by the planet's **celestial latitude** <note>original: "śara"</note>; it should be taken as is. It is noted in the commentary that some teachers calculate this declination by adjusting it with the latitude, but this should be known as useless. Why? Because a planet shifted by three signs does not possess a **deflection circle** <note>original: "vimaṇḍala"</note>. Therefore, only the simple declination degrees are to be determined. 

Then, if the declination and the observer's **terrestrial latitude** <note>original: "akṣa"</note> are in different directions (North/South), take their difference; if in the same direction, take their sum. This gives the **zenith distance** <note>original: "natāṃśa"</note>. Subtract this from 90 degrees to get the **altitude** <note>original: "unnatāṃśa"</note>. Now, calculate the sines <note>original: "jyā"</note> of both the zenith distance and the altitude separately. The sine of the zenith distance is multiplied by the planet's true celestial latitude (in digits, etc.) and further multiplied by three, then divided by the sine of the altitude. The resulting minutes are the **visibility correction** <note>original: "dṛkphala"</note>. 

If the latitude and the zenith distance are in the same direction, add this result to the true planet; if different, subtract it. This applies to the eastern horizon. For the western horizon, do the reverse: if the directions are the same, subtract; if different, add. The result is the "visible" position of the planet. Then, compare this corrected planet with the true position of the Sun. Whichever is smaller is designated as the "Sun," and whichever is larger is the "Rising Sign" <note>original: "lagna"</note>. The time between them in **ghatikās** <note>units of 24 minutes</note> should be calculated as before, using the remaining portion of the Sun's sign and the elapsed portion of the Rising Sign. For the western horizon, calculate the time for the Sun and the corrected planet after adding six signs to both. These ghatikās, multiplied by six, become the desired **time-degrees**. If these time-degrees are greater than those prescribed in the earlier text, the rising has already occurred; if less, it is yet to come. 

For the setting, it is the opposite: if the desired time-degrees are greater, the setting is yet to come; if less, the setting has already occurred. The difference between the prescribed and calculated time-degrees (in minutes) is multiplied by 300 and divided by the **rising time** <note>original: "udayapala"</note> of the sign where the Sun is positioned. For the west, use the rising time of the

<column-break/>

seventh sign from the Sun's position. The resulting minutes are divided by the difference between the daily motion of the planet and the Sun. If the planet is **retrograde** <note>original: "vakrī"</note>, divide by the sum of their motions. The final result is the number of days past or remaining for the rising or setting. 

For example, for a western rising: the planet with three signs added is 4s 17° 27' 6". The **tropical** <note>original: "sāyana"</note> position is 5s 5° 41' 6". The northern declination is 9° 32' 56". The southern terrestrial latitude is 24° 35' 9". Since the directions are different, their difference is 15° 2' 3", which is the zenith distance. The altitude is 74° 57' 47". The sine of the zenith distance is 3914; the sine of the altitude is 125.28 <note>the commentary uses specific sexagesimal Sine tables</note>. The zenith sine (3914) multiplied by the latitude (40' 9") equals 12471.9. Multiplied by 3, it is 37415.7. Divided by the altitude sine, the result is 32' 24", which is the visibility correction. Since the latitude and zenith distance are in different directions and it is the western horizon, this is added to the planet (11s 7° 27' 6"). The corrected visible planet is 11s 7° 59' 30". The Sun is 1s 3° 6' 12". In this western calculation, the Sun plus six signs and the planet plus six signs are used. The resulting time-degrees are 16.54. Since this is greater than the prescribed time-degrees for Mercury, the rising has already occurred. 

The difference in degrees is 3° 54', or 234 minutes. Multiplied by 300, it is 70,200. This is divided by the rising time of Scorpio (the 7th sign from the Sun's position), which is 343. The result is 204 minutes and 39 seconds of ecliptic arc. Mercury's daily motion is 10' 3" 38'''; the Sun's is 57' 28''. Their difference is 47' 10''. Dividing the arc by this difference gives 4 days, 35 minutes, and 58 seconds. Thus, the rising of Mercury in the west occurred approximately 4 and a half days before the desired time, which was the 8th day of the dark half of the month of Vaiśākha, a Tuesday, at 34 ghatikās and 2 minutes. This same method should be applied to all planets. ॥ १३ ॥

**Sudhākara:** — The meaning is clear. 
The underlying logic <note>original: "upapatti"</note> is as follows: In the disc of the planet situated on the horizon, the celestial latitude is the vertical side, the visibility correction is the base, and the distance on the horizon is the hypotenuse. Assuming a right-angled spherical triangle... <note>Sudhākara explains the geometric projection of the planet's latitude onto the ecliptic using the altitude of the ecliptic's pole</note>. The reasoning for addition and subtraction is explained in the *Gola* <note>the Spherical Astronomy section of larger treatises</note>.

### Special Instructions—
-> If the planet's visible position in the east is greater than the Sun's, or in the west is less than the Sun's, ॥ <-
-> then use the sum of the prescribed and calculated time-degrees to find the elapsed or remaining days. ॥ १४ ॥ <-

**Sumatiharsha:** — If the visible planet in the east is ahead of the Sun, or in the west is behind the Sun, one should sum the prescribed and calculated time-degrees. Use the minutes of this sum to determine the days...

<summary>This page details the mathematical procedures for calculating the heliacal rising and setting of planets, including the "visibility correction" (dṛkkarma) which accounts for a planet's celestial latitude. It provides a worked example for Mercury's rising in the western horizon, concluding that it occurred roughly 4.5 days prior to the calculation date.</summary>
<keywords>heliacal rising, heliacal setting, visibility arc, time-degrees, Mercury, declination, celestial latitude, visibility correction, Sumatiharsha, Sudhākara</keywords>
