<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for determining when celestial bodies become visible or invisible near the horizon. After establishing the general mathematical rules for planets on the previous page, the text now applies these rules to the star Agastya (Canopus) and the Moon, providing specific calculated examples from the 17th century.</meta>

# 86
## In the Karaṇakutūhala

Regarding the subject: If the desired **time-degrees** <note>original: "kālāṃśas"; degrees of the celestial equator used to measure visibility</note> are greater than the calculated value, the days that have passed are known as "elapsed"; otherwise, they are "yet to come." This means if the calculated value is greater, they are "elapsed," and if the calculated value is less, they are "yet to come." As stated in the *Siddhānta-śiromaṇi* <note>a famous 12th-century astronomical treatise by Bhāskara II</note>:
> "Indeed, if the desired time-degrees are greater than those stated, then the wise person must certainly know that the state of being 'passed' or 'future' for the days is reversed." (Siddhānta-śiromaṇi, Golādhyāya, 12) ॥ 14 ॥

**Sudhākara:** — The meaning is clear, and the proof is evident according to the standard astronomical methods.

### Statement on the Rising and Setting of Canopus—

-> Ninety-eight and seventy-eight, respectively added to and subtracted by the equinoctial shadow multiplied by eight; <-
-> When the Sun is equal to these values, the Pot-born Sage <note>original: "kumbhabhū"; a name for the star Agastya or Canopus, who was mythologically born from a water-jar</note> goes to his rising and setting, in that order. ॥ 15 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa:** — The **equinoctial shadow** <note>original: "akṣabhā"; the shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the equinox, used to determine local latitude</note> of one's own location is multiplied by eight. When this product is added to ninety-eight (98), the result is the degrees of the Sun at which the rising of Agastya (Canopus) occurs. When the product of the shadow multiplied by eight is subtracted from seventy-eight (78), and the Sun is at that degree, the setting of Agastya occurs. 

For example: in the city of Sirohi, the equinoctial shadow is 5;30 <note>5 digits and 30 minutes</note>. Multiplied by eight, this is 44;0. Adding this to the ninety-eight degrees (98) gives 142;0. Dividing by thirty to get signs, the position is 4 signs and 22 degrees <note>Leo 22°</note>. When the Sun is equal to this, Agastya rises. Now, the previously calculated 44;0 is subtracted from seventy-eight (78), which gives 34;0. In signs, this is 1 sign and 4 degrees <note>Taurus 4°</note>. When the Sun is at this position, Agastya sets. When one wishes to know how many days from the current day Agastya will rise, the difference in minutes between the current Sun and the required Sun for rising should be divided by the Sun's daily motion. The resulting days indicate when Agastya’s rising is "yet to come" if the rising-Sun is greater, or "already passed" if it is less. The same logic applies to the setting Sun.

### Now, the Calculation of the Rising and Setting of the Moon—

In the Śaka year 1571, on the 10th day of the bright fortnight of the month of Phālguna, a Thursday <note>approx. February 11, 1650 CE</note>, to observe the Moon’s rising, the elapsed years are 412, and the **day-count** <note>original: "ahargaṇa"; the total days since the beginning of the epoch</note> is 150,843. At the time of sunset at the local position, the planets are as follows: Sun 10|21|2|11; Moon 11|6|19|43; Moon's Apogee 1|3|42|36; Node 0|2|55|38. The **precession of the equinoxes** <note>original: "ayanāṃśas"</note> is 17|52|55. The true Sun is 10|22|58|53 with a daily motion of 60|8. The Moon is 11|19|26|17 with a daily motion of 735|4. The **ascensional difference** <note>original: "cara"</note> is -35. The Sun adjusted by the ascensional difference is 10|22|58|18; the Moon is 11|19|18|59; the Node is 0|2|55|33. The **celestial latitude** <note>original: "śara"</note> in digits etc. is South 28;52. Now, because this is a rising in the west <note>after the new moon</note>, the Moon is increased by three signs: 2|19|18|59. Its declination is North 23|50|17. The local latitude is South 24|35|9. The zenith distance is South 0|42|52. The result of the **visibility correction** <note>original: "dṛkkarma"</note> is -1|7. The Moon adjusted by this visibility correction is 11|19|17|52. Since it is said that the calculation follows the direction of the latitude and zenith distance, here the result came out positive, but because it is in the west, it must be applied as a negative correction.

<column-break/>

# 87
## Chapter on Rising and Setting—6

Now, for calculating the desired **time-degrees**: Having established the Moon adjusted by the visibility correction and the precession <note>tropical position</note>, increased by six signs, it is 5|27|10|47. The Sun, also adjusted by precession and increased by six signs, is 5|17|51|13. Between these two, the smaller value is considered the Sun and the larger is the **ascendant** <note>original: "lagna"</note>. Their difference, as per the calculation for the Sun and ascendant, results in 3;1 **ghatīs** <note>1 ghatī = 24 minutes</note>. Multiplied by six, this is 18;6. These are the desired time-degrees. Since these are greater than the prescribed time-degrees <note>for the Moon</note>, the rising has already passed. The difference between the desired and prescribed time-degrees in minutes is 266. This is multiplied by three hundred <note>original: "khābhra-agni"; 0-0-3</note> which is 109,800. Since the rising is in the west, this is divided by the rising time of the seventh sign from the tropical Sun (the sign of Virgo), which is 333. The result is 329;43. This is divided by the difference between the Sun’s and Moon’s daily motion, which is 675;32. The result in days is 0;29;17. The Moon's rising had passed by this many days.

### Now, the Calculation of the Moon's Setting.

In the Śaka year 1533, on the 30th day of the secular month of Jyeṣṭha, a Thursday <note>approx. June 10, 1611 CE</note>, for the calculation of the Moon's setting in the east: the elapsed years are 418, the intercalary months are 155, and the day-count is 152,762. The mean planets at dawn for the local position are: Sun 1|20|55|40; Moon 11|11|59|18; Apogee 7|7|14|28; Node 3|13|33|32. The precession is 17|58|10. The true Sun is 1|21|54|59; the Moon is 11|12|12|46. The ascensional difference is -104. The Sun adjusted by this is 1|21|53|17; the Moon is 11|21|4|58; the Node is 3|13|33|27. The celestial latitude in digits is North 51;49. Since it is located in the east, the Moon is decreased by three signs: 10|12|14|58. The declination is 12;53|10. The zenith distance is South 12;33|16. The altitude is 77;26|44. The sine of the zenith distance is 26;6; the sine of the altitude is 116;43. The visibility correction in minutes is -34;45. The Moon adjusted by this is 11|10|30|13. As before, the desired time-degrees are 11;18. Since it is in the east, it should be adjusted by six signs. Since it is less than the prescribed 12 degrees, the setting has already occurred <note>following the rule: "if it is less than the prescribed degrees, it is set"</note>. The difference between the desired and prescribed time-degrees in minutes is 42. Multiplied by three hundred (300), it is 12,600. Divided by the rising time of the tropical Sun's sign (Gemini), which is 305, the area-minutes are 41;18. Dividing by the difference in motion, 801;58, the days are 0;3;5. Thus, the setting has passed. On the 14th day of the dark fortnight, at the desired time in the remaining part of the night, 3;5, the Moon sets. The setting of the Moon occurs due to its proximity to the Sun; this should be calculated for the 14th day of the dark fortnight in the east. The rising of the Moon in the west occurs due to proximity to the Sun near the 2nd day of the bright fortnight. 

Now, the knowledge of the daily rising and setting of the planets is explained: The daily rising in the east for all planets must be calculated, and the daily setting in the west must be calculated. The planet seen in the east is the rising sign of the planet; the planet seen in the west is the setting sign. As stated by Bhāskarācārya:

It includes specific astronomical data from 1611 and 1650 CE to demonstrate how to determine if a celestial event has already occurred or is yet to happen.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific mathematical demonstrations for the visibility of celestial bodies. Having discussed the theory and the star Agastya (Canopus) previously, it now provides a worked example for the Moon's visibility on a specific date in 1595 CE, before concluding the sixth chapter of the manual.</meta>

# 88
## In the Karaṇakutūhala

> "The visible planet in the east is the ascendant known as the 'rising sign'; that same planet, when the Sun is subtracted at the time of setting, is the 'western visible planet.'"
> <note>original: "prāgdṛggrahaḥ syādudayākhyalagnamastāravyarkaṃ paścimadṛggrahaḥ saḥ"</note>
> (Siddhānta-śiromaṇi, Praśnottara, 1)

As an illustration, for the purpose of observing the moonrise on Saturday, the 4th day of the dark half of the month of Magha in the Shaka year 1517 <note>January/February 1595 CE</note>, the elapsed years are 412, and the total days elapsed <term>ahargaṇa|The number of days passed since the beginning of an astronomical epoch</term> are 150,831. The mean positions of the planets at sunset at this location are: the Sun 10|9|12|36 <note>10 signs, 9 degrees, 12 minutes, 36 seconds</note>; the Moon 5|28|12|45; the Moon’s apogee 1|2|22|15; the Node 0|12|7|26; the precession of the equinoxes 17|52|52. The local ascensional difference <note>original: "carapala"</note> is 57.

The corrected Sun is 10|17|53|56 with a daily motion of 60|28. The Moon is 5|26|50|54 with a daily motion of 855|35. The Node is 0|12|7|23 with a motion of 3|11. The celestial latitude of the Moon <note>original: "śara"</note> is 2|55 North. For the observation of the eastern rising, the Moon minus three signs is 2|26|5|24. Its declination is North 23|8|55. The latitude of the place is South 24|35|9. The zenith distance is South 12|6|14.

As previously described, the visibility correction <note>original: "dṛkphala"</note> is negative: 8|13. The Moon, corrected by this visibility calculation, is 5|26|5|41. This is the **eastern visible planet**. This is the rising sign of the Moon; when this specific sign is on the horizon, the Moon rises. It is also said—

> "When their respective rising signs emerge, the celestial bodies always appear in the sky. When the setting signs emerge, they set every day due to the rotation of the cosmic wind."
> (Siddhānta-śiromaṇi, Grahachāyādhikāra, 9)

This is how the time in <term>nāḍikās|A unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term> passed since sunrise is calculated. Now, at the time of setting, the desired ascendant is 4|10|53|56. When six signs are added to the position of the true Sun, that becomes the desired ascendant at the time of sunset. The rising sign is 5|26|50|41. The time interval between these two—calculated by the method of "the time between the rising sign and the desired sign"—is 8 hours and 30 minutes <note>original: "8|30 ghaṭī"</note>. At this time after sunset, the moonrise will occur in the east.

As it is said: "If the eastern visible planet is less than the desired ascendant, the rising has passed; if it is more, the rising is yet to come. Similarly, for the western visible planet, one should know if the setting has passed or is yet to occur based on whether it is less or more. The time in *ghatikas* resulting from their difference determines the past or future event, and when shifted by that, the planet’s own rising or setting is found." (Siddhānta-śiromaṇi, Praśnottara, 1-2). In this way, the rising and setting of all planets should be calculated daily. ॥ 5 ॥

### Sudhākara:—
The meaning is clear. Here is the mathematical proof: The mean declination of Agastya (Canopus) = 24. Because the difference is small, the true declination = 52 South. From these, using a simplified measure of the ascensional difference, (24 × 8 × equinoctial shadow) / (9 × 10) and (52 × 8 × equinoctial shadow) / (9 × 10). For the two half-chords of the true and mean declination—

<column-break/>

<header>Chapter on Rising and Setting—6</header>
# 89

—and so forth, the degrees of visibility correction due to latitude are equal to the true ascensional difference: (77 × 8 × equinoctial shadow) / 90 = 8 times the equinoctial shadow (with a small margin of error). Now, the polar longitudes of Agastya = 87; the time-degrees required for visibility = 12. Therefore, the degrees of the ecliptic for rising and setting are assumed to be 11 and 9 respectively. Thus the proof is established; the rest is clear.

### Conclusion:—

> Thus, in this "Curiosity of the Planets" <note>original: "Grahāgame Kutūhale"</note> spoken by Bhāskara,
> Which is dear to the intellects of the learned, the means of calculating the rising and setting of planets is complete. ॥ 6 ॥

### Sumatiharṣa:—

Thus, in the commentary on the *Karaṇakutūhala* titled *Gaṇakakumudakaumudī* <note>The Moonlight for the Lily of Astronomers</note>, the investigation into the rising and setting of planets has been performed. This concludes the Sixth Chapter: On Rising and Setting. ॥ 6 ॥

### Sudhākara:—

> In the *Vāsanāvibhūṣaṇa* <note>The Ornament of Exposition</note> created by the son of the illustrious Kṛpālu,
> The excellent exposition of the mathematics of rising and setting, accompanied by good words and logic, is here completed.

Thus, in the *Karaṇakutūhala*, in the *Vāsanāvibhūṣaṇa* commentary, the chapter on the rising and setting of celestial bodies is finished. ॥ 6 ॥

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the Moon's visibility, moving into Chapter 7, which focuses on calculating the specific appearance and orientation of the Moon's crescent, known as the "elevation of the horns."</meta>

# Chapter 7: The Elevation of the Moon's Horns

## Calculating the Deflection—

> The declination minutes of the Moon, corrected by its celestial latitude, and the declination of the Sun increased by six signs; these, multiplied by the sine of the difference between the Moon and Sun and the local latitude, then divided by one hundred and twenty, provide the corrected deflection. ॥ 1 ॥
> The declination of the Moon, after all corrections, divided by five times the longitude of the Moon-minus-Sun, results in the digits of deflection.

**Sumatiharṣa—** Here, the north-south distance between the Moon and the Sun is the <term>base|bhuja</term>, and the east-west distance is the <term>upright|koṭi</term>. The distance between them forms the hypotenuse, creating a triangle that serves as the basis for calculating the elevation of the horns. This applies to the Moon at sunrise during the <term>dark fortnight|kṛṣṇapakṣa</term> or at sunset during the <term>bright fortnight|śuklapakṣa</term>. It is said:

> "The elevation of the Moon’s horns should be understood on the day of the first or last quarter of the month. It should be calculated at sunrise, sunset, or during the night." 
> <note>original: "māsāntapāde prathame'thavendoḥ śṛṅgonnatiryādivase'vagamya..."</note>
> (Siddhānta-śiromaṇi, Śṛṅgonnatyadhikāra, 3)

The Moon's declination in minutes is corrected by its <term>celestial latitude|śara</term>—subtracted if the directions are different and added if they are the same. This is then compared with the declination of the Sun (with six signs added to it). Why are six signs added? To ensure that the calculation correctly accounts for the north-south distance between the two bodies regardless of their positions in the zodiac. 

Next, the <term>sine of the longitude|bhujajyā</term> of the Moon-minus-Sun is multiplied by the local latitude and divided by 120. This gives a correction based on the observer's location. Finally, the total corrected declination is divided by five times the Moon's longitude relative to the Sun to find the <term>deflection|valana</term> in <term>digits|aṅgula</term>. The direction of the deflection follows the direction of the declination.

**Example Calculation:**
In the Shaka year 1517, on Thursday, the 1st day of the bright half of the month of Phalguna <note>February 1595 CE</note>, we observe the elevation of the horns. At sunset:
*   **True Moon:** 11 signs, 9°, 18', 59"
*   **Moon with Precession:** 11 signs, 27°, 11', 54"
*   **Base (Bhuja):** 0 signs, 2°, 48', 6"
*   **Declination (South):** 67' 36"
*   **Celestial Latitude (South):** 86' 16"
*   **Combined (Same direction, so added):** 153' 52"
*   **Sun (plus 6 signs) Declination (North):** 45' 6" 15"
*   **Difference (Different directions, so subtracted):** 30' 12" 3" (North)
*   **Moon minus Sun:** 0 signs, 16°, 20', 41"
*   **Sine of the difference:** 33' 41"
*   **Local Latitude (South):** 24° 35' 9"
*   **Multiplied and divided by 120:** 6' 54" 4" (South)
*   **Final Corrected Declination:** 1' 51" 41" (South)

The deflection is then calculated by dividing this final declination by the Moon-Sun difference (multiplied by 5). In this case, the resulting deflection is 0.47 digits. Since the final declination was South, the deflection is also South. ॥ 1 ॥

## Describing the White and Black Portions—

> Divide the longitude of the Moon-minus-Sun by fifteen; this is the divisor. Divide thirty-six by this divisor. ॥ 2 ॥
> Place that result in two positions: subtract the divisor from one and add it to the other. Half of these results will be the "dark" and "bright" portions respectively.

**Sumatiharṣa—** Take the longitude of the Moon minus the Sun and divide it by 15 to get the "divisor." Divide 36 by this divisor. Take this result and, in one instance, subtract the divisor; in the other, add the divisor. Half of the first value is the <term>dark portion|vibhā</term> and half of the second is the <term>bright portion|svabhā</term> (the illuminated part of the Moon).

**Example:**
*   **Moon-minus-Sun Longitude:** 7 signs, 3°, 29', 19"
*   **Divided by 15:** 4.54 (This is the divisor)
*   **36 divided by 4.54:** 7.20
*   **Position 1 (7.20 - 4.54):** 2.26. Half of this is 1.13 (**Dark Portion**)
*   **Position 2 (7.20 + 4.54):** 12.14. Half of this is 6.07 (**Bright Portion**) ॥ 2 ॥

## Description of the Diagram—

> Drawing a circle with a radius of six digits using a cord, mark the directions and the deflection on the circle. ॥ 3 ॥
> In the bright fortnight, mark the dark portion from the center toward the east; in the dark fortnight, toward the west. From that point, draw another circle using the bright portion as the radius to see the shape of the Moon’s phase. ॥ 4 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa—** On a level piece of ground, paper, or a board, draw a circle representing the Moon’s disk using a compass or cord set to six digits. Mark the East, West, North, and South directions. Apply the previously calculated deflection in its proper direction. In the <term>bright fortnight|śuklapakṣa</term>, mark it on the eastern side; in the <term>dark fortnight|kṛṣṇapakṣa</term>...

---

<summary>This page explains how to calculate the deflection (valana) and the illuminated versus dark portions of the Moon to visually represent its phase. It provides a specific mathematical example from February 1595 CE.</summary>
<keywords>elevation of the horns, deflection, lunar phases, declination, Sumatiharṣa, Shaka 1517, astronomical diagrams, bright fortnight, dark fortnight</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Chapter 7 of the Karaṇakutūhala, moving from the basic definitions of deflection to the practical geometry of drawing the Moon's crescent and providing detailed worked examples for specific dates in 1617 CE.</meta>

# Chapter 7: The Elevation of the Moon’s Horns (Continued)

In the western part of the diagram, where the tip of the **deflection** <note>original: "valanāgra"</note> is located, a string should be stretched toward the center. On that string, the "shadow-segment" <term>vibhā|the portion of the lunar diameter that is illuminated or dark</term> should be marked. From the center toward the tip of the deflection, the measure of the *vibhā* is laid out. Sometimes, after drawing the lunar disk, the line for the *vibhā* may fall far outside; in that case, a circle should be drawn with a compass using the measure of the "self-shadow" <note>original: "svabhā"</note> from the tip of the *vibhā*. It should be understood that the portion of the Moon's disk outside this circle is the visible crescent. The "horn" <note>the tip of the crescent</note> situated in the direction of the deflection is considered the "lower" horn, while the one in the opposite direction is the "elevated" horn. 

Now for a specific detail: when the *vibhā* exceeds six digits, a mark should be made on the ground or a drawing board at the distance of the *vibhā* from the center, following the deflection line. Then, a circle is drawn based on that shadow measure. This usually does not happen on the eighth day of the bright fortnight. On the New Moon <note>Amāvasyā</note>, because the Sun and Moon are in the same position, there is no *vibhā*; the shadow measure is six digits. On the eighth day, where the upright <note>koṭi</note> is absent, the *vibhā* and *svabhā* are both eighteen digits; in such cases, the deflection is generally considered southern. In the city of Shivapuri, the maximum deflection is nearly four digits. As the latitude increases, the deflection increases accordingly. This occurs in the south; northern deflection, however, stays within the range of a single digit.

### Example 1: Jodhpur (Bright Fortnight)
For the Shaka year 1539, month of Ashvina, bright fortnight, 6th day, Friday:
**Day-count** <term>ahargaṇa|total days elapsed since the start of the epoch</term>: 158,733.

| Planet/Parameter | Value (Signs.Degrees.Minutes.Seconds) |
| :--- | :--- |
| Mean Sun (Evening) | 5s 27° 26' 32" |
| Mean Moon | 8s 17° 44' 34" |
| Moon's Apogee | 5s 22° 20' 28" |
| Ascending Node | 2s 0° 6' 46" |
| Precession <note>ayanāṃśa</note> | 18° 14' 31" |

**Calculations:**
The Sun's equation of center is negative (2° 8' 5"). The Moon's equation of center is negative (5° 10' 9"). The ascensional difference <note>cara-phala</note> is 26 positive, but 26 negative when reversed. The Sun, corrected for latitude, is 5s 25° 17' 17" with a daily motion of 59' 21". The Moon is 8s 12° 31' 0" with a daily motion of 774' 5".
The Moon's southern declination is 1437' 11". In Jodhpur, the equinoctial shadow is 5;5. The Moon's southern celestial latitude <note>śara</note> is 197' 57". The declination corrected by latitude is 1635' 8" South. Now, the declination of the Sun plus six signs is 316' 31". Because the directions are different, the difference is 1308' 37", or 21° 48' 37". The Moon-minus-Sun <note>vyarkendu</note> is 2s 17° 16' 43", and its sine is 116' 21". Multiplying by Jodhpur's latitude (25° 58' 43") and dividing by 120, we get 25;15,13. This southern value, combined with the previous 12° 48' 37", gives 38;3,50. The Moon-minus-Sun segment is 2s 17° 16' 43". Multiplying by five and converting to arc-seconds gives 44,783. Dividing the total corrected declination (169,427 seconds) by this, the resulting **deflection** <note>valana</note> is 3 digits, 46 minutes, 59 seconds South.

<column-break/>

The Moon-minus-Sun is 2s 17° 16' 43". Its "upright" <note>koṭi</note> is 0s 21° 43' 17". If this segment represents a full sign <note>30 degrees</note>, then it should be converted to degrees and divided by fifteen. Here, the sign-value is zero and the degrees are 12;43,17. Dividing by fifteen, we get a divisor of 0;50,53. Using the total arc-seconds (3,053) and dividing the total degrees (129,600), we obtain 42;27.
This value is placed in two positions: 42;27.
1. Subtracting the divisor (0;50,53) gives 41;36,7.
2. Adding it gives 43;17,53.
Dividing these results in the two shadow-segments:
**Vibhā** <note>illuminated portion</note>: 20 digits, 48 minutes, 33 seconds.
**Svabhā** <note>dark portion</note>: 21 digits, 36 minutes, 53 seconds.

### Example 2: Dark Fortnight
For the Shaka year 1539, month of Kartika, dark fortnight, 13th day, Friday:
For the purpose of calculating the elevation of the horns at sunrise, the **day-count** is 158,754.

| Planet/Parameter | Value |
| :--- | :--- |
| Mean Sun (Morning) | 6s 18° 3' 33" |
| Mean Moon | 5s 17° 44' 53" |
| Moon's Apogee | 5s 24° 37' 25" |
| Ascending Node | 2s 19° 12' 0" |
| Precession | 18° 14' 34" |

The ascensional difference is 66. The corrected Sun is 6s 17° 20' 36" (motion 61' 28"). The Moon is 5s 18° 34' 30" (motion 722' 20"). The Moon's declination is 64' 34" South. The celestial latitude is 24' 50" South. Since they are in the same direction, the sum is 369' 24". The declination of the Sun plus six signs is 814' 21" North. The difference between these opposite directions is 444' 57" North. The sine of the Moon-minus-Sun is 57' 39". Multiplying by the local latitude (24° 35' 9") and dividing by 120, we get 11;48,41. Subtracting the previous 7;24,57 due to different directions gives 4;35,44 South. Using the "upright" and "base" of the Moon-minus-Sun, we find:
**Deflection** <note>valana</note>: 0 digits 59 minutes South.
**Divisor**: 3;50.
**Vibhā**: 2 digits 45 minutes.
**Svabhā**: 6 digits 36 minutes.
This concludes the calculation of the elevation of the horns as prescribed in the *Karaṇakutūhala*.

**Sudhākara—** The meaning is clear.

**Geometric Proof:**
The elevation of the horns is calculated at the Sun's setting or rising. The Moon's gnomon-shadow <note>śaṅku</note> is assumed to be equal to the Moon-minus-Sun. Since there is generally no discrepancy in the elevation of the horns when using the corrected declinations and the measure of the horizon-intercept <note>agrāṃśa</note>, the corrected base <note>bhuja</note> is defined as:
*Base = 2 × (Moon’s corrected declination ± Sun’s declination ± Moon’s gnomon shadow)*
This simplifies to:
$2 \times \text{Moon's Corr. Decl.} \pm 2 \times \text{Sun's Decl.} \pm \frac{2 \times \text{Sine of Lat. } \times \text{Latitude}}{120}$

<summary>This page provides the geometric instructions and mathematical examples for calculating the Moon's appearance (elevation of the horns) for two specific dates in 1617 CE at Jodhpur, including values for deflection and shadow segments.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Chapter 7, which deals with the geometry of the Moon's appearance (the elevation of its horns), and begins Chapter 8, focusing on the planetary diameters and their conjunctions. It transitions from calculating the lunar crescent to the mathematical sizing of the five visible planets—Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn.</meta>

# In the Joy of Calculation (Karanakutūhala)

This sine-based base <note>original: "jyamātmakobhujo"</note> is divided by two to get degrees. 
**Apparent Declination ± Solar Declination / 120 = Sine of the degrees.**

Here, if the directions are different, take the difference; if the directions are the same, take the sum. For this purpose, the declination is brought from the Sun increased by six signs <note>this refers to calculating the opposite point on the ecliptic</note>. To obtain the result for a circle of six digits <term>angula|the standard unit of measure in Indian astronomy, roughly the width of a finger</term>, the proportion is used: if this is the value for the full sine of the Moon minus the Sun, then what is it for six digits? The form is: (base × 6) / (2 × sine of degrees) = (base × 6) / (Radius × 30) = base / (Radius / 5).

The reasoning for the remaining procedures is easily understood through the standard methods of the astronomical treatises <note>original: "siddhāntavidhinā"</note>.

**Conclusion:**
->Thus, in this "Joy of Calculation," the planetary manual composed by Bhāskara,<-
->highly valued by the wise, concludes the determination of the elevation of the horns. [7]<-

**Sumatiharsha:**
Thus ends the seventh chapter, titled "The Elevation of the Horns," in the *Brahmatulya* commentary. [7]

**Sudhākara:** The excellent explanation of the mathematics of the elevated horns, accompanied by good sayings and logic, has been composed here by Sudhākara, the son of the illustrious Kripālu, as an ornament for this manual.

Thus ends the section on the elevation of the Moon’s horns in the *Vāsanāvaravibhūshana* <note>meaning "The Best Ornament of Explanation"</note> commentary on the *Karanakutūhala*. [7]

This concludes the seventh chapter on the Elevation of the Horns, featuring the two commentaries by Sumatiharsha and Sudhākara Dvivedi, edited by Satyendra Mishra.

<column-break/>

<header>Chapter 8: The Conjunction of Planets</header>

### Calculation of the Angular Diameters of Mars and the Others in Minutes—

> The values five, eight, seven, nine, and five are placed separately. They are multiplied by the difference between the Radius and the current distance <term>shīghrakarna|the distance from the Earth to the planet calculated via the "fast" epicycle</term>. Then, they are divided by three times the specific divisors <note>original: "parākhya"</note>. The results are added to or subtracted from the mean values placed separately. When the distance is more or less than the Radius <note>120 minutes in this system</note>, and then divided by three, the result is the angular diameter in digits for Mars and the others. [1]

**Sumatiharsha:** First, the diameters are written according to their linear size in *yojanas* <term>yojana|a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8-10 miles</term>:
Mars is 1,885; Mercury is slightly more than 279; Jupiter is 16,649; Venus is 1,110; and Saturn is 2,955. A planet that is far from the Earth appears small, while one that is near the Earth appears large. As it is said in the *Siddhānta Shiromani* (Chapter on Planetary Conjunctions, 7):

> "A celestial body situated high up is very far, and one situated low is near the Earth. Therefore, the disk appears small when far and large when near, just as it does for the Sun in its far and near orbits."

The mean angular diameters are: 5 minutes <note>original: "kalā"</note> for Mars, 6 for Mercury, 7 for Jupiter, 9 for Venus, and 5 for Saturn. These five values are placed separately and multiplied by the difference between the Radius <note>120</note> and the planet’s distance. This is divided by the planet’s own "divisor" <note>parākhya</note> multiplied by three. The result should be subtracted from the mean diameter if the distance is greater than 120, and added if it is less. These are the true angular diameters of the planets in minutes. When divided by 3, the result is the diameter in digits. 

**Example for Jupiter:** The mean diameter is 7 minutes. The difference between the Radius and the distance is 1,519. Multiplying 7 by 1,519 gives 10,613. Dividing this by Jupiter's divisor (23) multiplied by 3 (which is 69) gives 153.9 <note>this appears to be a specific worked example from the commentator's time</note>. Since the distance was greater than the Radius, this value is subtracted from the mean, resulting in a true diameter of 5 minutes and 29 seconds. Dividing by 3, we get the diameter in digits as 1 digit and 49 minutes of a digit. 

**Example for Venus:** The mean diameter is 9 minutes. The Radius is 120 and the distance is 82;33. Their difference is 37;37. Multiplying 9 by 37;37 gives 275;33. Dividing this by the divisor (87) multiplied by 3 (which is 261) gives 1;3. Since the distance (82;33) is less than the Radius (120), this is added to the mean of 9, giving 10;3. Divided by 3, the true diameter of Venus in digits is 3 digits and 21 minutes. The diameters for all planets should be calculated in this way. [1]

<summary>This page concludes the chapter on calculating the Moon's crescent and begins Chapter 8, which provides the mathematical formulas and worked examples for calculating the apparent size (angular diameter) of the five planets based on their distance from Earth.</summary>

<header>In the Joy of Calculation</header>

# Knowledge of the Time of Conjunction—

Divide the difference in minutes of arc between two sky-dwellers <note>original: "divaukas", referring to planets</note> by the difference of their daily motions. [2]

If one planet is retrograde <term>vakri|planets that appear to move backward in the sky from the perspective of Earth</term>, divide by the sum of their motions. The result is the time in days. If the planet with the slower motion is behind <note>i.e., has a lower longitude</note>, the conjunction has passed; otherwise, it is yet to come. If both planets are retrograde, the rule is reversed. [3]

**Sumatiharsha:** When one wishes to know the conjunction of two planets, first calculate their true positions for the desired day. In this commentary, it is said that one should first perform the visibility correction <note>original: "ayanadrukkarma"</note> and then calculate the conjunction. This is appropriate, as it is stated: "Having performed the visibility correction, one should again calculate the conjunction for that specific time." However, the author of this text has combined both operations. If the conjunction is calculated using only the longitudes, it may differ; therefore, it is proper to calculate it only after the visibility correction. If the conjunction is calculated without this correction, the result is still stable: divide the minutes of their difference by the difference in their daily motions <term>bhukti|the daily distance traveled by a planet</term> to get the days and smaller units. If one is retrograde, divide by the sum of their motions. If the slower planet is less than the faster one, or if the retrograde planet is less, the conjunction is "past" <note>it has already happened</note>. Otherwise, if the slower or retrograde planet is greater, the conjunction is "future" <note>it is yet to happen</note>. If both are retrograde, the logic is inverted: divide the difference by the difference of motions; if the slower one is less, the conjunction is future; if the slower one is greater, it is past.

For example: In the year 1541 of the Shaka era <note>approximately 1619 CE</note>, on the 14th day of the dark half of the lunar month of Vaishakha, on a Sunday at sunrise, the day-count <term>ahargana|total days elapsed since the start of the epoch</term> was 159,288.
**Jupiter's data:** Center of anomaly 1s 3° 3' 5"; Correction for anomaly 7° 42' 31"; True Jupiter 11s 16° 39' 7"; Motion 11' 31".
**Venus's data:** Correction for mean motion 1° 31' 9" (positive); Apex of fast motion 8s 20° 49' 52"; Fast hypotenuse 89;23; True Venus 11s 16° 51' 26"; Motion 60' 57".
**Solar data:** True Sun 1s 3° 6' 12"; Precession 18° 16' 10"; Ascensional difference 86.
Jupiter is at 11s 16° 39' 7"; Venus is at 11s 16° 51' 26". Their difference is 0s 0° 12' 21" 19". Converting this to seconds <note>original: "vikalāḥ"</note>, we get 739. The difference in their motions is 49' 26", which is 2,966 seconds. Dividing 739 by 2,966, the result in days and smaller units is 0;14,56 <note>about 6 hours</note>. Since Jupiter, the slower planet, is less than Venus, the faster planet, the conjunction is known to be "past." [3]

# Determination of the Conjunction—

->By these calculated days of conjunction, the two planets are made equal.<-
->The "arrows" <note>celestial latitudes</note> must be calculated, and for the Moon, it must be corrected by its parallax.<-

<column-break/>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Planetary Conjunctions—8</header>

If the latitudes are in different directions, take the sum of the two planetary latitudes; if in the same direction, take the difference. This north-south distance between the two celestial bodies, when divided by twenty-four, gives the distance in cubits <term>hasta|a unit of measure, approximately 18-20 inches</term>. [4]

The two planets are known by the directions of their own latitudes. If they are in the same direction, the one with the smaller latitude is subtracted from the other to find the clear latitude <note>original: "sphuṭeṣu"</note>. If the distance between the two bodies is less than half the sum of their diameters <note>original: "mānaikyārdha"</note>, there is an occultation or overlap. All procedures such as parallax <term>lambana|the difference in the apparent position of a celestial object viewed along two different lines of sight</term> should be performed as in a solar eclipse. [5]

Even if the Sun is slow or the Moon is fast or retrograde, the Moon is considered to be joined with the Sun, and that is the direction of the latitude. If the planet is less than the Ascendant <term>lagna|the point of the ecliptic rising on the eastern horizon</term> at night, or greater than the Ascendant plus six signs, the conjunction is visible. One must calculate the time the planet has been in the sky based on its own daily motion. [6]

**Sumatiharsha:** Thus, using the previously mentioned days of conjunction, if the conjunction is past, subtract the motion; if future, add it. For a retrograde planet, do the opposite. If both are retrograde, also do the opposite: for a past conjunction add, and for a future one subtract. Once this is done, the two planets will be equal in signs, degrees, etc. Then, calculate their latitudes <note>original: "śara", literally 'arrow'</note> as before. Among these, for the Moon, calculate the parallax <note>original: "nati"</note> and adjust the Moon's latitude. If the latitudes of the two planets are in the same direction, take the difference; if in different directions, take the sum. This gives the north-south interval between the planets. That interval divided by 24 gives the result in cubits. 

For example, using the previous time 0;14,56, the adjusted equal positions for Jupiter and Venus are both 11s 16° 36' 15". Using these positions, calculate the latitudes again. Jupiter’s node is 9s 8° 0' 0". Corrected by the anomaly 7° 42' 31" (subtracted because it is positive), Jupiter's true node is 9s 0° 17' 32". Combined with Jupiter's position, we get 8s 16° 53' 44". Its sine is 116;26. Multiplying by the maximum latitude 76 and dividing by the hypotenuse 135, we get 65;26. Divided by 3, the latitude in digits <term>angula|finger-breadth</term> is 21;48 in the southern hemisphere. For Venus, the node is 10s 0° 0' 0", corrected by the mean anomaly 1° 31' 11" to get 10s 1° 31' 11". Combined with the fast apex 8s 20° 49' 52", we get 6s 22° 21' 3". The sine is 45;28. Multiplying by the maximum latitude 136 and dividing by the hypotenuse 89;23, we get 69;10. Divided by 3, the latitude is 23;3 digits...

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the calculation of planetary conjunctions. It focuses on determining the relative positions (north or south) of planets and introduces the method of applying "parallax" (lambana) and "latitude deflection" (nati) to these conjunctions, treating the interaction between two planets similarly to a solar eclipse.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>In the Joy of Calculation</header>

...southern. Now, another characteristic should be known: the planet that possesses a southern latitude <term>shara|the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</term> is to be understood as "situated to the south." The one with a northern latitude is "situated to the north." If both latitudes are in the same direction, the one with the smaller latitude is considered to be in the opposite direction relative to the one with the larger latitude <note>i.e., if both are North, the one with the smaller value is 'south' of the other</note>. Now, the difference between the two situated in the north or south is known as the "true arrow" <note>original: "spashta-ishu" or "spashta-bana," technical terms for the corrected celestial latitude</note>. This true latitude represents the visual conjunction <note>original: "drishya yuti"</note> of the two bodies. From this, the parallax and other corrections must be calculated. As it has been said:

> "If those two planets are fit to be seen at the time of conjunction, then this procedure must be performed at that very time."
(Siddhanta Shiromani, Chapter on Planetary Conjunctions, verse 8). The specific characteristics of this will be explained further on.

### The Concept of Sun and Moon in Conjunctions

Now, for the purpose of calculating parallax <term>lambana|the shift in an object's apparent position when viewed from different points, specifically the difference between the geocentric and topocentric positions</term>, we must designate one planet as the Sun and the other as the Moon. Between two planets moving along their paths, the one with slow motion <note>original: "manda-gati"</note> is to be designated as the "Sun." Alternatively, if one is retrograde <term>vakri|appearing to move backward</term>, then the one moving quickly—whether naturally fast or slow—is designated as the "Moon." If both are retrograde, the rule is reversed: the faster one is designated as the "Sun" and the other as the "Moon." Having established this, one must determine in which direction (north or south) the "imaginary Moon" is situated relative to the "imaginary Sun"; this provides the direction of the latitude. The "Sun" is the body being covered, and the "Moon" is the coverer.

### The Method for Calculating Parallax

Now, for the means of achieving the parallax: take the calculated time of conjunction and treat it as the end of a lunar day <note>original: "darshanta", the moment of a New Moon</note>. From those desired hours, calculate the ascendant <term>lagna|the point of the ecliptic rising on the eastern horizon</term> using the Sun's position increased by six signs <note>original: "sashadabha-surya"</note>. Why is the Sun increased by six signs? Because the conjunction of planets is usually visible only at night; therefore, the position is flipped by 180 degrees. Sometimes, due to the rotation of the celestial sphere, the conjunction is not visible at night because the planets have set, and they are certainly not visible during the day due to the Sun; these characteristics will be explained. Then, that ascendant must be adjusted for the precession of the equinoxes <note>original: "sayana"</note> and the nonagesimal <term>vitribha|the point on the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the ascendant</term> must be calculated. Then, having treated the planet as the "Sun" with equal minutes of arc using the previous method, one calculates the "middle parallax" and "true parallax" using the degrees between the imaginary Sun and the nonagesimal, applying the "seventy-seven" rule <note>original: "saptadraya," likely referring to a specific sine-table constant in the text's algorithm</note>. In the hours of the conjunction time, if the imaginary Sun is in the eastern half relative to the nonagesimal, the parallax is subtracted; if it is in the western half, it is added. One should know that the six signs ahead of the imaginary Sun are "greater" and the six signs behind it are "lesser." This explains the increase or decrease relative to the nonagesimal, as explained in the section on solar eclipses. Generally, the six signs ahead of a planet's current sign are greater, and the six behind are lesser. Thus, the time is corrected by parallax. Using that same corrected time, calculate the nonagesimal again and determine the "degrees of zenith distance" <note>original: "natamsha"</note>. Multiply those degrees by one-fifteenth of the mean motion of the faster planet and divide by the radius <note>original: "trijya"</note>. The result is the latitude deflection <term>nati|parallax in latitude</term> in minutes. If this is further divided by two and a half, it becomes the deflection in digits <note>original: "angula"</note>. The latitude of the Moon <note>the faster planet</note> should be corrected by this. As it is said:

<column-break/>

<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>Chapter 8: Conjunction of Planets</header>

> "The ecliptic secondary <note>original: "drukkshepa"</note>, the Moon’s own mean daily motion, multiplied by the fifteenth part [of the zenith distance] and divided by the radius; these are the deflections <note>original: "nati"</note> of the Sun and Moon."
— Siddhanta Shiromani, Section on Eclipses, verse 11.
---
**Example:** At the time of conjunction, the remaining hours of the night are 14 ghadi and 56 vighadi. For the sake of parallax, the Sun at this time is 9 signs, 2°, 51', 54". The desired time is 14;56. The ascendant for the precession is calculated from the "purified Sun" <note>the Sun adjusted for the observer's location</note>, but not the imaginary declination; the "purified Sun" is used for ease. The corrected ascendant is 9s 26° 2' 39". Its declination is 10° 20' 16" South. The zenith distance <note>natamsha</note> is 34° 55' 25", and the altitude <note>unnatamsha</note> is 55° 4' 35". The sine of altitude is 98.5. Jupiter <note>Guru</note> is the slower planet, so it is imagined as the Sun. The faster planet is Venus <note>Shukra</note>, acting as the Moon. The position of Jupiter at the time of conjunction is 0s 4° 52' 25". The nonagesimal is 6s 26° 21' 39". The difference between them is 5s 8° 30' 46". The base sine <note>bhuja</note> is 0s 21° 2' 9". By the "seventy-seven" rule, the middle parallax is 2;10 and the true parallax is 1;52. Because the planet is in the remaining signs, it is an eastern parallax. By the rule, the nonagesimal is also less, so it is subtracted. At the time of conjunction, 45 ghadi and 4 vighadi have passed since sunrise on Saturday. Subtracting the parallax gives 43;12 as the true time of conjunction. For the sake of the latitude deflection <note>nati</note>, the Sun at this time is 1s 3° 43' 12". The Sun with precession is 1s 21° 16' 17". The nonagesimal is 7s 11° 17' 39". The eastern zenith distance is 40° 22'. The mean motion of the imaginary Moon is 59' 8". Multiplied by one-fifteenth (3;0), it is 158;8. Divided by the radius, the result is 1;19. This deflection, divided by two and a half (2;30), gives the deflection in digits as 0;31 South. By this, the "Moon's" latitude, which was 2;31 South, is corrected to 2;3" 34'. For the two latitudes <note>the two planets</note>, the value is 2;3' 48'. Since they are in the same direction, the difference is 1;14 South. This is the true north-south "arrow" <note>visual distance</note>. Because they are in the same direction, Jupiter (the one with the smaller latitude) is to the north of Venus. The true arrow is less than the sum of their semi-diameters (2;35), so there is an "overlap conjunction" <term>bheda-yoga|a conjunction where the disks of the planets appear to touch or overlap</term>. However, due to the rotation of the heavens, it is night for Jupiter, so the conjunction is not visible at that time. At midnight, if the planet is less than the ascendant of the Sun's night, or less than the ascendant plus six signs, but the planet is much greater... then it is only visible on the planet's day, not the planet's night. Thus, the conjunction is not considered visible. Here, the planet is at 0s 4° 52' 25", which is greater than the ascendant (9s 26° 21' 39") but less than the ascendant plus six signs (3s 26° 21' 39"). Therefore, the conjunction is not visible, and no further work was done.

**Another Example:** In the Shaka year 1541, month of Phalguna, bright half, 13th day, Monday at sunrise. Elapsed years: 436. Total days <note>ahargana</note>: 1,596,625. Mean positions: Jupiter's fast-correction is 4° 39' 31" subtractive. Sun: 11s 16° 6' 28". Jupiter: 8s 0° 59' 58". Venus: 1s 6° 23' 40". Venus's slow-correction: 1° 29' 45" 40'. Hypotenuse <note>karna</note>: 181;10. Precession: 18° 16' 57". Ascensional difference <note>charapala</note>: 64. Length of day: 29;48. Length of night: 30;12. The Sun corrected for ascensional difference: 11s 18° 44' 19". Motion: 59;41. Jupiter: 0;11...

It includes a detailed mathematical example of a Jupiter-Venus conjunction, concluding with a check for visibility and the start of a new calculation for the year 1541.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of calculating planetary conjunctions using the methods typically reserved for solar eclipses. The previous page established that one planet must be treated as the "Sun" and the other as the "Moon" to apply the math of parallax (lambana) and latitude. Here, the text provides a worked example involving Jupiter and Venus in the month of Phalguna.</meta>

19 | 13 is the motion [of Jupiter]; 13 | 27 is the motion of **Venus**; 0 | 15 | 7 | 57 is the motion of both. 
As before, for the day of conjunction and so on: 0 | 38 | 14 has passed. By this, on the 12th day of the bright half of the month of **Phalguna** <note>February–March</note>, from sunrise on Sunday, the time of conjunction is 21 <term>ghatis|a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term> and 46 <term>palas|1/60th of a ghati, or 24 seconds</term>. At this time, the mean positions should be made "true" <note>corrected for the observer's specific time and location</note>. Because the difference is small, the motion is used to calculate the seconds and so on. The Sun at the time of conjunction is 11 | 7 | 36 | 18; **Jupiter** <note>original: "Guru"</note> is 0 | 11 | 0 | 58; **Venus** <note>original: "Shukra"</note> is 0 | 12 | 0 | 55.

As before, the latitudes <term>shara|the angular distance north or south of the ecliptic</term> are: Jupiter’s latitude in <term>angulas|literally "fingers," a traditional unit of arc-measurement</term> and so on is 2 | 0 | 56. Venus’s latitude in angulas is 11 | 34. The disk <note>diameter</note> of Jupiter is 1 | 38; the disk of Venus is 2 | 18. For the purpose of calculating parallax <term>lambana|the apparent displacement of a celestial object</term>, the "conjunction-time" <term>sayana|with the precession of the equinoxes added</term> point of the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the Sun <note>vilibha</note> is 1 | 12 | 56 | 41. As before, its degrees of altitude are 81 | 26 | 52, and the sine <note>jya</note> is 118 | 17. 

The assumed *sayana* Sun is 0 | 19 | 27 | 48. The difference between the two [the Sun and the nonagesimal] is 0 | 23 | 28. By the table segments, the mean parallax is 2 | 28, which is now "true" <note>corrected</note>. Because it is in the west and the nonagesimal is greater, 21 | 46 is added <note>positive/dhanam</note> to the conjunction time, resulting in 23 | 48 as the corrected time of conjunction. At this moment, the Sun's position is 5 | 25 | 56. The remainder of the day is 0 | 6. The nonagesimal of the rising sign <note>ascendant</note> is 2 | 23 | 36 | 55. The degrees of zenith distance are 5 | 37 | 37. The mean motion of the Moon <note>the planet acting as the Moon</note> is multiplied by the part of the lunar day, 3 | 57, multiplied by 22 | 13. Divided by the radius 120, the result is 0 | 12. Dividing by two and a half (2 | 30), the result is 0 | 4. 

The southern latitude of the Moon <note>Venus, in this example</note> is 11 | 34; when corrected, the southern latitude is 11 | 38. The difference between North and South is 9 | 18; this is the true "arrow" <note>corrected latitude</note>. Divided by 24, it gives 0 | 3 | 18 in <term>hastas|cubits</term>. Venus is to the North. The sum of the semi-diameters <term>manaikyardha|the distance at which two celestial disks just touch</term> is 1 | 58. Because the latitude is greater than this sum, there is no "occultation" <note>original: "bhedya-yoga"; a conjunction where one body overlaps the other</note>. 

However, for the sake of an example, let us assume a latitude in angulas of 11 | 0. Calculating as one would for a solar eclipse: the sum of the semi-diameters is 1 | 58. Subtracting the latitude leaves 0 | 58 as the "obscured" portion. Using the rule "from twice the latitude" and so forth, the half-duration <term>sthityardha|half the total time from first contact to last contact</term> is 5 | 56 ghatis. Applying this to the true conjunction time of 23 | 48, we find the contact and release times for the two bodies. The **first contact** <note>sparsha</note> occurs at 18 | 42 ghatis from sunrise. The **release** <note>moksha</note> occurs at 28 | 44 ghatis from sunrise.

---

Now, for the sake of clarity, the positions of the Sun and Jupiter at the time of **first contact** are: Sun 5 | 25 | 50 | 12; Jupiter 0 | 0 | 19 | 20. The nonagesimal of the ascendant is 0 | 23 | 13 | 33. The altitude degrees are 74 | 33 | 51; the sine is 115 | 16. Using the difference segments between the assumed nonagesimal of Jupiter and the Sun, the true parallax is 0 | 24. Because it is in the west and the planet is near noon, the corrected contact time is 19 | 6. The ascendant at that time is 3 | 28 | 56 | 40. 

Now, to find the "day-measure" <note>duration of daylight</note> of the planet, the *sayana* planet at the horizon is 9 | 19 | 27 | 48. Its declination is 22 | 19 | 51 South. The zenith distance is 46 | 55 | 0; the altitude is 43 | 5 | 0. The sine of the zenith distance is 87 | 22; the sine of altitude is 81 | 37. Multiplying the deflection and so on, the result is 67 | 13 minutes, which is added. Corrected by the "visibility calculation" <term>drik-karma</term>, the rising sign <note>ascendant</note> is 0 | 2 | 28 | 40. 
For the setting sign <note>descendant</note>, the *sayana* planet plus three signs is 3 | 19 | 27 | 48; the declination is North

<column-break/>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>Chapter 8: On the Conjunction of Planets</header>

22 | 19 | 51. As before, the result of the visibility correction is negative: 2 | 19. Corrected by visibility, it is 0 | 1 | 8 | 22. The seventh sign from the setting sign is 6 | 1 | 8 | 22. This is the setting sign, and the *sayana* rising sign is 0 | 20 | 35 | 1. The part of the setting sign yet to rise and the part of the rising sign already risen is 2 | 25. The sum of the intermediate rising times is 1577. The total of these is the "day-measure" for the planet Jupiter: 31 | 2. Subtracting from 60, the night-measure is 28 | 58. 

Alternatively, one can calculate the day-measure by treating the *sayana* planet as the Sun and using the ascensional difference segments <term>chara-khandaka</term>. For the *sayana* planet 0 | 19 | 47 | 48, using the segments 55 | 44 | 18, the northern ascensional difference in minutes is 35. This makes the day-measure 31 | 10. 

Now, to find the **contact deflection** <note>sparsha-nata</note>, we calculate the time passed in the day. The desired ascendant at contact is 3 | 28 | 56 | 40. The rising sign is 0 | 20 | 25. The time between them—the portion of the smaller yet to rise plus the portion of the larger already risen plus the intermediate rising times—gives 16 | 0 ghatis as the time passed in Jupiter's day. The midday point is 15 | 31. The difference between these is the deflection: 0 | 29. Because it is in the west, and using the calculation "multiplied by zero and nine" etc., the half-day of the planet is 15 | 31. The resulting deflection at release is 1 | 12 South. 

Because it is near noon, it is small. The planet at the time of contact is 0 | 19 | 27 | 13. The cosine <term>kotijya</term> is 113 | 6. The precession-related deflection <term>ayana-valana</term> is North 22 | 39. The sum of the semi-diameters is 1 | 58. The true deflection is North 0 | 43. 

Now for the values at the **time of release** <note>moksha</note>: 28 | 54. The Sun is 5 | 26 | 0 | 20. Jupiter is 0 | 19 | 29 | 22. The nonagesimal of the ascendant is 2 | 21 | 8 | 27. Its declination is 23 | 19 | 17. As before, the altitude sine is 119 | 46. As before, the true deflection is positive: 3 | 57. Because it must be calculated for the planet, the final true time of release is 32 | 51 ghatis from sunrise. The Sun at this time is 5 | 26 | 4 | 15. Jupiter is 0 | 19 | 30 | 14. The time passed in the night is 30 | 3. The corresponding ascendant is 6 | 12 | 36 | 45. The rising sign is 0 | 3 | 35 | 1. The difference in time is 29 | 46. The time passed in the planet's day, as before, shows a deflection of 14 | 45. In the west, using the half-day of 15 | 31, the deflection at release is South 24 | 19. The planet at release is 0 | 19 | 30 | 14. The cosine is 113 | 14. The precession-related deflection is North 22 | 38. The true deflection at release is South 0 | 30. 

In this way, the method for calculating a conjunction with occultation <note>bhedya-yoga</note> should be understood. 
**Note:** If the planet designated as the "Moon" has a slower motion or is **retrograde** <term>vakra</term>, then the **first contact** <note>sparsha</note> occurs on the **Eastern** side and the **release** <note>moksha</note> on the **Western** side. If it has faster motion or is **direct** <term>margi</term>, then the **first contact** is on the **Western** side and the **release** is on the **Eastern** side.

It concludes with a general rule on how a planet's speed and direction (direct vs. retrograde) determine the orientation of the conjunction's onset and end.</summary>

### Conclusion
Thus, in this *Kutuhala* <note>original: "Karana-kutuhala," the title of the work</note>, an astronomical system spoken by **Bhaskara**, is the calculation of the conjunctions arising from the planets, which is dear to the intellect of wise scholars. || 8 ||

**Sumatiharsha** — Thus ends the eighth chapter, the Chapter on Conjunctions, in the commentary *Moonlight for the Lily of the Calculator* <note>original: "Ganaka-kumuda-kaumudi"</note> on the *Karana-kutuhala*. || 8 ||

**Sudhakara** — The meaning is clear; all underlying rationales <term>vasana|the physical or mathematical explanation behind a rule</term> are to be understood as already stated in the *Siddhantas* <note>the foundational astronomical treatises of India</note>.

->In the excellent "Jewel of Rationale" composed by the son of the illustrious **Krpalu**,<-
->the rationale of the mathematics of planetary conjunctions, combined with fine logic, is prepared here.<-

Thus ends the section on the "Elevation of the Moon’s Horns" in the *Vasana-vara-vibhushana* <note>The Excellent Jewel of Rationale</note> commentary.
