# Chapter on Aspects — 8 <note>Though the previous section also ended as Chapter 8, the manuscript continues with this numbering for the section on Pata</note>

**The Possibility of the *Pata* and Knowledge of Past or Future Occurrences—**

> Here, the Sun and Moon are taken as joined with the degrees of precession <term>ayanamsha|the angle between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs</term>, but without the Moon’s node <note>Rahu</note>. When they have the same declination, they are called **Vyatipata** and **Vaidhrita** respectively, occurring when their sum equals six signs or twelve signs. || 1 ||

The *Pata* <note>a mathematical point where the Sun and Moon have equal declination</note> is found by the minutes remaining or exceeding the sum of six or twelve signs, divided by the sum of their daily motions, which results in the days passed or to come.

**Sumatiharsha** — First, the background is written: In the **Saka** year 1539, in the month of **Kartika**, during the dark fortnight, on the 10th day, a Tuesday, the years elapsed were 434 and the total days <term>ahargana|the count of days since the start of the epoch</term> were 158,751. At the time of sunrise in **Jodhpur**, the mean positions were: the Sun at 6 | 12 | 45 | 32; the Moon at 4 | 12 | 2 | 47; the precession <term>ayanamsha</term> was 18 | 14 | 34; the Node <note>Rahu</note> was 2 | 12 | 31. The "with-precession" <term>sayana</term> Sun was 7 | 1 | 0 | 6 and the Moon was 5 | 10 | 12 | 1 <note>corrected from OCR 5|10|72|1</note>.

In this calculation of the *Pata*, the Sun and Moon are taken only as joined with the precession. Where it is not specified to include the Node, one must use the *Sayana* values. When the sum of these two equals six signs <note>180 degrees</note>, it is named **Vyatipata**; when the sum equals twelve signs <note>360 degrees</note>, it is named **Vaidhriti**. This occurs when their declinations are equal. This equality of declination is how the possibility of a *Pata* is known. Regarding the "remaining or exceeding" minutes: the minutes by which the sum is less than or greater than six or twelve signs should be divided by the sum of the Sun and Moon’s daily motions. The result is the time in days and so on. If the minutes were "remaining" <note>less than the target</note>, the time is yet to come; if they were "exceeding," the time has already passed. || 1 ||

**Knowledge of the past and future of the *Pata*—**

> Having calculated the positions of those two [the Sun and Moon] and the Node <note>Rahu</note> for that specific moment, one should calculate the latitude <term>vishikha</term> in minutes and so on as before. || 2 ||

> Depending on whether the Moon is in an odd or even quadrant, and whether it is in the same or opposite celestial hemisphere as the Node-included Moon, one can truly know if the *Pata* has passed or is yet to come at that time. || 3 ||

**Sumatiharsha** — "For that specific moment": Using the calculated days passed or to come, the Sun, Moon, and the Node are adjusted to their "true" positions for that specific moment by multiplying the motion by the time...

<summary>This page concludes the chapter on planetary conjunctions and begins the eighth chapter on the Vyatipata and Vaidhrita aspects. It provides the mathematical rules for determining when the Sun and Moon reach equal declination and includes a worked example from Jodhpur in 1617 CE.</summary>
<keywords>Vyatipata, Vaidhrita, declination, Ayanamsha, Bhaskara, Sumatiharsha, Jodhpur, Saka 1539, astronomical aspects, Pata</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating the Pata (astronomical aspects), specifically focusing on whether the phenomenon has already occurred (past) or is yet to occur (future), and how to determine the correct mathematical segments for the calculation.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>In the Joy of Calculation</header>

Having determined the latitude <term>shara|celestial latitude</term> in minutes and seconds from the table segments as previously described, one must now determine the characteristics of whether the aspect is past or future. If the tropical Moon <note>original: "Sayana-vidhu," the Moon's position adjusted for the precession of the equinoxes</note> is situated in an odd quadrant <term>oja-pada|the 1st or 3rd quadrants of the zodiac, i.e., 0–90° or 180–270°</term> and is in the same celestial hemisphere as the Moon-with-node, or if it is in an even quadrant <term>yugma-pada|the 2nd or 4th quadrants, i.e., 90–180° or 270–360°</term> and is in the opposite hemisphere from the Moon-with-node, then the aspect is known to be past <note>original: "gata-pata"</note>. This is calculated from the time when twelve or six signs were completed. Otherwise, if the Moon is in an odd quadrant and in the opposite hemisphere, or in an even quadrant and in the same hemisphere, the aspect is to be known as future <note>original: "eshya-pata"</note>.

**Calculation Example:**
The tropical Sun is at 7 signs, 1°, 0', 6". The Moon-with-node is at 5 signs, 0°, 17', 21". Their sum is 12 signs, 1°, 17', 27". Since this exceeds twelve signs, the excess is 1°, 17', 27", which equals 77 minutes and 27 seconds. When this is divided by the sum of the daily motions of the Sun and Moon (80;55,8), the result is 0 days, 5 ghatikas, and 47 palas <note>units of traditional Indian time; 60 ghatikas make one day</note>. Because the sum exceeded twelve signs, the aspect is "past" by 0;5,47 days.

**Now for the calculation at that specific moment:**
On the ninth lunar day, with 5 ghatikas and 47 palas of the night remaining, the positions for that moment are:
The Sun: 6s 21° 39' 42"
The Moon: 4s 10° 51' 17"
The Node <note>original: "pata"</note>: 2s 2° 2' 12"
The latitude segments are derived from the Moon-with-node.
The tropical Sun: 7s 0° 54' 9"
The tropical Moon: 4s 29° 51' 51"
The sum is 0s 0° 0' 0".
The latitude in minutes is 57' 29" South.
The Moon-with-node is at 6s 11° 53' 30" in the Southern hemisphere. Here, the tropical Moon is at 4s 29° 51' 21" in an even quadrant (the second quadrant) in the Northern hemisphere. Therefore, because it is in an even quadrant and in a different hemisphere from the Moon-with-node, the aspect is "past" by 0;5,47...

### Sudhakara: — This meaning is clear.

The rationale <term>upapatti|the underlying logic or proof</term> is as follows: If the Moon is in an odd quadrant, its declination <term>kranti|distance from the celestial equator</term> is increasing. If the Moon-with-node is in the same direction, that declination, when added to the latitude, becomes even greater; therefore, the aspect is past. Likewise, in an even quadrant, the declination decreases; since the Moon-with-node is in the opposite hemisphere, the latitude is subtracted, making it even smaller; thus, the aspect is also past. This is how it is established.

### The Positive or Negative Nature of the Declination Segments —

> The segments of declination are positive in order; in reverse, they should be considered negative.

### Sumatiharsha: — The segments of declination and latitude should be arranged as follows: the first six segments of declination are designated as positive, the next six in reverse order are negative, the next six in order are positive again, and the final six in reverse are negative. This calculation should be performed by placing them one below the other through all four quadrants. A similar arrangement of positive and negative signs should be made for the latitude segments across all four quadrants.

<column-break/>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>Chapter on Aspects — 9</header>

### Determining the Elapsed Segments —

> The degrees of the Moon and the Moon-added-to-node, divided by fifteen, result in the "past segments." || 4 ||

### Sumatiharsha: — One should take the total degrees of the tropical Moon and the Moon-with-node separately and divide each by fifteen <note>original: "tithi," the number 15, representing the 15-degree segments of the table</note>. The quotients represent the "past segments" <note>original: "gata-khanda"</note> for each. These should be counted forward or backward according to the positive and negative designations established previously.

**For the latitude segment:**
Moon-with-node: 6s 11° 53' 30" = 191° 53' 30".
Divided by 15, the quotient is 12, with a remainder of 11° 53' 30".
**For the declination segment:**
Tropical Moon: 4s 29° 51' 21" = 149° 5' 21".
Divided by 15, the quotient is 9 "past segments," with a remainder of 14° 5' 21".
Counting from the declination segments, the 10th segment is the current one <term>bhogya|the segment currently being traversed</term>. In the second quadrant, this is the 4th segment (299), which is designated negative. For the latitude segments, it is the 13th segment; counting into the third quadrant, the 1st segment (70) is designated positive. The remainders for both are: Declination remainder 14° 5' 21", Latitude remainder 11° 53' 30". || 5 ||

### Determining the Past and Future of the Aspect —

> The calculation should be done in forward or reverse order; the remaining degrees are called the "arc." If the aspect is past, the degrees are as they are; if it is to come, subtract them from fifteen. These two are used to find the current segment values. || 5 ||
> Place two or three current segments separately—future segments if the aspect is to come, past segments if it has passed. The "current segment" <note>original: "bhogya-khanda"</note> is kept in one place for whichever has the smaller arc degrees. || 6 ||
> By the thirteenth part <note>original: "vishvamsha," the number 13</note> of the current declination segment, and from the current latitude segments, after correcting the previous ones, they gradually become the "clear" <term>sphuta|corrected or accurate</term> values. || 7 ||

### Sumatiharsha: — When the aspect is past, the remaining degrees for both are known as the "arc degrees" <note>original: "chapa-ansha"</note>. If the aspect is future, the remaining degrees found previously must be subtracted from 15; the result is the "arc degrees" for both. One should set these arc degrees for declination and latitude aside. Here, since the aspect is past, the remainders themselves are the arc degrees: 14° 5' 21" for declination and 11° 53' 30" for latitude.

Then, starting with the "current segment" of declination and latitude, one should set down two or three segments separately in two places. If the aspect's characteristics show it is past, use the past segments; if future, use the future segments. Between the latitude and declination, the one with the smaller arc degrees has its "current segment" kept in one place, while the others are placed in two. The positive or negative status of these segments has already been determined. For the smaller latitude, use two segments; for the larger, use three...

<vocab>Karana-kutuhala, Pata-adhikara, Sayana-chandra, Sapata-chandra, Kranti-khanda, Shara-khanda, Sumatiharsha, Sudhakara, Jyotisha</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the mathematical procedures for determining if a celestial aspect (Pata) is past or future based on the Moon's quadrant and hemisphere. It provides detailed calculations for dividing zodiacal degrees into 15-degree segments to find the "current segment" for correcting declination and latitude.</summary>

<keywords>celestial latitude, declination, tropical moon, odd quadrant, even quadrant, past and future aspects, mathematical segments, Karana-kutuhala, Sumatiharsha, Sudhakara Dvivedi</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating the Pāta (the astronomical aspect where the Sun and Moon have equal declination). The previous page established whether the event was past or future; this page provides the specific mathematical steps to find the "middle" or exact time of that occurrence.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>In the Joy of Calculation</header>

...thus, the mean values are obtained. By arranging them in this manner, the true calculations should be performed. Here, because the aspect is past <note>original: "gatapatatvat," referring to the Pāta having already occurred</note>, the "remaining portion" <term>bhogya-khaṇḍa|the part of a mathematical segment yet to be traversed</term> is taken first. The "past portions" <term>gata-khaṇḍa|segments already traversed</term> are placed in two locations. Because the celestial latitude <term>shara|the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the ecliptic</term> has small values, its remaining portion is placed in one spot, and the remaining past portions are placed in two spots. The designations of "positive" and "negative" (plus and minus) are to be known from the instrument as previously established.

Now, the calculation for the true positions: By a "thirteenth part" <note>original: "vishvamshakena," referring to the number 13, often used as a divisor in these specific planetary equations</note>. With this thirteenth part (13), one should first correct the remaining portions of the declination <term>krānti|the angular distance of a body north or south of the celestial equator</term>. If the two values are of the same type (both positive or both negative), take their difference; if they are of different types, take their sum. In the same celestial hemisphere <term>gola|either the northern or southern half of the celestial sphere</term>, the rule is reversed: add those of the same type and subtract those of different types. Although not explicitly stated in the root text, this must be done as explained in the commentary. Thus, the declination segments become "true" <note>refined for accuracy</note>. Furthermore, when corrected by the latitude segments, they become even more accurate. The corrections are as follows: in the same hemisphere, add those of the same type; in the other hemisphere, add those of the same type and take the difference for different types—this is established by the commentary. For instance, with the thirteenth part (13) of the remaining declination segment, values that are negative remain negative due to being in the southern hemisphere. When the latitude segments are of a different hemisphere, one takes the difference for the same type and the sum for different types.

### Finding the Middle of the Aspect (Pāta) —

> In the first part, the multiplier should be equal to the degrees of the small arc; in the even segments, it is the difference of the arc degrees.
> Subtract those from fifteen in the odd segments; then, subtract the segments multiplied by their own parts from the "arrows multiplied by fifteen" (latitude). ॥ 8 ॥
>
> Divide the remainder by the "uncorrected true segment" to find the degrees and so on. Add this to the degrees of the corrected segments.
> Divide the result (multiplied by sixty) by the daily motion; the resulting days truly indicate the middle of the aspect, whether past or future. ॥ 9 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa's Commentary:** In the first part, the multiplier is the degrees of the small arc. If the difference between the arc degrees of both is four, then if possible, one should multiply the sixth segment. These multipliers, derived from the arc degrees of the even segments, are subtracted from fifteen. Use the remainder to multiply the third and fifth segments. By multiplying all in this way, they become "true" and are called "multiplied by their own parts." 

Once this calculation of multipliers is done, what follows? He says: "Subtract." One should subtract these "segments multiplied by their own parts" from the "latitude multiplied by fifteen" <note>original: "tithighna-bāṇa," where tithi stands for 15 and bāṇa (arrow) stands for celestial latitude</note>, which is the minutes of the Moon's latitude multiplied by fifteen. Subtract as many as can be subtracted. Divide the remainder by the "uncorrected true segment." The result should be taken in degrees and so on. Add this to the degrees of the segments that were subtracted. Sum the multipliers derived from the arc degrees of those segments that were fully subtracted, and add that to the degrees. Then, multiply by sixty and divide by the Moon's daily motion to find the time in days, ghatikas, and so on. If the characteristics previously showed the aspect was "past," then from the time when the twelve signs were completed—

<column-break/>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>Chapter 9: On the Pāta (Aspects)</header>

—the middle of the aspect is found by the days and units just calculated. If the characteristics showed it was "future," then the middle of the aspect will occur after that many days from the previously mentioned time. 

Now, because the "latitude multiplied by fifteen" (832;15) is small, the segments cannot be subtracted. Therefore, the multipliers of the segments are calculated. The remainder is 832;15. When divided by the "uncorrected true segment" (392), the result is 0;27,23 in degrees and so on. Since there are no multipliers, the "corrected segment degrees" are zero. These (0;27,23) are multiplied by sixty and divided by the Moon's daily motion (741;50). The result is 0;10,18 days and so on. By subtracting this from the previous time of 5;47 (since the aspect was past), the middle of the aspect is found. Thus, in the month of Kartika, during the dark fortnight on the ninth day, at the time of 16;5 ghatikas remaining in the night, the middle of the aspect should be known. 

At the time of the middle of the aspect, having calculated the instantaneous positions of the Sun, Moon, and Node, one should calculate the declination of the Sun. When the declinations of both (Sun and Moon) are equal, the middle of the aspect is "pure" (accurate); otherwise, it is not. For example: Sun 6s 12° 26' 42"; Moon 4s 8° 10' 10"; Node 2s 11° 1' 32". Sun's declination is 12° 54' 31". <note>The text repeats the Sun's declination with a slight variation: 12° 54' 32".</note> The Moon's latitude is 48' 48" South. The Moon's declination corrected by latitude is 12° 11' 43", while the Sun's is 11° 56' 42". Since the difference is very small, it is not considered an error. ॥ 9 ॥

### Special Rule —

> When the remaining portion of the declination is added to the latitude segment, 
> then in this calculation of the aspect, the "past" and "future" status is reversed. ॥ 10 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa's Commentary:** When the "to-be-traversed" (remaining) segment of the Moon's declination is such that it can be subtracted from the remaining segment of the latitude, then the status of "past" and "future" is swapped: a "past" aspect should be known as "future," and a "future" one should be known as "past." ॥ 10 ॥

**Sudhākara's Commentary:** Everything is clear in its proper order. Here is the rationale <term>upapatti|mathematical proof or logic</term>: At the moment when the sum of the tropical Sun and Moon is six or twelve signs, the difference in their true declinations is exactly equal to the latitude. By increasing the Moon's mean declination segments by fifteen degrees and correcting them with the latitude segments, the true declination segments are made. Then, by correcting them with the corresponding solar declination segments, the "declination-difference" segments are created. In calculating the solar declination segments, the Moon's motion is taken as equal to fifteen degrees, while the motion of the node is 15/13. Then the proportion is: if fifteen degrees gives a certain remaining segment, what does the solar motion give? The first declination segment is equal to a thirteenth part (1/13), and by successive subtraction, all declination segments are measured by thirteenth parts. Now, regarding the latitude...

<summary>This page details the mathematical procedures for finding the exact time (the "middle") of the Pāta aspect, involving complex corrections for declination and latitude. It includes a specific numerical example and explains a rule for reversing the "past" or "future" status of the event based on segment values.</summary>
<keywords>Pāta, middle time calculation, declination, celestial latitude, Moon's daily motion, Sumatiharṣa, Sudhākara, Karaṇakutūhala, mathematical segments</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for determining the exact timing of the Pāta (the moment when the Sun and Moon reach equal declination). It follows the previous discussion on identifying whether the event is past or future based on the remaining portions of the mathematical segments.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>In the Joy of Calculation</header>

When the difference between the declinations <note>original: "krāntyantaram"</note> decreases and becomes zero, that is precisely the middle time of the Equality of Declination <term>krāntisāmya|the astronomical phenomenon where the Sun and Moon have identical declination, considered a significant time for ritual and observation</term>. For that purpose, just as one calculates the ascendant <term>lagna|the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term> using the rising times of one’s own location, one performs the calculation for the time elapsed or remaining in the segment of the declination difference. Specifically, if the remaining portion <note>original: "bhogya-khaṇḍa"</note> is fifteen units, then using the remaining degrees or fifteen minus the remaining degrees—called the "arc"—one calculates as follows: 

<margin>Remaining Arc<br/>-------<br/>15</margin>

Whether the time is past or future, the movement of the declination difference corresponds to the remaining degrees. Its form is:

15 × Remaining Arc
-------------------
When the celestial latitude <term>shara|the angular distance of a planet or the Moon north or south of the ecliptic</term> is subtracted from this, the result is obtained. Then, for the other segments, having multiplied them by fifteen through the method of common denominators and subtracting, a proportion is made: if by the "impure segment" <term>ashuddha-khaṇḍa|a mathematical interval that has not yet been cleared or fully calculated</term> one gets fifteen degrees, then what is obtained by the remaining fifteen degrees? The result is:

Remaining
----------
Impure Segment

Then, the degrees are added to the number of cleared segments. With those degrees, a proportion is made to find the time: if by the minutes of the Moon’s motion one gets sixty <term>ghaṭīs|a traditional unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>, then what is obtained by the minutes of these degrees?
The resulting past or future time = 
Degrees × 60 × 60
------------------
Moon's motion in minutes

This is consistent with the result divided by sixty from midday. In the refinement process, because the remaining segments have been surpassed, the procedure is reversed—this logic is straightforward.

### Calculating the Duration—

->The number 203 divided by the impure segment yields the time-units;<-
->The duration remains the same from the middle time, both before and after. ॥ 11 ॥<-

**Sumatiharṣa:** The number 203 <note>original: "tri-khā-ashvi," where tri=3, kha=0, and ashvi=2, read right-to-left in the Sanskrit system</note> is to be divided by the previously calculated impure segment. The result, in time-units such as ghaṭīs, is the duration <term>sthiti|the period during which the astronomical aspect or eclipse is in effect</term> before the middle time. The duration after the middle time is exactly the same. For example, if 203 is divided by an impure segment of 329, the result is 5 ghaṭīs and 37 minutes of duration. Because the aspect is past, subtracting this from the middle time of 16 ghaṭīs and 5 minutes gives the end of the aspect at 10:28; adding it gives the start at 12:42. If 38:19 ghaṭīs have passed since sunrise, that is the time of contact <note>original: "sparsha," the beginning of the aspect</note>; at 43:55 ghaṭīs past sunrise is the middle of the aspect; and at 42:32 ghaṭīs past sunrise is the release <note>original: "moksha," the end of the aspect</note>. ॥ 11 ॥

### Investigation of the Cleared Segments—

->When all the segments here, of the nature of the first segment,<-
->Are subtracted, if the remainder is greater than 480;<-
->Then the occurrence of the Pāta is not possible; when it is possible,<-
->Then one must calculate the past or future time from the cleared segment. ॥ 12–13 ॥<-

<column-break/>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>Chapter on the Pāta—9</header>

**Sumatiharṣa:** When, among those multiplied by fifteen, all the segments multiplied by their own factors are subtracted and cleared—meaning, for example, when the six segments in their positive form are subtracted in order, or when the six segments are subtracted in their basic form—if the remainder of the celestial latitude <term>shara</term> is greater than 480 <note>original: "kha-nāga-sāgara," where kha=0, nāga=8, sāgara=4</note>, then the Pāta aspect cannot occur. When, in all these impure segments, the remainder of the latitude is less than 480, then it is possible. Now, he explains the calculation of past or future time when such a Pāta is possible: from the cleared segment, the multipliers and segments that were cleared within the latitude should be combined. That cleared segment, added to the latitude and divided by the Moon's motion and multiplied by sixty, allows one to calculate the past or future time. The signs of past and future are as described before. ॥ 13 ॥

### Calculating Duration when the Pāta Occurs—

->Likewise, the remainder of the latitude is subtracted from 480;<-
->Multiplied by nine and divided by the final segment, the result is the duration. ॥ 14 ॥<-

**Sumatiharṣa:** Subtract the remainder of the latitude that allowed the Pāta to occur from 480. The result should be multiplied by nine and divided by the "final segment" <note>original: "antya-khaṇḍa"</note>. Half of the resulting value in ghaṭīs and minutes is the duration. This occurs both before and after midday: the contact <note>sparsha</note> is before the middle of the Pāta, and the release <note>moksha</note> is after the middle of the Pāta. ॥ 14 ॥

### Definition of the Equality of Declination and Duration—

->As long as the difference is less than half the sum of the diameters,<-
->That is considered the Equality of Declination, and that is its duration. ॥ 15 ॥<-

**Sumatiharṣa:** As long as the difference between the declinations of the Sun and Moon is smaller than half the sum of their angular diameters <note>original: "mānayogārdha"</note>, it should be understood as the Equality of Declination itself. The duration of that Pāta lasts for that same period. Due to the lack of distinction in units like fingers <term>angula|a traditional unit of length</term>, it is tripled to become units like arc-minutes <term>kalā</term>, since the difference in declination is measured in arc-minutes. Now, regarding the fruit <note>spiritual result</note> of the Pāta in the established Siddhānta:

> "The interval during the duration of the Pāta is not recommended for worldly actions by the wise.
> However, bathing, prayer, fire-offerings, and charity performed here truly lead to infinite merit. ॥ 15 ॥"

As stated here by Bhāskara <note>referring to the astronomer Bhāskara II</note>, the meaning is clear.

Now, for another example: in the Śaka year 1543, during the month of Śrāvaṇa, bright fortnight, on the 9th lunar day, Tuesday, at 16:33 ghaṭīs. The Nakṣatra is Viśākhā at 12:54. The Yoga is Śukla at 15:26. At 38:42 ghaṭīs, the elapsed years...

# The Joy of Calculation

### Page 110

The day-count <term>ahargaṇa|the total number of days elapsed since the beginning of a specific astronomical epoch</term> is 161,230. The Mean Sun is at 3 signs, 16° 56' 16". The Moon is at 6 signs, 26° 18' 30". The Node is at 4 signs, 13° 45' 34". At sunrise, the local Precession of the Equinoxes <term>ayanāṃśāḥ|the angular distance between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs</term> is 18° 18' 21". The ascensional difference <term>carapala|a time correction for sunrise based on local latitude</term> is 72 units. The Sun, corrected by the ascensional difference, is at 3 signs, 15° 51' 57", with a daily motion of 57' 2". The Moon is at 7 signs, 0° 20' 4", with a motion of 829' 35". The Node is at 4 signs, 13° 45' 30". 

The Tropical Sun <term>sāyanārka|the Sun's position measured from the vernal equinox</term> is 4 signs, 4° 11' 23", and the Tropical Moon is 7 signs, 18° 54' 15". Their sum <note>original: "yoga"</note> is 11 signs, 23° 5' 38". This is subtracted from 12 signs, leaving a remainder of 6° 54' 22". Converting this to minutes of arc gives 414' 22". This value is divided by the sum of their daily motions (886' 37"), resulting in 0 days, 27 minutes, and 2 seconds. This is the time remaining <note>original: "gamya"</note> to reach the exact conjunction.

At that specific time, the Tropical Sun is 4s 4° 38' 2", the Moon is 7s 25° 21' 51", and the sidereal Node is 4s 13° 46' 59". The Moon's position plus the Node <note>original: "sapāta candra"</note> is 11s 2° 0' 58" 29"'. The celestial latitude <term>bāṇa|the angular distance of the Moon north or south of the ecliptic</term> is 42' 44" South. 

Now, to determine if the moment of equal declination <note>original: "pāta"</note> has passed or is yet to come: if the Tropical Moon is in an odd quadrant and the sum of the Moon and Node indicates the solar hemisphere, then the event has passed. To calculate the exact values, the Tropical Moon's degrees are 235° 21' 51". When divided by the segment of 15 degrees <note>original: "tithi-bhaktā"</note>, we find that 15 segments of declination have passed, and the current segment's remaining portion <note>original: "bhogya-khaṇḍa"</note> is 236. The remainder is 10° 29' 51". For the Moon-plus-Node position (350° 58' 29"), dividing by 15 shows that 23 segments of latitude have passed, with a remaining negative segment of 70, and a remainder of 5° 58' 29". 

The rule for calculation is: "The sum of values of the same type, and the difference of values of different types" <note>original: "ekajātyoryaugo bhinnajātyorantaram"</note>. This is the universally accepted method for combining the declination and the celestial latitude. Both being positive or both being negative requires addition; if they differ, subtraction is performed. 

Taking the remaining declination segment (236) as positive and combining it with the latitude segment which is negative, we account for the celestial spheres. Here, the latitude (42' 44") multiplied by fifteen is small (641' 0"). Dividing this by the "impure segment" (184' 9") gives 3° 28' 25". When multiplied by 60 and divided by the Moon's motion (829' 35"), the result is 0 days, 15 minutes, 15 seconds. This indicates the time elapsed since the equal declination. On Tuesday, the 9th day of the bright fortnight of the month of Shravana, 22 ghatis <term>ghaṭī|a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term> after sunrise, the conjunction of the Sun occurred.

<column-break/>

### Page 111
### Chapter 9: On the Equal Declination

From the calculated time of 0:15:13, the equal declination has passed. In this day, the middle of the event <note>original: "pāta-madhya"</note> occurs at 12:49 ghatis after sunrise. At this time, the Tropical Sun and Moon and the Node are: Sun 4s 4° 22' 34", Moon 7s 21° 59' 28", and Node 4s 13° 46' 11". The Sun's declination is 19° 19' 8" 53"' 30"". The Moon's declination is 18° 29' 53" South, which when corrected by the Southern latitude of 59' 18", becomes 19° 29' 11". The difference between the two declinations is only 18 seconds of arc. Because this difference is so small, the calculation is considered accurate; the declinations have achieved equality.

Now, calculating the duration of the event:
The mathematical interval (2203) divided by the "impure segment" (184' 9") gives a duration of 11:57 ghatis. The middle time is 12:49. The difference gives the time of onset <term>sparśa-kāla|the beginning of the period of equal declination</term> at 0:52 ghatis after sunrise. Adding the duration gives the time of completion <term>mokṣa-kāla|the end of the event</term> at 24:46 ghatis.

The solar disk <note>original: "ravi-bimba"</note> is 11' 12" and the lunar disk is 10' 19". Their sum is 21' 31", and half of that sum is 10' 46". Triple that value is 32' 18". At the moment of onset:
Sun: 4s 4° 12' 12"
Moon: 7s 19° 6' 15"
Node: 4s 13° 45' 38"
The Sun's declination is 19° 3' 55" North, while the Moon's declination is 17° 46' 34" South. The Southern latitude is 67' 45", making the corrected Moon's declination 18° 54' 19". The difference between the declinations is 22' 32", which is less than the combined half-sum of the disks (32' 21"), confirming the onset of equality.

At the time of release <note>original: "mokṣa-kāla"</note>:
Sun: 4s 4° 34' 55"
Moon: 7s 24° 36' 41"
Node: 4s 13° 46' 49"
The Sun's declination is 19° 25' 54" North. The Moon's declination is 19° 13' 13" South, and the latitude is 46' 51" South. The corrected declination is 20° 0' 4". The difference is 34' 7", which is greater than the combined half-sum, indicating the event has ended.

Another example is given for the Śaka year 1290, in the month of Kārtika, on the 7th day of the bright fortnight, a Thursday. At sunrise, the day-count was 67,823.
Mean Sun: 7s 5° 42' 58"
Moon: 3s 29° 4' 17"
Node: 9s 12° 10' 96" <note>The number 96 likely represents a high fraction of a degree</note>
True Sun: 7s 4° 18' 53", motion: 60' 40"
True Moon: 3s 20° 45' 17", motion: 725' 35"
Ascensional difference: 58. Precession: 14° 5' 40".
Tropical Sun: 7s 18° 23' 53". Tropical Moon: 4s 4° 54' 17".
Their sum: 11s 23° 18' 10". Subtracting from 12 signs gives 6° 41' 50". Dividing by the sum of motions (786' 15") gives 0:30:40 days. Thus, the event occurs 30:40 ghatis after sunrise on that Thursday.

<page-num>112</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>112 In the Joy of Calculation</header>

At the time when the aspect <term>pāta|the moment of equal declination between the Sun and Moon</term> will occur, the calculated time in <term>ghaṭis|units of 24 minutes</term> is 30:40. At this specific moment, the Sun is at 7 signs, 4° 49' 57" <note>In the sign of Libra</note>. The Moon is at 3 signs, 27° 0' 8". The Node is at 9 signs, 12° 11' 15". From the Moon plus its Node <note>original: "sapāta-candra"</note>, the celestial latitude <term>bāṇa|the Moon's distance north or south of the ecliptic</term> in minutes and seconds is 16' 1" 21" North. The Tropical Moon <term>sāyana-candra</term> is at 4 signs, 12° 5' 58". The Moon with its Node is at 1 sign, 9° 12' 1". It resides in the celestial sphere <note>original: "dekgole," likely referring to the direction of declination</note>, thus the upcoming aspect should be understood. At the time when twelve conjunctions <note>original: "yoga"</note> have passed, the designation of the declination segments as positive or negative is established as before. 

The degrees of the Tropical Moon are 13° 15' 58". Dividing this by 15 <note>a standard mathematical interval representing a "tithi" or phase segment</note>, the result is 8 with a remainder of 11° 5' 8". This means eight declination segments <term>krānti-khaṇḍa</term> have passed. By calculating in order and reverse, the third remaining segment of the declination is negative, valued at 236. For the degrees of the Moon plus its Node (3 signs, 9° 12' 1"), dividing by 15 gives 2. Two latitude segments have passed, and the third is the remaining segment, valued at 56, which is positive. With the remainder being 9° 12' 11", and because the aspect is still to come, the position of both remainders is subtracted.

The arc degrees for both are: Declination arc 3° 5' 15" and Latitude arc 5° 4' 47". By taking the remaining segments and the upcoming aspect, the remaining segment is doubled and placed in two positions. Following the rule "two and three," the two segments are placed twice. For the declination segment of 236, divided by the universal fraction, the negative declination segments are combined with the 13 units. Just as latitude segments are positive, declination segments should be added to them. When one is negative and the other is negative, they are added; if they differ, the difference is taken. 

The corrected segments processed for latitude are: the first segment is 197. Multiplied by the small arc degrees of 5° 4' 12", the result is 77° 5' 3". Subtracted from the previous latitude value of 25° 40' 15" multiplied by fifteen, the remainder is 17° 55' 12". The second segment is 61. The arc degrees are 3° 5' 4" 12" and 5° 4' 7" 57". Multiplying by their difference of 1° 5' 3" 45" results in 492° 3' 27". Subtracting this from the previous latitude remainder of 17° 55' 12" leaves 127° 3' 9". This remainder is irregular. The third segment is 274. Multiplied by the difference of 1° 5' 3", which is subtracted from 15 to get 13° 6' 53", the result is 359° 3'. Since 127° 3' 9" cannot be subtracted from this, the latitude remainder 127° 3' 9" is divided by the uncorrected segment 274, resulting in 4° 38' 48" in degrees. This is added to the segment's degrees of 3° 4' 52", totaling 10° 26' 47". 

Multiplying by sixty (627:47) and dividing by the Moon's motion (725:35) gives 0 days, 51 minutes, and 50 seconds. Adding these days to the previous time (30:40) gives 11:22:30. Thus, on Friday, the 8th day of the dark fortnight of the month of Kartika, at 22:30 <term>ghaṭis</term> from sunrise, the middle of the aspect occurs. At this time, the Sun is at 7s 5° 4' 15" 50"', the Moon is 4s 7° 26' 57"', and the Node is 9s 12° 14' 38"'. The Sun's declination is 8° 7' 14" 4"' and the Moon's latitude is 20' 5" 57"'. The true declination is 77° 7' 44"'. Since both declinations are equal, this is the correct calculation.

<vocab>पातः (pātaḥ - aspect/declination equilibrium), क्रान्तिः (krāntiḥ - declination), घटिका (ghaṭikā - 24-minute unit), सूर्यः (sūryaḥ - Sun), चन्द्रः (candraḥ - Moon), सायनचन्द्रः (sāyana-candraḥ - Tropical Moon), क्रान्तिखण्ड (krānti-khaṇḍa - declination segment), शरः (śaraḥ - latitude/arrow)</vocab>

<page-num>113</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>Chapter 9: On the Aspect (Pāta) 113</header>

The time is 22:3 <term>ghaṭis</term>. Dividing by the uncorrected value of 274, the duration is 8:2. This duration is subtracted from the middle of the aspect (22:30), resulting in 14:28. This is the moment of contact <note>original: "pāta-sparśa"</note> in <term>ghaṭis</term> passed since sunrise. When added to the middle of the aspect, it is 30:32; this is the moment of exit <note>original: "pāta-nirgama"</note> from sunrise. The angular diameter <term>māna</term> of the Sun is 11' 3" and the Moon is 9' 48". Half their sum <term>mānaikyārdha|the sum of the semi-diameters of two celestial bodies</term> is 10' 25". This value in digits <note>original: "aṅgula"</note> multiplied by three gives 31' 15" in minutes of arc. Thus, one should calculate the difference in declination at the moments of contact and release. As long as the difference is less than half the sum of the diameters, the duration is recognized. Afterward, the middle of the aspect is determined according to the rules.

->**Sudhākara** <note>Sudhākara Dvivedī, a 19th-century mathematician and commentator</note>: This is for the sake of clarity.<-

Here is the mathematical proof <term>upapatti</term>. The equality of the two declinations lasts as long as the difference between them exists. When the difference in declination is equal to half the sum of the diameters, that is the moment of contact <note>beginning</note> and release <note>end</note>. In this calculation, the teacher has taken the mean sum of the semi-diameters and the mean lunar motion. Then, by proportion: if fifteen degrees correspond to the uncorrected segment, what corresponds to half the sum of the diameters? Furthermore, if sixty <term>ghaṭis</term> correspond to the lunar motion, what is the duration in <term>ghaṭis</term> for those calculated minutes?

The formula is:
(15 × 32 × 60 × 6) / (Uncorrected Segment × Lunar Motion)

If the lunar motion <note>original: "ga" or "ca ga"</note> is 790, then:
Duration = 2187 / Uncorrected Segment.

If the lunar motion is 780, then:
Duration = 2215 / Uncorrected Segment.

Taking the average of both, we get 2201 / Uncorrected Segment. The teacher has adopted the value 2203 / Uncorrected Segment due to the very small difference. 

Now, when the remaining measurement is 480/15 = 32, then the difference equal to half the sum of the diameters does not necessarily result in declination equality from the perspective of the centers. It is not possible if it is greater, and only possible when it is less; that is the midpoint. From this, the proportion for bringing the half-duration is: if there are fifteen parts in the final segment, then what is the result for (480 - remainder)/15?

Then, if there are sixty <term>ghaṭis</term> for the Moon's motion, what is the duration for the minutes obtained?

The formula is:
((480 - remainder) × 60 × 60) / (Uncorrected Segment × 800) 
= ((480 - remainder) × 36) / (Uncorrected Segment × 8)

<vocab>पातस्पर्शः (pāta-sparśaḥ - beginning of the aspect), पातनिर्गमः (pāta-nirgamaḥ - end of the aspect), सुधाकरः (sudhākaraḥ - Name of the commentator), उपपत्तिः (upapattiḥ - mathematical proof/derivation), मानैक्यार्द्ध (mānaikyārdha - half the sum of the diameters), चन्द्रगतिः (candra-gatiḥ - lunar motion)</vocab>

# Chapter 10: On the Possibility of Eclipses

<margin>Calculating the celestial latitude—</margin>
> Multiply the month-count <term>māsagaṇa|the total number of months elapsed since the start of the epoch</term> by two and divide by three. Add 272. Add one-twentieth of the elapsed years. Apply the Sun’s <term>ghatī-phalam|time correction</term> divided by five as an addition or subtraction. To those degrees, add the month-count as signs. For a solar eclipse, add half a sign <note>15 degrees</note>. Taking the "arm" <term>bāhu|the sine of the arc</term>, the degrees plus half of themselves result in the celestial latitude <term>śara|literally "arrow," referring to the Moon's angular distance north or south of the ecliptic</term> in digits <note>original: "aṅgula"</note>. || 1 ||

**Sumatiharṣa**—Following this are four verses in the *Śārdūlavikrīḍita* meter... <note>The commentator explains the mathematical procedure for Verse 1</note>. The month-count, calculated from the Śaka era after subtracting the directions and five <note>a mnemonic for the number 510</note> and adding the intercalary months, should be multiplied by 2 and divided by 3. The resulting number in degrees and minutes should be increased by 272. Then, it should be increased by the twentieth part of the years elapsed since the epoch. To this value in degrees, the "time correction of the Sun" mentioned by the teacher <note>Bhāskara II</note> divided by 5 should be added or subtracted as appropriate. Then, add the month-count to the place of the signs. If a solar eclipse <note>original: "ravi-grahaṇa"</note> is possible, add half a sign (15 degrees) to the previous total. Then calculate the "arm" <note>the sine portion</note>; those degrees plus their own half give the celestial latitude in digits and minutes. In a solar eclipse, the direction is known by the hemisphere where the half-sign was added.

For example: In the Śaka year 1542, on the full moon of the month of Mārgaśīrṣa, Wednesday. The years elapsed: 437. The month-count: 5414.
5414 multiplied by 2 = 10828.
Divided by 3 = 3609° 20'.
Add 272 = 3881° 20'.
Years elapsed are 437; adding their twentieth part (21° 51') = 3903° 11'.
For the arc, the previous Sun’s time correction (3) divided by 5 = 0° 36'.
Since it begins from Cancer, subtract it from the previous 3903° 11' = 3902° 35'.
Converting these degrees into signs (dividing by 30) gives 130 signs, 2° 35'.
Add the month-count (5414) to the signs = 5544 signs, 2° 35'.
Dividing the signs by 12, we ignore the quotient 462 as it represents full cycles.
The remainder is 0 signs, 2° 35'.
The "arm" <note>sine</note> is the same: 2° 35'.
Added to its own half (1° 17') = 3° 52'.
This is the celestial latitude in digits <note>aṅgulas</note>.
Now, for the possibility of a solar eclipse in Śaka 1522, on the 30th lunar day <note>New Moon</note> of the month of Śrāvaṇa, Monday. Elapsed years: 417. Month-count: 5161... <note>Calculation continues</note> 5161 multiplied by 2 = 10322. Divided by 3 = 3440° 40'.

<summary>This page concludes the chapter on planetary nodes (Pāta) and begins Chapter 10 on eclipses (Parvasambhava). It provides the mathematical formula and a step-by-step example for calculating the Moon's celestial latitude (śara) using the elapsed month-count.</summary>

Combined with two signs: 37|2|4|0|0. Increased by the twenty-first part of the years (417): 20|51.
37|33|31|0. The pre-noon time in the sign of Cancer: 3 <note>ghatikas</note>. The result, diminished by the latitude divided by the divisor: 0|36. Result: 37|32|55|0. As stated, the positions beginning with signs: 12|41|21|55|0. The position of the sign combined with the month-count: 51|61. Added: 52|85|12|55|0. Divided by twelve, the remainder in signs: 5|12|52|0. Because it is a solar eclipse, it is combined with half a sign (0|15|0|0), resulting in: 5|27|55. Its "arm" <note>the arc used for sine calculations</note> is: 0|2|5|0. These degrees are: 1|5|0. Combined with its own half (1|2): 3|7. The latitude <term>śara|celestial latitude; the angular distance of a heavenly body north or south of the ecliptic</term> in digits and so on is North, because the position (including half a sign) is located in the northern hemisphere. || 1 ||

### Calculation of Zenith Distance—

> At the end of the conjunction, the zenith distance in time (natanāḍikā) is divided by four. The Sun, in signs etc., is decreased or increased by this, depending on whether it is in the eastern or western hemisphere. This is combined with the degrees of the precession of the equinoxes. The "arm" of that result is multiplied by the "seven" <note>the number 7</note>. Doubled, this becomes the declination in degrees. From this, the latitude is found by its own process. The degrees of zenith distance divided by four give the parallax. The latitude is then adjusted by that for a solar eclipse. || 2 ||

**Sumatiharṣa—** Regarding "at the end of the conjunction": having calculated the zenith distance (nata) at the time the conjunction ends, a fourth part of it is taken. The hours of zenith distance are divided by four <gloss>4</gloss>; the resulting signs are obtained. The remainder is multiplied by thirty and again divided by four to get degrees. The remainder of that is multiplied by sixty and divided by four to get minutes. Thus, the result in signs etc. is obtained. By this, the Sun in signs etc. at the end of the conjunction is decreased in the eastern hemisphere and increased in the western hemisphere. Then, adding the degrees of precession, one finds the "arm" <note>bhuja</note>. With that arm, the "seven" <gloss>7</gloss> are multiplied and subtracted. That becomes the declination in degrees etc. Then the latitude is determined. From the zenith distance degrees, the parallax <term>nati|parallax in latitude; the apparent displacement of an object when viewed from different positions</term> is calculated as one-fourth of those degrees. The previously calculated latitude, when adjusted by this parallax, becomes "clear" or corrected.

For example: the end of the conjunction is at 28|56. Midday is 16|43. The difference between these is the zenith distance in time: 12|43. Since it is in the west, it is divided by four. Signs obtained: 3. Remainder: 0|13. Multiplied by thirty: 6|30. Divided by four: degrees 1. Remainder: 2|30. Multiplied by sixty: 150. Divided by four: minutes 37. Remainder: 2. Multiplied by sixty: 120. Divided by four: seconds 30. Thus, in signs etc.: 3|1|37|30. Even if the Sun is "gross" <note>unrefined/approximate</note> at the end of the new moon or by the desired elapsed time: 3|0|35|34. Combined with the western zenith distance: 6|2|13|4. Combined with the precession (17|57): 6|20|10|24. Its arm: 0|20|10|24. By this, seven signs (7) are decreased: 6|9|49|36. Again multiplied by the arm (0|20|10|24) using the "cow-urine" method <note>original: "gomūtrikā"; a zigzag method of multiplication</note>: 2|40|20. Doubled: 5|21|40. This is the declination in degrees etc. without further adjustment. Since the Sun (with precession) is in the southern hemisphere, it is adjusted by the southern latitude of the observer (24|35|9).

---

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>Chapter 10: On the Possibility of Eclipses</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

The resulting southern zenith distance degrees are 29|55|49. Divided by four: 7|27. This is the southern parallax. The previously calculated northern latitude (3|7), when adjusted by this—since the directions are different, the difference is taken—becomes 4|20. This is the "clear" <note>corrected</note> latitude, which is North. || 2 ||

### Possibility and Impossibility of Eclipses—

> Nineteen for the Moon; and for the Sun, if the latitude is less than the half-sum of the diameters, an eclipse occurs. This possibility should be considered first by the wise. The sum of the mean Sun and the Node, starting from the beginning of the zodiac, is doubled and increased by two. This identifies the "Lord of the Phase" (Parveśa), such as Brahmā and others, when divided by seven. || 3 ||

**Sumatiharṣa—** Regarding "nineteen": Nineteen is the half-sum of the diameters <term>mānaikyārdha|the sum of the angular semi-diameters of the two bodies involved in an eclipse</term> for the Moon. When the Moon's latitude is less than this half-sum, an eclipse occurs. This possibility should be known beforehand by scholars. For example: the Moon's latitude is 3|52. This is less than the half-sum (19); therefore, a lunar eclipse is possible. Then, the calculation for a solar eclipse should be done just like the lunar one. The Sun's clear latitude is 4|20. This is less than the Sun's half-sum of diameters (11); therefore, a solar eclipse is possible. The calculation is done as previously described for both.

Now, regarding the "beginning of the zodiac": the sum of the mean Sun and the Node (without the additive constants), starting from the signs, is doubled. Add two. Divide by seven. The remainder tells which "Lord of the Phase" governs the current period. The lords are: Brahmā, the Moon (Śaśī), Indra, Kubera, Varuṇa, Agni, and Yama. For example, in a lunar eclipse, the mean Sun without the constant is 4|37|6|0|59|38. The Node is 23|6|14|38|25. Their sum is 46|10|9|53|8|3. Doubled: 92|20|39|19|16. Plus two: 924. Divided by seven, the remainder is 0. Starting from Brahmā, seven have passed; thus Brahmā is the Lord of the Phase. In a solar eclipse, the Sun is 4|17|4|1|29|13. The Node is 22|5|9|24|18. Their sum is 43|9|19|0|45|31. Doubled: 87|9|6|29|31|2. Plus two: 88|10|43|2|4. Divided by seven (7), the remainder is 6. The Lord of the Phase should be known as Yama. || 3 ||

**Sudhākara—** This is clear.

Here is the reasoning: The months are taken separately, doubled, and increased by the parts of the king's body <note>a metaphorical numerical constant</note>. Divided by three, the result in degrees, combined with the signs of the month-group... this describes the beginning of the text's calculations. By the sum of the nodes at the fifteen-day mark, the term "constant" (kṣepa) is established.

## Description of His Own Lineage —

> There was a twice-born <note>Brahmin</note> of the Shāṇḍilya lineage, in a home that was a dwelling for virtuous people. He was clever in the essence of the inquiry into the Vedas and the Law-books, a jewel-mine of gentleness. He was the foremost of astronomers, known on earth for his own virtues by the name Maheshvara. His son, the poet Bhāskara, composed this Joy of Calculation. || 4 ||

> Thus, in this Joy of Calculation, a manual on the motion of the planets spoken by Bhāskara, the favorite of the refined intellect, concludes the chapter on the possibility of eclipses of the Sun and Moon. || 10 ||

**Sumatiharṣa:** — "There was..." etc. The meaning is to be understood as clear. || 4 ||

Thus ends the tenth chapter, on the possibility and impossibility of eclipses, in the venerable Karaṇakutūhala. || 10 ||

**Sudhākara:** — The meaning is clear.

> In this "Ornament of the Excellent Proof," composed by the son of the illustrious Kripālu, the excellent proof for the rule of the possibility of eclipses, combined with good words and logic, is gathered here.
> May virtuous people always read this "Ornament of the Excellent Proof," which gives joy to the Goddess of Speech, composed by Sudhākara.
> Sudhākara, the son of Rāmaprasāda, made this "Ornament" for the Karaṇakutūhala of the venerable Bhāskara, for the delight of the wise; by wearing this, Speech becomes elegant.
> This was completed on the sixth day of the bright fortnight of the month of Āshvina, in the Shaka year 1803 <note>approx. late September 1881 CE</note>, by the grace of the Lord of the Universe.

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>Chapter on the Cloud-Sun—11</header>

# Chapter on the Cloud-Sun — 11

**Calculation of the Cloud-Sun—**

->The wise scholar should subtract one sign from the <term>True Sun|Samaliptārka; the Sun's longitude corrected for its true position</term>.<-
->Subtract the remaining degrees and fourteen minutes from a full circle <note>12 signs or 360 degrees</note>.<-
->Convert this to minutes, square them, double the result, and divide by the minutes in a circle <note>21,600 minutes</note>.<-
->Add the result to the other set, then add thirteen degrees.<-
->Subtract this from the Sun’s position at sunrise, combined with the True Sun.<-
->The remainder, when combined with the previously calculated "added value," is the true <term>Cloud-Sun|Niradārka; a calculated solar position used specifically for meteorological predictions during eclipses</term>. ॥ 1–3 ॥<-

**Sumatiharṣa—**Subtract one sign and fourteen degrees (1s 14° 0' 0") from the mean position of the True Sun. Subtract that remainder from twelve signs (12s). Convert that value into minutes and square it. Multiply that square by two and divide by the minutes of a circle (21,600). The result obtained should be set aside and named "the added value" (āḍhya). This value, combined with thirteen (13) degrees, is added to the True Sun. From that sum, subtract the Sun's position at sunrise. The final remainder, combined with the "added value" calculated earlier, becomes the clear and true Cloud-Sun.

For example, in a lunar eclipse, the True Sun is 8s 10° 16' 10". Subtracting one sign and fourteen degrees (1s 14° 0' 0"), the remainder is 6s 16° 16' 10". Subtracting this from a circle (12s), we get 5s 13° 43' 50". Its value in minutes is 9823' 50". The square of this is 96,507,701' 21". Doubled, it is 193,015,402' 42". Dividing by the circle minutes (21,600), the result in minutes etc. is 8935' 53". Dividing by sixty to get degrees, we get 148° 55' 53". Dividing by thirty to get signs, we get 4 signs, 28 degrees, 55 minutes, and 53 seconds (4s 28° 55' 53"). This value—the "added value"—combined with thirteen degrees (13°) is 5s 11° 15' 53". Adding this to the True Sun (8s 10° 16' 10") gives 1s 21° 31' 3". Subtracting the Sunrise Sun (7s 29° 35' 20"), the remainder is 6s 21° 7' 13". Adding the "added value" (4s 28° 55' 53") to this, we get 12s 16° 19' 10".

**The Purpose of This—**

->Observing the <term>ninth-division|navāṃśa; a 3°20' arc of the zodiac used in Vedic astrology for finer detail</term> of the Sun or Mars, one should predict a cloudless sky.<-
->If it falls in the ninth-division of Saturn or Mercury, there will be light or scattered rain. ॥ 4 ॥<-

<note>1. This Chapter on the Cloud-Sun appears to be an interpolation added by someone later, rather than part of the original text.</note>

<column-break/>

->In the ninth-division of the Moon or Venus, there is great water (heavy rain) during the rainy season.<-
->If it reaches the ninth-division of Jupiter, clouds are seen with strong winds. ॥ 5 ॥<-
->The one who knows the shadow of the clouds during an eclipse or at the moment of the ascendant—<-
->I worship his pair of feet with a handful of flowers. ॥ 6 ॥<-

**Sumatiharṣa—**If the Cloud-Sun is in the ninth-division of the Sun or Mars, then there are no clouds. In the ninth-division of Saturn or Mercury, there is "small rain," meaning light showers. In the ninth-division of the Moon or Venus, there is "abundant rain" (monsoon clouds). When it is in Jupiter's ninth-division, there is light rain accompanied by wind. ॥ 5 ॥

->Whatever I have stated according to my intellect or from tradition,<-
->Whether appropriate or inappropriate, may the wise forgive my speech. ॥ 1 ॥<-
->There is a well-known city in the groves of the Vindhya mountains, filled with all kinds of learned men.<-
->Its leader is Bhata, the ornament of his family, born of the Chaukiya lineage. ॥ 2 ॥<-
->For the delight of the illustrious Vīra, expert in good policy, who stood at the forefront of the lineage of the Golden Mountain (Hemādri),<-
->Who became a king of kings by uprooting firm-standing rival monarchs. ॥ 2 ॥<-
->In the month of Vaiśākha, when Jupiter was in the beloved Taurus, while engaged in acts of charity,<-
->When the auspicious era of the illustrious Vikrama had passed by [1135 years],<-
->In the month of Prauṣṭhapada (Bhadrapada), on the day of Vināyaka (Ganesha), on a Friday—<-
->This was completed with joy in the year of Sumati, through the grace of the illustrious Teacher. ॥ 3 ॥<-
->The total verses/units of this work are eighteen hundred and fifty (1850).<-
->Should there be a surplus or deficiency due to the abundance of figures, it is not a fault. ॥ 4 ॥<-
->In this *Karaṇavrata* composed by Sumatiharṣa,<-
->In the *Moonlight of the Lotus of Calculators* (Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī), the occurrence of eclipses is determined. ॥ 5 ॥<-

**Conclusion—**Thus ends the chapter on the consideration of the Cloud-Sun in the *Karaṇakutūhala* composed by the illustrious master Bhāskarācārya. ॥ 11 ॥

**Sumatiharṣa—**Thus ends the chapter on the consideration of the Cloud-Sun in the section on the occurrence of eclipses, within the *Moonlight of the Lotus of Calculators* (Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī), a commentary in the style of the *Brahmatulya* composed by the illustrious Sumatiharṣa.

This is the eleventh chapter, the Chapter on the Cloud-Sun, edited by Satyendra Mishra, containing the commentaries of Sumatiharṣa and Sudhākara Dvivedī.

<meta>This page transitions from the specific meteorological calculations of the "Cloud-Sun" back to the foundational principles of the Karaṇakutūhala. We are now at the beginning of the first chapter, which deals with "Mean Motion" (Madhyamādhikāra). This section establishes the mathematical epoch and the methods for calculating the number of days elapsed since the start of the era (Ahargaṇa) and the average positions of the planets before applying corrections.</meta>

<page-num>123</page-num>

# The Calculation of Wonders (Karaṇakutūhala)
## With the "Shivamanti" Hindi Commentary
### Chapter on Mean Motion—1

**Verse 1—Invocation**

I (Bhāskara) bow to Ganesha, Sarasvati, Brahma, Vishnu, Shiva, and the planets beginning with the Sun, and then I shall explain the method for determining the clear positions of the planets, which is equivalent to the <term>Brahma-Siddhanta|An ancient and authoritative astronomical treatise</term>, in a simplified manner.

**Verses 2, 3—Calculating the Total Day-Count (Ahargaṇa)**

By subtracting 1105 from the desired <term>Shaka Era|A historical calendar system starting in 78 CE</term> year, one obtains the "Elapsed Years." Multiply the number of elapsed years by 12 and add the months passed since the month of Chaitra to obtain the "Solar Months." Multiply the solar months by 2 and add 66; set this value in two places. Divide the first by 900 and subtract the resulting quotient from the second place. Divide that remainder by 65 to find the <term>Intercalary Months|Adhimāsa; extra lunar months added to synchronize the lunar and solar years</term>. Adding these Intercalary Months to the Solar Months gives the "Lunar Months." Multiply the lunar months by 30 and add the lunar days (<term>Tithis</term>) passed in the current month to obtain the "Lunar Days."

Set the Lunar Days in two places. To the first, add 3 and divide by 703. Add the resulting quotient to the second place and divide by 64. The result is the number of <term>Omitted Days|Kshayadina; days dropped in the lunar calendar to maintain alignment with the moon's phases</term>. Subtracting the Omitted Days from the Lunar Days results in the <term>Ahargaṇa|The total count of civil or "Savana" days elapsed since the epoch</term>.

Divide the Ahargaṇa by 7. The remainder corresponds to the weekday, starting with Thursday. For example: a remainder of 1 = Thursday, 2 = Friday, 3 = Saturday, 4 = Sunday, and so on.

**Verses 4, 5, 6—Planetary Epoch Positions (Kshepaka)**

| Planet | Sun | Moon | Moon's Apogee | Moon's Node | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Sign** | 10 | 10 | 4 | | 9 | 7 | 2 | 2 | 8 | 4 |
| **Degree** | 29 | 29 | 15 | 17 | 21 | 21 | 4 | 18 | 3 |
| **Minute** | 13 | 5 | 12 | 25 | 24 | 14 | 0 | 5 | 43 |
| **Second** | 0 | 50 | 59 | 9 | 21 | 30 | 51 | 55 | 17 |

<column-break/>

<header>Chapter on Mean Motion—1 123</header>

By adding these starting values (<term>Kshepaka|The constant position of a planet at the beginning of an era</term>) to the mean positions derived from the day-count, one obtains the mean positions of the planets at sunrise at <term>Lanka|The hypothetical center of the earth in ancient Indian geography</term>.

**Verse 7—Calculating the Mean Sun**

Set the day-count (Ahargaṇa) in two places. Multiply the first by 13 and divide by 903. Subtract the resulting degrees and minutes from the value in the second place to find the mean position of the Sun, Mercury, and Venus.

Divide the number of elapsed years by 64. The resulting minutes should be subtracted from the mean Sun, Mercury, and Venus as a yearly correction (<term>Abdabija</term>).

**Verse 8—Calculating the Mean Moon**

Multiply the day-count by 10 and set it in two places. Divide one by 17 and subtract the resulting degrees and minutes from the value in the second place. Further, subtract the 8600th part of the day-count to obtain the degrees and minutes of the mean Moon.

**Verse 8.5—Calculating the Moon's Apogee**

Set the day-count in two places. Divide one by 9 and the other by 4012. The sum of these two results provides the degrees and minutes of the <term>Moon's Apogee|Candrocca; the point where the moon is furthest from the Earth</term>.

**Verse 9—Calculating the Node**

Set the day-count in two places. Divide one by 19 and the other by 2700. The sum of these two results provides the degrees and minutes of the <term>Moon's Node|Candrapata; the point where the moon's orbit crosses the ecliptic</term>.

**Verse 10—Calculating Mars and Mercury’s Apex**

Multiply the day-count by 11 and set it in two places. Divide one by 21 and the other by 5144. The sum of these two results provides the mean position of Mars.

Multiply the day-count by 4 and set it in two places. Divide the first by 43 and add the result to the second place. Then subtract the 1421st part of the day-count to obtain the position of the <term>Apex of Mercury|Budhashighra; a point used to calculate the planet's variable velocity</term>.

**Verse 10.5—Calculating Jupiter**

Set the day-count in two places. Divide one by 12 and the other by 4227. The difference between these two results provides the degrees and minutes of the mean Jupiter.

<summary>This page introduces the fundamental methods for calculating the day-count (Ahargaṇa) and the mean positions of the planets according to the Karaṇakutūhala. It provides specific mathematical constants and formulas to determine planetary motion relative to the Shaka era starting in 1183 CE (Shaka 1105).</summary>

<header>In the Calculation of Wonders</header>

### Verse 11½—Determining the Fast Anomaly of Venus

Multiply the <term>Day-Count|Ahargaṇa; the total days elapsed since the epoch</term> by 16 and place the result in two locations. Divide the first by 7,451 and the second by 10. By summing the resulting <term>degrees and minutes|Anshadi; units of circular measurement</term> from both, the <term>Fast Anomaly|Shīghrocca; the mathematical point used to calculate the planet's faster orbital variations</term> of Venus is obtained.

### Verse 12—Determining Saturn

Place the Day-Count in two locations. Divide the first by 30 and the second by 9,367. Summing the resulting degrees and minutes from both provides the position of **Mean Saturn**.

### Verse 13—The Mean Motion of the Sun and Other Planets

<note>This table provides the daily average distance (in arc-minutes and arc-seconds) that each celestial body travels across the sky.</note>

| Planet | Sun | Moon | Moon's Apogee | Node | Mars | Mercury's Center | Jupiter | Venus's Center | Saturn |
|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| **Arc-minutes** | 59 | 790 | 6 | 3 | 31 | 245 | 5 | 96 | 2 |
| **Arc-seconds** | 8 | 35 | 41 | 11 | 26 | 32 | 0 | 8 | 0 |

### Verse 14—The Prime Meridian

The cities that fall along the <term>meridian|yāmyottara; the north-south line of longitude</term> stretching from the city of Lanka <note>original: "Lankānagara"; historically considered the intersection of the equator and the prime meridian in Indian astronomy</note> (zero latitude) to Sumeru (the North Pole) are: Kanyakumari, Kanchi, Sitaparvat (the White Mountain), Vatsa, Ujjain, Gargarata, and Kurukshetra. These locations are known as the **Meridian Countries**.

### Verse 15—Longitude Correction

Multiply the distance of your location east or west from the Meridian by the daily motion of the planet, then divide by 80. The resulting value is either added or subtracted. Specifically, subtract the result for locations east of the Meridian and add it for locations to the west; this yields the **Longitude-Corrected** planets.

### Verse 16—Secular Corrections for the Planets

Subtract 1105 from the desired <term>Shaka year|An Indian calendar system</term> to find the "Elapsed Years" (<term>Gatābda</term>).
- Elapsed Years ÷ 78 = Moon's Correction (negative, in arc-seconds).
- Elapsed Years ÷ 63 = Jupiter's Correction (negative).
- Elapsed Years ÷ 33 = Lunar Node/Rahu Correction (positive).
- Elapsed Years ÷ 30 = Moon's Apogee Correction (positive).
- Elapsed Years ÷ 22 = Mercury's Fast Anomaly Correction (positive).
- Elapsed Years ÷ 9 = Venus's Correction (positive).

<column-break/>

<page-num>125</page-num>

## Chapter 2: True Motion

### Verse 1—The Apogees of the Planets

<note>The "Apogee" (Mandocca) is the point in a planet's orbit where it is furthest from the Earth and appears to move at its slowest speed.</note>

| | Sun | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn |
|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| **Zodiac Sign** | 2 | 4 | 7 | 5 | 2 | 8 |
| **Degree** | 18 | 8 | 15 | 22 | 21 | 21 |
| **Minute** | 0 | 30 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 |

According to the opinions of some teachers, the Apogee of Saturn is at 7 Signs and 28 Degrees.

### Verse 2—The Specific Constant Values

These constant values (<term>Parasaṅkhyā</term>) are utilized in calculating the <term>Fast Equation|Shīghraphala; the correction for the planet's second inequality</term>.

| Planet | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn |
|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| **Zodiac Sign** | 2 | 1 | 0 | 2 | 0 |
| **Degree** | 21 | 14 | 23 | 27 | 13 |

The Sun and Moon do not have Fast Anomalies. The Fast Anomalies for Mercury and Venus were described in the previous chapter on Mean Motion. For Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, the **Mean Sun** acts as their Fast Anomaly.

### Verse 3—Positive and Negative Anomaly

The <term>Anomaly|Kendra; the angular distance from the apogee</term> is found by subtracting the planet's position from its Apogee. Subtracting the planet from its Slow Apogee yields the **Slow Anomaly** (<term>Mandakendra</term>), and subtracting it from the Fast Anomaly yields the **Fast Anomaly** (<term>Shīghrakendra</term>). If the Anomaly begins with the signs Aries through Virgo, the resulting correction is positive; if it begins with Libra through Pisces, the correction is negative.

### Verse 4—Calculating the Base and Perpendicular Arcs

If the Anomaly is less than 3 signs, that value itself is the **Base Arc** (<term>Bhuja</term>). If it is between 3 and 6 signs, subtract it from 6 signs to find the Base. If it is between 6 and 9 signs, subtract 6 signs from it to find the Base. If the Anomaly exceeds 9 signs, subtract it from 12 signs to find the Base.

Subtracting the Base Arc from 3 signs yields the **Perpendicular Arc** (<term>Koṭi</term>).

### Verse 5, 5½—Refining the Apogee of Mars

Depending on which quadrant (a "pad" or quarter of 3 signs) the Fast Anomaly of Mars falls in, subtract the degrees already passed from 3 signs to find the remaining degrees of that quadrant. Between the degrees passed and the degrees remaining, take the smaller value, convert it to arc-minutes, and divide by 400 to find the correction in degrees...

<vocab>Day-count, Venus's Fast Anomaly, Mean Saturn, Mean Motion, Equator, Meridian, Longitude Correction, Secular Correction, Apogee, Constant Value, Slow Anomaly, Fast Anomaly, Base and Perpendicular Arcs</vocab>

<summary>This page details the mathematical ratios for finding the mean positions of Venus and Saturn, defines the Indian Prime Meridian, and begins the second chapter on "True Motion" by establishing apogees and the trigonometry of planetary anomalies.</summary>
<keywords>Karaṇakutūhala, Bhāskara II, planetary mean motion, Indian Prime Meridian, Ujjain, Mandocca, Shighrocca, Bhuja-Koti, Bija correction</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues from the calculations of the mean positions of the planets. Having determined where the planets would be if they moved in perfect circles at constant speeds, Bhāskara II now introduces the "corrections" (Sanskirt: *saṃskāra*) necessary to find their true positions in the sky as seen from Earth, accounting for their elliptical-like orbits and varying speeds.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>
<header>In the Calculation of Wonders</header>

By applying the correction to the <term>Apogee of Mars|mandocha; the point in the orbit where the planet is furthest from the Earth and moves slowest</term>, the true apogee of Mars is determined. (If the <term>anomaly|kendra; the angular distance of the planet from its apogee</term> begins from the sign of Cancer, the result is subtracted; if it begins from Capricorn, it is added).

By subtracting one-third of the resulting degrees and minutes from the <term>Mars-parameter|parākhya; a specific constant used for Mars in this system</term>, the true Mars-parameter is obtained.

### Verses 6, 7 — Calculating the Sine

The nine <term>Sine-segments|jyā-khaṇḍa; the differences between values in a sine table</term> are, in order: 21, 20, 19, 17, 15, 12, 9, 5, and 2. To calculate the <term>Sine|jyā</term> of a planet or an anomaly, first determine its <term>base-arc|bhuja; the distance from the nearest equatorial point</term> in degrees. Divide these degrees by 10. The quotient indicates how many full sine-segments to sum together. For the remaining degrees, multiply them by the "current" (next) sine-segment, divide by 10, and add this result to the previous sum to find the desired Sine value.

| Seg. 1 | Seg. 2 | Seg. 3 | Seg. 4 | Seg. 5 | Seg. 6 | Seg. 7 | Seg. 8 | Seg. 9 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Sine Segments** | 21 | 20 | 19 | 17 | 15 | 12 | 9 | 5 | 2 |
| **Running Total** | 21 | 41 | 60 | 77 | 92 | 104 | 113 | 118 | 120 |

### Verse 8 — Calculating the Arc

To find the <term>Arc|dhanu; the inverse sine</term> of a given number: subtract the sine-segments from that number. The segments that can be fully subtracted are called "pure," and the one that cannot is "impure." Multiply the remainder by 10 and divide by the value of the "impure" segment. Add this result to 10 times the number of "pure" segments to find the total arc.

By reversing this process, the Sine can be found from the Arc once again (i.e., changing addition to subtraction and multiplication to division).

### Verses 9, 10 — Calculating the Equation of Center

To find the <term>Equation of Center|mandaphala; the correction for the planet's non-uniform motion due to its elliptical orbit</term> for any planet, first calculate the Sine of the degrees of its <term>Slow Anomaly|mandakendra</term>. Multiply that Sine by 10 and divide by the following divisors:

| Calculation | Result |
|---|---|
| Sine × 10 ÷ 550 | = **Sun’s** Equation of Center |
| Sine × 10 ÷ 107 | = **Mars’s** Equation of Center |
| Sine × 10 ÷ 198 | = **Mercury’s** Equation of Center |
| Sine × 10 ÷ 228 | = **Jupiter’s** Equation of Center |
| Sine × 10 ÷ 784 | = **Venus’s** Equation of Center |
| Sine × 10 ÷ 157 | = **Saturn’s** Equation of Center |

<column-break/>

<page-num>127</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Positions: 2</header>

The correction for the Equation of Center is added or subtracted based on the anomaly. If the anomaly is in the half-circle starting with Aries, it is added; if it starts with Libra, it is subtracted.

The Sun and Moon reach their **true positions** through this Equation of Center correction alone. However, the five star-planets (Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn) are only "Slow-Corrected" (*manda-sphuṭa*) at this stage and require further adjustment.

### Verse 10 ½ — Special Correction for the Moon

By applying a correction equal to 1/27th of the Sun’s Equation of Center to the Moon’s position, the **True Moon** is found. This correction is applied to the Moon in the same direction (addition or subtraction) as the Equation of Center was applied to the Sun.

### Verses 11, 12 — Calculating the Correction for Velocity

Determine the base-arc of the planet's anomaly. In the process of finding the Sine, use the "current" sine-segment (the one being transitioned through) to calculate the <term>Correction for Velocity|gatiphala; the adjustment to the planet's daily speed</term>. The rules are:

| Rule | Result |
|---|---|
| Current Segment ÷ 9 | = **Sun’s** velocity correction (in minutes) |
| Current Segment × 13 ÷ 4 | = **Moon’s** velocity correction |
| Current Segment × 2 ÷ 7 | = **Mars’s** and **Mercury’s** velocity correction |
| Current Segment ÷ 50 | = **Jupiter’s** velocity correction |
| Current Segment ÷ 12 | = **Venus’s** velocity correction |
| Current Segment ÷ 120 | = **Saturn’s** velocity correction |

By applying this correction to the mean motion (adding if the planet is in the half-circle starting with Cancer, subtracting if in Capricorn), one obtains the **True Velocity** for the Sun and Moon, and the "Slow-Corrected Velocity" for the five star-planets.

### Verse 13 — Calculating the Equation of Conjunction for Mars and others

Subtract the "Slow-Corrected" position of the planet from its <term>Fast Apogee|shīghrocca</term> to find the <term>Fast Anomaly|shīghrakendra</term>. Calculate the Sine (*bhuja-jyā*) and Cosine (*koṭi-jyā*) of this anomaly.

Multiply the Cosine by the planet's parameter, double it, and add it to 14,400. To this sum, add or subtract the square of the parameter (subtract if the anomaly is in the Cancer half-circle, add if in Capricorn). The square root of this final sum is the <term>Hypotenuse|shīghrakarṇa; the distance from the Earth to the planet</term>.

Multiply the Sine of the anomaly by the parameter and divide by the Hypotenuse; the result is the <term>Equation of Conjunction|shīghraphala</term>. By applying this correction to the "Slow-Corrected" planet (adding if the anomaly starts in Aries, subtracting if in Capricorn <note>The text likely means Libra, following standard Sanskrit astronomical convention for the second half-circle</note>), the **True Planet** is determined.

<summary>This page details the mathematical procedures for converting "mean" planetary positions into "true" positions using trigonometry (Sines) and two major corrections: the Equation of Center (manda) and the Equation of Conjunction (shīghra). It also provides a specific velocity correction for each celestial body and a refined lunar correction based on the Sun's position.</summary>

<keywords>trigonometry, sine tables, equation of center, true velocity, Mars, Moon correction, hypotenuse, astronomical corrections, Bhāskara II, planetary longitude</keywords>
