Verse 14 — The Correction of Results.

By applying half of the <term>Equation of Center|manda-phala; the correction due to the planet's elliptical orbit</term> to the <term>mean planet|madhyam-graha; the theoretical position if the orbit were a perfect circle</term>, it becomes the "Slow-Corrected" position. Next, one should apply the correction for half of the <term>Equation of Conjunction|śīghra-phala; the correction for the Earth's own motion relative to the planet</term>. Finally, by applying the full Equation of Center and the full Equation of Conjunction, the <term>True Planet|spaṣṭa-graha</term> is determined.

| Correction | Result (Phala) |
| :--- | :--- |
| First | Half Equation of Center |
| Second | ,, Equation of Conjunction |
| Third | Full Equation of Center |
| Fourth | ,, Equation of Conjunction |

Verses 15, 16 — Correcting the Motion.

When the <term>Motion-Correction|gati-phala</term> is applied to the <term>mean motion|madhya-gati</term>, the "Slow-Corrected Motion" is obtained. By then applying the correction for the motion of the <term>conjunction point|śīghrocca</term> to this result, one finds the motion of the conjunction center.

Multiply the motion of the conjunction center by the <term>Sine-difference|bhogya-jyā</term> used in calculating the Equation of Conjunction. Multiply that product by 40, and divide by seven times the <term>variable hypotenuse|śīghra-karṇa</term>. Subtract the resulting quotient from the speed of the conjunction point to find the <term>True Motion|spaṣṭa-gati</term>. If the quotient is greater than the speed of the conjunction point, subtract the speed from the quotient to find the true motion. In such a case, the planet will be <term>Retrograde|vakrī; appearing to move backward in the sky</term>.

Verse 17 — Calculating the Precession.

Subtract 1105 from the current <term>Śaka year|the historical era starting in 78 CE</term>. Divide the remainder by 60. Add the resulting degrees and minutes to 11 degrees. This sum provides the <term>Precession of the Equinoxes|ayanāṃśa</term> for the beginning of the current Śaka year.

Verse 18 — The Equation of Time.

Add the Precession to the mean position of the Sun and calculate its <term>base-arc Sine|bhuja-jyā</term>. Multiply this Sine by 2 and divide by 5; the result is the <term>Equation of Time|udayāntara; the correction to convert mean solar time to true solar time</term> in seconds of arc. Add this to the <term>tropical Sun|sāyana-surya; the Sun's position relative to the equinox</term> if it is in an even quadrant (the 2nd or 4th); subtract it if it is in an odd quadrant (the 1st or 3rd).

Divide the base-arc Sine of the Moon by 21 to find the Moon's Equation of Time in minutes of arc. Similarly, add this in even quadrants and subtract in odd quadrants.

<column-break/>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>Chapter on True Positions — 2</header>

Verses 19, 20 — Ascensional Difference.

At the desired location, measure the shadow of a <term>12-digit gnomon|dvādaśāṅgula-śaṅku; a vertical rod used for astronomical measurements</term> at noon on the day before the Sun enters tropical Aries. Multiply this shadow by 10, 8, and 3 1/3 respectively to find the three <term>Daylight-Difference segments|cara-khaṇḍa</term>. By arranging these three in forward and reverse order, one obtains the segments for the 12 zodiac signs.

To find the specific <term>Ascensional Difference|cara-khaṇḍa</term> for a planet, calculate based on the signs already traversed and the remaining degrees. This correction is negative when the planet is in the Northern hemisphere and positive when it is in the Southern hemisphere.

Multiply this result by the planet’s true motion and divide by 60. Apply this final result (in seconds) to the planet's position according to its hemisphere to obtain the <term>Ascensionally-Corrected planet|cara-saṃskṛta-graha</term>.

Verses 21, 22 — Calculating Tithi, Karaṇa, Nakṣatra, and Yoga.

Subtract the Sun's position from the Moon's position. Convert the remainder into degrees and divide by 12. The quotient represents the number of <term>Tithis|lunar days</term> elapsed since the new moon. Multiply the Tithi number by 2 and subtract 1; the result indicates which of the seven "moving" <term>Karaṇas|half-lunar days</term> (starting with *Bava*) is active. Additionally, from the second half of the 14th day of the dark fortnight to the first half of the 1st day of the bright fortnight, the four "fixed" Karaṇas (starting with *Śakuni*) occur.

Convert the Moon's position into minutes of arc and divide by 800. The quotient indicates the number of elapsed <term>Nakṣatras|lunar mansions</term> (starting with *Aśvinī*). Subtract the remainder from 800 to find the remaining portion. Multiply the elapsed or remaining minutes by 60 and divide by the Moon's daily motion to find the time passed or remaining in the current Nakṣatra in <term>Ghaṭīs|units of 24 minutes</term>.

Add the positions of the Sun and the Moon. Convert the sum into minutes and divide by 800. The quotient indicates the number of elapsed <term>Yogas|astrological longitudinal sums</term> (starting with *Viṣkumbha*). Subtract the remainder from 800 to find the remaining portion. Multiply these by 60 and divide by the combined motion of the Sun and Moon to find the time passed or remaining in the current Yoga in Ghaṭīs.

<note>original: "बवादि" (bavādi) refers to the first of the moving Karaṇas.</note>

While the previous page focused on planetary corrections and orbital math, this section moves into the "Chapter on the Three Problems" (Tri-praśna-adhikāra), which deals with the relationship between time, direction, and place—specifically how to calculate the Ascendant (Lagna) and the shadow cast by a gnomon.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>

### Chapter 3: On the Three Problems (Tri-praśna-adhikāra)

**Verses 1–4**—Calculating local rising times and the Ascendant.

278, 299, and 323—these are the three <term>Equatorial Rising Times|laṅkodayamāna; the time in 'palas' it takes for the first three signs of the zodiac to rise at the equator</term>. By arranging these three values in direct and then reverse order, one obtains the rising times for all twelve zodiac signs at the equator.

By adding the <term>Ascensional Difference|charakhaṇḍa; the local variation based on latitude (calculated according to verses 19 and 20 of the 'Spaṣṭādhikāra')</term> to these equatorial rising times beginning with Aries, the <term>Local Rising Times|svodayamāna</term> are determined.

For the <term>Tropical Sun|sāyana ravi; the Sun's position adjusted for the precession of the equinoxes</term>, take the remaining degrees of the current sign. Multiply these degrees by the local rising time of that sign and divide by 30. The result will be the "remaining rising time" of the Sun in <term>palas|a unit of time equal to 6 seconds</term>.

Convert the <term>Desired Time|iṣṭakāla; the time elapsed since sunrise</term> into *palas*. Subtract the Sun’s remaining rising time from it. From the remainder, continue subtracting the local rising times of the signs following the Sun’s current sign. The sign whose rising time is successfully subtracted is called the "purified sign," and the one that cannot be fully subtracted is the "unpurified sign." Multiply the final remaining time by 30 and divide by the rising time of the unpurified sign; the result will be in degrees. Adding these degrees to the count of purified signs gives the **Tropical Ascendant** (Sāyana Lagna). Subtracting the <term>Precession|ayanāṃśa</term> from the Tropical Ascendant yields the **Sidereal Ascendant** (Nirayana Lagna).

If the Sun’s remaining rising time is greater than the Desired Time, multiply the Desired Time by 30 and divide by the local rising time of the Sun's current sign. Adding the resulting degrees to the Sun's position will give the Tropical Ascendant.

**Verses 5–6**—Calculating the Desired Time from the Ascendant.

Multiply the degrees of the Tropical Sun already passed in its current sign by the rising time of that sign and divide by 30; this gives the Sun's "passed rising time" in *palas*.

Similarly, multiply the degrees of the Tropical Ascendant remaining in its sign by its rising time and divide by 30 to find its "remaining rising time" in *palas*.

Add the Sun’s remaining rising time to the Ascendant's passed rising time. To this sum, add the local rising times of all the signs between the Sun and the Ascendant. Divide the total by 60 to find the Desired Time in <term>ghaṭīs|a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>.

If the Tropical Sun and the Tropical Ascendant are in the same sign, multiply the difference in their degrees by the rising time of that sign and divide by 30 to find the Desired Time.

<column-break/>

<header>Chapter 3: On the Three Problems</header>
<page-num>131</page-num>

Even if they are in the same sign, if the Tropical Ascendant is less than the Tropical Sun, subtract the calculated Desired Time from 60 to find the time elapsed during the night. Alternatively, subtract it from the <term>Duration of Night|rātrimāna</term> and add the <term>Duration of Day|dinamāna</term> to find the time elapsed since sunrise.

Just as 6 signs (180 degrees) are added to the Tropical Sun to calculate nighttime positions, one should add 6 signs to the Sun when calculating the Desired Time from a nighttime Ascendant.

**Verse 7**—Calculating the Hour Angle and Altitude.

By adding the <term>Ascensional Difference|charapala</term> to 15 *ghaṭīs* in one place and subtracting it in another, one finds the <term>Mid-day|dinārddha</term> and <term>Mid-night|rātryardha</term> respectively. This applies when the Sun is in the six signs starting with Aries. In the six signs starting with Libra, the opposite is done: adding the difference to 15 gives the Midnight duration, and subtracting it gives the Mid-day duration.

If the Desired Time is less than the Mid-day duration, it is the "Forenoon Altitude-Time" (Pūrva Unnatakāla). If it is greater than the Mid-day duration, subtract the Desired Time from the total Day-duration to find the "Afternoon Altitude-Time" (Para Unnatakāla).

Subtracting the Forenoon Altitude-Time from the Mid-day duration gives the "Eastern Hour Angle" (Pūrva Nata). Subtracting the Afternoon Altitude-Time from the Mid-day duration gives the "Western Hour Angle" (Paścima Nata).

**Verses 8–12**—Calculating the Shadow from Time and Time from the Shadow.

**Calculating the Divisor (Hara):** Subtract 5 from the Mid-day *ghaṭīs*; this is the "Mid-day Divisor."

Take the square of the <term>Hour Angle|nata</term> and set it in two places. Multiply the first by 50 and add 900. Divide this by the second square. Subtract the result from the Mid-day Divisor to get the "Desired Divisor" (Iṣṭahara).

If the Hour Angle is greater than 15 *ghaṭīs*, subtract the Mid-day duration from the Hour Angle to find the Desired Divisor. Divide the Ascensional Difference by the first <term>Sine-segment|charakhaṇḍa</term>, halve the result, and square it. Subtract one-sixth of that square from itself, add 10, and multiply by the <term>Equinoctial Shadow Hypotenuse|palakarṇa</term> to find the <term>Hṛti|a specific diagonal used in spherical trigonometry</term>.

<note>The text describes the derivation of the hypotenuse here.</note> By adding the squares and taking the square root, the Latitude-Hypotenuse (Palakarṇa) is found.

Divide the *Hṛti* by the Desired Divisor to find the <term>Hypotenuse of the Shadow|karṇa</term> in <term>aṅgulas|digits/inches</term>. Set this value in two places: add 12 in one and subtract 12 in the other. Take the square root of the product of these two values; that is the shadow of the **12-digit gnomon** at the desired time.

**Verses 13–14**—Calculating Declination from Sine-segments.

<vocab>
**Three Problems:** Time, place, and direction.
**Equatorial Rising Time:** The time a zodiac sign takes to rise at the Earth's equator.
**Local Rising Time:** Rising time adjusted for the observer's latitude.
**Tropical Ascendant:** The rising point of the ecliptic adjusted for precession.
**Desired Time:** Time elapsed since sunrise.
**Ascensional Difference Segment:** A mathematical tool for latitude adjustment.
**Hour Angle and Altitude:** Coordinates measuring the sun's position relative to noon and the horizon.
**Mid-day/Mid-night:** Half-duration of day and night.
**Desired Divisor:** A specific coefficient in shadow math.
**Equinoctial Shadow Hypotenuse:** The shadow's diagonal length when the sun is on the equator.
**Hṛti:** A technical term for a specific chord in the celestial sphere.
**12-digit Gnomon:** The standard vertical rod used to measure shadows.
**Declination:** The angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator.
</vocab>

<summary>This page details the mathematical procedures for calculating the Ascendant (Lagna) and the shadow of a gnomon. It explains how to adjust equatorial rising times for local latitude and how to relate the time of day to the length of a shadow cast by a twelve-digit rod.</summary>
<keywords>Ascendant calculation, Lagna, Gnomon shadow, Tropical Sun, Local rising times, Hour Angle, Indian Astronomy, Siddhānta</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical instructions from the Karanakutuhala, transitioning from general astronomical coordinates (declination and latitude) into the specific calculations required for Chapter 4: On Lunar Eclipses. It establishes the foundational values for the sizes of celestial bodies and the Earth's shadow.</meta>

362, 341, 299, 236, 150, and 52—these six are the <term>Declination Segments|krāntikhaṇḍa; numerical values used to calculate the Sun or a planet's distance from the celestial equator</term>. To calculate the declination of a planet, first add the <term>Precession|ayanāṃśa; the shift of the equinoxes over time</term> to its position to find its <term>Tropical Longitude|bhujāṃśa</term>. Divide this longitude by 15; the quotient indicates how many full declination segments have passed. Sum these passed segments. Then, multiply the remaining longitude by the next segment (the "current" one) and divide by 15. Add this result to the previous sum to find the declination in minutes. Divide these minutes by 60 to find the declination in degrees.

The hemisphere (North or South) of the declination will be the same as the hemisphere of the planet's sign.

### Verse 15—An Alternative Method for Finding Declination.

Subtract the degrees of the <term>Tropical Planet|sāyana graha</term> from 180. Multiply this remainder by the planet's degrees and set the result in two places. In the first place, divide by 77; subtract this result from 442 degrees and 42 minutes. Divide the value in the second place by this remainder to find the declination in degrees. (This declination also follows the hemisphere of the planet’s position.)

### Verse 16—Calculating Local Latitude.

Add 410 to the <term>Equinoctial Shadow|palabhā; the length of the shadow cast by a 12-digit gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox</term>. Divide the sum by 60 and add the result to the <term>Hypotenuse of the Shadow|akṣakarṇa</term> to find the "divisor." 

Multiply the equinoctial shadow by 90 and divide by this divisor to find the <term>Local Latitude|akṣāṃśa</term>. By applying the declination to this latitude, one finds the <term>Zenith Distance|natāṃśa</term>.

## Chapter 4: On Lunar Eclipses

### Verses 1, 2, 3—Calculating the Hour Angle.

Subtract the <term>Hour Angle|nata; the time or angular distance from the meridian</term> from 30 and multiply the remainder by the hour angle itself. Divide this product by 11,250 to find the result in degrees. Subtract this from 10 degrees to find the <term>Solar Divisor|ravihara</term>. Subtract one-tenth of the Solar Divisor from itself to find the <term>Lunar Divisor|candrahara</term>. Divide the <term>Equation of Center|mandaphala; the correction for the planet's elliptical orbit</term> of the Sun and Moon by their respective divisors to find the "zenith correction" for each. This correction is added to the Solar zenith distance in the west and subtracted in the east. Conversely, for the Moon, it is subtracted in the west and added in the east.

1. Regarding the Hour Angle for the Moon:
From noon to midnight = <note>original: "pūrvakapāla"</note> the Eastern Hemisphere.
From midnight to noon = <note>original: "paścimakapāla"</note> the Western Hemisphere.
For the Sun:
From noon to midnight = Western Hemisphere.
From midnight to noon = Eastern Hemisphere.

<column-break/>

By applying these corrections to the positions of the Sun and Moon, one calculates the specific <term>Lunar Day|tithi</term> appropriate for eclipse calculations.

### Verse 4—Calculating Instantaneous Positions.

Multiply the planet's speed by the time elapsed (or remaining) in <term>ghaṭīs|a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>. Adjust the planet’s position by this result to find the <term>Instantaneous Position|tātkālika graha</term>. At the exact moment of the eclipse's end, the degrees and minutes of the Sun and Moon become equal.

### Verse 5—Calculating Celestial Latitude.

Subtract the <term>Ascending Node|rāhu; the point where the Moon's orbit crosses the ecliptic</term> from 12 signs; the remainder is the "Node." Calculate the sine of the longitude of the Moon-plus-Node. Multiply this sine by 3 and divide by 7 to find the <term>Celestial Latitude|śara</term> in <term>digits|aṅgula; a traditional unit of measurement</term>. The latitude follows the hemisphere of the Moon-plus-Node: Aries through Virgo is North, and Libra through Pisces is South.

### Verse 6—Solstice Knowledge and Alternative Latitude Calculation.

The <term>Southern Solstice Path|dakṣiṇāyana</term> runs from Cancer to Sagittarius, and the <term>Northern Solstice Path|uttarāyaṇa</term> runs from Capricorn to Gemini. 
70, 65, 56, 43, 23, and 9—these are the six <term>Latitude Segments|śarakhanda</term>. Divide the longitude of the Moon-plus-Node by 15 to find the passed segments. Multiply the remainder by the next segment and divide by 15. Adding this to the sum of passed segments gives the latitude in minutes. Divide these minutes by 3 to find the latitude in digits.

### Verses 7-8—Calculating the Diameters of the Sun, Moon, and Earth's Shadow.

Divide the Moon’s speed by 74 to find the <term>Lunar Diameter|candrabimbāṅgula</term> in digits. Multiply the Sun’s speed by 2 and divide by 11 to find the <term>Solar Diameter|sūryabimbāṅgula</term> in digits.

Multiply the Moon’s speed by 3 and divide by 67, then subtract one-seventh of the Sun’s speed to find the diameter of the <term>Earth’s Shadow|bhūbhā-bimba</term> in digits.

The Shadow of the Earth eclipses the Moon when it enters the shadow's circle, and the Moon eclipses the Sun when the Sun enters the Moon’s circle. Therefore, in a lunar eclipse, the Moon is the <term>Eclipsed|chādya</term> and the Earth’s shadow is the <term>Eclipsing|chādaka</term> body. In a solar eclipse, the Sun is the eclipsed and the Moon is the eclipsing body.

### Verse 9—Calculating the Magnitude of the Eclipse.

Add the diameters of the eclipsed and eclipsing bodies together. Subtract the celestial latitude from half of this sum...

<note>The text cuts off here, but it describes the "Grāsamāna" or magnitude of the eclipse, which determines how much of the disk is covered.</note>

<summary>This page provides mathematical formulas for calculating declination, local latitude, and the specific dimensions of the Sun, Moon, and Earth's shadow required to predict eclipses. It defines the roles of celestial bodies as either the 'eclipsed' or the 'eclipsing' entity during these events.</summary>

<keywords>declination, lunar eclipse, celestial latitude, solar diameter, lunar diameter, Earth's shadow, precession, zenith distance, Karanakutuhala</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the astronomical calculations for eclipses from the Karanakutuhala. The previous page established declination and latitude; this page applies those values to determine the magnitude, duration, and visual orientation (deflection) of a lunar eclipse.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>In the Karanakutuhala</header>

By subtracting, the <term>Magnitude of the Eclipse|grāsamāna; the portion of the celestial body covered by the shadow</term> is found. If the magnitude of the eclipse is greater than the size of the body being eclipsed, the remainder indicates a <term>Total Eclipse|khagrāsa; literally "eating the whole," when the entire moon enters the Earth's shadow</term>.

### Verse 10—Calculating the Duration of the Eclipse and Totality.

Multiply the <term>Latitude|śara; the angular distance of the Moon north or south of the ecliptic</term> by 2, add the magnitude of the eclipse, and then multiply the result by the magnitude itself. Take the square root of this value. Multiply this root by 180 and divide by the <term>Difference in Motion|gatyantara; the relative speed between the Sun and the Moon</term>. The result will be the <term>Duration of the Eclipse|sthiti-ghaṭī; the time from the first moment of contact to the final exit</term> in units of <term>Ghatis|ghaṭikā; a traditional Indian unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>.

Multiply the latitude by 2, add the magnitude of total eclipse, and multiply by the total eclipse magnitude itself. Take the square root. Multiply the root by 180 and divide by the difference in motion between the Sun and Moon. The result will be the <term>Duration of Totality|vimarda-ghaṭikā; the time the Moon spends entirely within the shadow</term>.

### Verses 11-13—Calculating the Five Contact Times.

Divide the latitude (measured in <term>Digits|aṅgula; a traditional linear unit used here to measure celestial arcs</term>) by 48. Use the resulting time to add or subtract for the corrections of <term>First Contact|sparśa</term>, <term>Duration|sthiti</term>, <term>End of Eclipse|mokṣa</term>, and the <term>Beginning and End of Totality|sparśa-vimarda and mokṣa-vimarda</term>. If the <term>Moon with its Node|sapāta-candra; the Moon's position adjusted for the Rahu/Ketu nodes</term> is in the 2nd or 4th quadrant, adding the result gives the time of Exit and subtracting gives the time of Contact. If it is in the 1st or 3rd quadrant, subtracting gives the time of Exit and adding gives the time of Contact.

In a solar eclipse, these same duration calculations provide the times for Contact, Exit, and the moments when totality begins and ends.

### Verses 15-16—Calculating Deflection (Valana).

Multiply the <term>Hour Angle|nata; the time distance from the meridian</term> at the moment of contact by 90 and divide by the <term>Half-Day|dinārddha; half the duration of daylight</term> (using the half-day of the solar eclipse for solar calculations) to find the <term>Zenith Distance Degrees|natāṃśa</term>. Find the sine of these degrees and multiply by the local latitude. Divide the product by the <term>Radius|trijyā; standardly 120 in this system</term> to find the <term>Terrestrial Deflection|akṣavalana; the angle between the vertical circle and the hour circle</term> at contact.

Add the <term>Precession|ayanāṃśa</term> to the Moon's position at contact and find the sine of its <term>Complementary Arc|koṭi</term>. Divide this sine by 5 to find the <term>Ecliptic Deflection|ayanavalana; the angle between the ecliptic and the celestial equator</term>. The direction of this deflection follows the direction of the calculated Moon's position.

If the directions of the local latitude and the <term>Tropical Moon|sāyana-candra</term> are the same, add the Terrestrial and Ecliptic deflections; if they are different, find the difference. Find the sine of this result. Multiply by the sum of the radii of the Moon and the Earth’s shadow and divide by 120 to find the <term>True Deflection|sphuṭa-valana; the final orientation angle for the eclipse diagram</term> at contact.

---

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>Chapter 4: On Lunar Eclipses</header>

In the same manner, use the hour angle at the time of exit to calculate the Terrestrial Deflection at exit, and the Moon's position at exit to find the Ecliptic Deflection at exit. From these two, calculate the True Deflection at the time of exit.

### Verse 17—Calculating Latitude at Contact and Exit.

If the latitude was calculated from the Moon and Node in an odd quadrant, subtract one-third of the duration of totality from the latitude to find the latitude at contact, and add it to find the latitude at exit. If they were in an even quadrant, add one-third of the duration of totality to find the latitude at contact, and subtract it to find the latitude at exit.

### Verses 18-19—Instructions for the Eclipse Diagram.

On a smooth, level piece of ground, draw a circle from a fixed center point using a radius equal to the half-sum of the diameters of the eclipsing body (shadow) and the eclipsed body (Moon), and another circle with the radius of the Moon itself. Mark the cardinal directions (East, West, North, South) on this center point. The circle made from the half-sum of the diameters is called the <term>Circle of Combined Radii|mānaikyārddha vṛtta</term>. In this circle, mark the Deflection at contact starting from the East and the Deflection at exit starting from the West, just as one draws a sine. In a solar eclipse, the contact deflection is marked from the West and the exit deflection from the East. If the latitude is South, mark the deflection digits toward the South; if North, mark them toward the North.

The following elements are necessary for calculating a lunar eclipse:

| | | |
|----------|----------|----------|
| (1) Time of the full moon | (6) Difference in motion per ghati | (10) Moon's latitude after 5 ghatis |
| (2) Moon's daily motion | (7) Latitude in time | (11) Earth's shadow segment |
| (3) Sun's daily motion | (8) Moon's latitude at the full moon | (12) Sum of radii segment |
| (4) Difference in motion over 5 ghatis | (9) Moon's latitude 5 ghatis prior | (14) Moon's hourly motion <note>The number 13 is omitted in the original table.</note> |
| (5) Difference of radii | | |

### Verse 20—Locations of Contact, Middle, and Exit.

From the point of the middle of the eclipse, mark the "middle latitude" in the appropriate direction. Then mark the three points of latitude (Contact, Middle, and Exit). Using the radius of the Earth's shadow, draw a circle from the point of the contact latitude. On the eastern side of that circle, the contact of the lunar eclipse occurs, and on the western side, the exit occurs.

### Verses 21-23—Description of the Desired Eclipse Magnitude.

The path of the eclipsing body is defined by a tangent line from the point of the <term>Middle Latitude|madhya-śara; the Moon's latitude at the moment of maximum eclipse</term> to the point of the <term>Latitude at Contact|sparśa-śara</term>, and the path of exit is from the middle to the point of the <term>Latitude at Separation|mokṣa-śara</term>. The line that touches all three latitudes (Contact, Middle, and Separation) is shaped like a bow (an arc).

The contact and the middle of the eclipse occur on the tangent line from the point of contact-latitude to the middle-latitude. In a circle drawn from the center with a radius equal to the <term>Sum of the Semi-diameters|bimbyogārdha; half the sum of the diameters of the eclipsing and eclipsed bodies</term>, where the aforementioned paths of contact and separation intersect this circle, the "immersion" occurs on the path of contact and the "emergence" occurs on the path of separation within the circle formed by the radius of the Earth's shadow.

By multiplying the <term>Digits of the Path|mārgāṅgula</term> by the <term>Desired Time|iṣṭakāla</term> and dividing by the <term>Clear Duration|spaṣṭa-sthiti</term>, the value of the <term>Desired Magnitude|iṣṭa-grāsa</term> at that specific time is obtained in <term>Digits|aṅgula; a traditional unit of measure used for the magnitude of an eclipse</term>.

---

# Chapter 5: Solar Eclipses

### Verse 1—Calculating Zenith Distance and Altitude.

Calculate the position of the Sun at the <term>End of the New Moon|darśānta; the moment of conjunction</term> and then determine the <term>Ascendant|lagna; the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term>. Add the <term>Precession of the Equinoxes|ayanāṃśa</term> to the Ascendant to make it the <term>Tropical Ascendant|sāyana-lagna</term>. By subtracting 3 signs from the Tropical Ascendant, the <term>Nonagesimal|vitribha-lagna; the point of the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the ascendant</term> is found. Calculate the <term>Declination|krānti</term> of this Nonagesimal. The direction of the Nonagesimal will be the same as the direction of its declination. By combining the declination and the <term>Latitude of the Observer|akṣāṃśa</term> (adding if they are in the same direction, subtracting if different), the <term>Zenith Distance|natāṃśa</term> is found. Subtracting the Zenith Distance from 90 degrees gives the <term>Altitude|unnatāṃśa</term>.

### Verses 2, 3—Calculating Parallax in Longitude and Latitude.

Find the sine of the difference between the Tropical Sun at the moment of conjunction and the Tropical Nonagesimal. Dividing this sine by 30 gives the <term>Mean Parallax in Longitude|madhya-lambana</term>. Multiply the Mean Parallax by the sine of the altitude and divide by 120 to find the <term>Clear Parallax in Longitude|spaṣṭa-lambana</term>. The correction for parallax is as follows:

->The correction for parallax:<-

If the Tropical Nonagesimal is greater than the Tropical Sun and the time of conjunction falls in the <term>Western Hemisphere|paścim-kapāla</term>, then: Conjunction Time + Clear Parallax. If the Tropical Nonagesimal is less than the Tropical Sun and the time of conjunction falls in the <term>Eastern Hemisphere|pūrv-kapāla</term>, then: Conjunction Time - Clear Parallax.

---

<page-num>१३७</page-num>
<header>Chapter 5: Solar Eclipses</header>

Calculate the sine of the <term>Zenith Distance|natāṃśa</term> and place it in two locations. In one, divide by 10 and add the result to the second location. Then, divide that total by 8; the resulting value will be the <term>Parallax in Latitude|nati</term> in digits and minutes. Parallax in latitude is always considered to be in the Southern direction <note>This is a specific rule of this text's geographic orientation.</note>.

### Verses 4-5—Alternative Method for Calculating Parallax in Longitude.

77, 141, 188, 219, 235, 240, 236, 240, 200—these are the 9 <term>Parallax Coefficients|lambana-piṇḍa</term>.

Calculate the sine of the difference between the calculated Tropical Sun at the end of the New Moon and the Nonagesimal. Divide these degrees by 15 to find the number of completed parallax coefficients. Multiply the difference between the current and the next coefficient by the remaining degrees, divide by 15, and adjust the current or next coefficient (if the next is greater than the current, subtract; if the current is greater, add). Dividing the result by 60 gives the Mean Parallax.

Find the sine of the altitude of the Nonagesimal, multiply it by the Mean Parallax, and divide by 120 to find the Clear Parallax. The correction of this parallax to the time of conjunction follows the same rules previously mentioned.

### Verses 6-7—Calculating Duration.

The sum of the Moon and its <term>Node|pāta; refers to Rāhu</term> at the moment of conjunction is the "Moon with Node." Find the sine of this value, multiply by 3, and divide by 4 to find the <term>Latitude|śara</term>. By combining the previously calculated Parallax in Latitude and this Latitude, the <term>Clear Latitude|spaṣṭa-śara</term> is found. (If the Latitude and Parallax in Latitude are in the same direction, add them; if opposite, find the difference.)

Calculate the diameters of the Sun and Moon, add them together, and halve the result to find the <term>Sum of the Semi-diameters|bimbyogārdha</term>. Subtracting the Clear Latitude from the Sum of the Semi-diameters gives the <term>Magnitude of the Eclipse|grāsāṅgula</term> in digits.

Add the Magnitude to twice the Clear Latitude, then multiply the result by the Magnitude itself and take the square root. Multiply the root by 180 and divide by the <term>Relative Motion|gatyantara; the difference in speeds of the Sun and Moon</term> to find the <term>Half-Duration|sthityardha</term>. In a solar eclipse, there is an absence of <term>Total Immersion|vimarda; the period of total darkness</term> <note>This suggests that for the purposes of this specific calculation, the focus is on partial phases or that the duration of totality is calculated differently than in lunar eclipses.</note>.

### Verse 8—Calculating Contact and Separation.

Subtract the <term>Half-Duration|sthityardha</term> (in units of time like ghatis) from the moment of conjunction. Treat the remainder as the "Desired Time" and use it to calculate the position of the Sun and the Ascendant. Convert the Sun and Ascendant into the Tropical Sun and...

<vocab>solar eclipse chapter, desired magnitude, zenith distance, altitude, parallax in longitude, parallax in latitude, nonagesimal, sum of semi-diameters, half-duration, Karanakutuhala</vocab>

<summary>This page details the complex mathematical procedures for calculating a solar eclipse, focusing on determining the nonagesimal point and accounting for parallax in both longitude and latitude. It also provides formulas for finding the magnitude of the eclipse and the half-duration of the event.</summary>
<keywords>solar eclipse, parallax, lambana, nati, nonagesimal, vitribha lagna, celestial latitude, gatyantara, sthityardha, Karanakutuhala</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating solar eclipses, specifically focusing on parallax adjustments for the start and end of the eclipse, before transitioning into Chapter 6, which deals with the heliacal rising, setting, and retrograde motions of the five planets.</meta>

<page-num>१३८</page-num>
<header>In the Karanakutuhala</header>

By determining the <term>Nonagesimal|vintribhalagna; the point on the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the ascendant</term> and using it to calculate the <term>Parallax|lambana</term>, one obtains the parallax at the start of the eclipse. In the same manner, one should calculate the initial <term>Latitude-Parallax|nati</term> and the <term>Celestial Latitude|śara</term>.

By adding the <term>Half-Duration|sthityarddha; half the total time of the eclipse</term> to the <term>End of the New Moon|darśānta</term> and treating that as the "desired time," one should calculate the <term>Tropical Sun|sāyana-sūrya</term> and the Nonagesimal. Calculating the parallax from these values gives the <term>Parallax of Emergence|maukṣika-lambana</term>, along with the latitude-parallax and celestial latitude at the time of emergence.

Using these initial and emergence values for parallax, latitude-parallax, and celestial latitude, one then calculates the precise time of <term>Contact|sparśa; the beginning of the eclipse</term> and <term>Separation|mokṣa; the end of the eclipse</term>.

### Verse 6—Identifying the Color of the Sun and Moon during an Eclipse.

In a solar eclipse, if the <term>Magnitude|grāsamāna</term> is equal to or less than 1/12th of the Sun’s disk, it is not visible to the eye. Similarly, in a lunar eclipse, if the magnitude is equal to or less than 1/16th of the Moon’s disk, it is also not visible.

During a minor eclipse, the Moon appears smoke-colored; in a half-eclipse, it appears black; and during a total eclipse, it appears a reddish-brown color. In either a partial or total solar eclipse, the Sun always appears black.

---

# Chapter 6: The Rising and Setting of Planets

### Verses 1, 2—Calculating the Rising and Setting of Jupiter.

The <term>Elapsed Years|gatābda</term> (for the desired Shaka year 1105) should be recorded in two places. From the first, calculate the <term>Day-Count|ahargaṇa</term>. In the second, divide the years by 10 and add the resulting quotient to the Day-Count. From this sum, subtract 105 and then divide by 399. If the remainder is 15, Jupiter is <term>Rising|udaya; its first appearance in the sky after being obscured by the Sun</term>. If the remainder is greater than 384, it is <term>Setting|asta; its disappearance as it nears the Sun</term>.

Take the <term>Rising Time|svodayamāna</term> of the sign in which the <term>True Sun|spaṣṭa sūrya</term> is located, adjust it by 300, multiply the remainder by 15, and divide by the Rising Time. The resulting days and minutes should be added to the time of Jupiter’s rising.

### Verses 3, 4—Calculating the Rising and Setting of Venus.

Subtract 115 from the Day-Count and add 1/16th of the Elapsed Years. Divide the sum by 584. If the remainder is greater than 36, it rises in the West; if greater than 287,

<column-break/>

<page-num>१३९</page-num>
<header>Chapter 6: On Rising and Setting</header>

it sets in the West; if greater than 297, it rises in the East; and if greater than 548, it sets in the East.

Take the Rising Time of the sign occupied by the Sun and adjust it by 300. If Venus is setting in the West, multiply by 35; if setting in the East, multiply by 36. Divide by the Rising Time to find the remaining days and minutes. This adjustment provides the final time for the rising or setting of Venus.

### Verse 5—Knowledge of Retrograde Motion from the Anomaly.

The <term>Five Planets|pañcatārā graha; Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn</term> become <term>Retrograde|vakrī; appearing to move backward in the sky</term> and <term>Direct|mārgī; resuming forward motion</term> according to the degrees of their second <term>Anomaly|śīghrakendra</term>. The degree at which they begin retrograde motion, when subtracted from 360, gives the degree at which they return to direct motion. For example:

| Planet | Degrees (Retrograde) | Motion | Degrees (Direct) | Motion |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Mars | 163 | Retrograde | 197 | Direct |
| Mercury | 145 | Retrograde | 215 | Direct |
| Jupiter | 125 | Retrograde | 235 | Direct |
| Venus | 167 | Retrograde | 193 | Direct |
| Saturn | 113 | Retrograde | 247 | Direct |

### Verse 6—Rising and Setting Times for Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.

<note>Though the heading mentions Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn, the verse text continues defining visibility limits for Mercury and Venus.</note>
If Mercury's second anomaly is 50 degrees and Venus's is 24, they rise in the West. If Mercury is at 155 and Venus is at 177, they set in the West. If Mercury is at 205 and Venus is at 183, they rise in the East. At 310 for Mercury and 336 for Venus, they set in the West <note>original text likely means East for the final setting</note>.

### Verse 8—Calculating the Duration of Retrograde Motion.

Determine the degrees over which the retrograde motion starts and ends. Convert these degrees into minutes of arc and divide by the speed of the anomaly; this will give the days and minutes of the retrograde period. The duration of direct motion can be calculated in the same way.

### Verse 9—Degrees of Visibility and Nodes of the Planets.

According to the ancient teachers, the <term>Degrees of Visibility|kālāṃśa; the minimum angular distance from the Sun required for a planet to be visible</term> for the planets starting from the Moon are as follows: Moon 12, Mars 17, Mercury 13, Jupiter 11, Venus 9, and Saturn 15. For the planet's...

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical procedures for planetary visibility and motion. Having discussed the solar eclipse on the previous page, the text now shifts to calculating the specific visibility conditions of the five star-planets (Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn) and the rising of the star Canopus.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>140 In the Karanakutuhala</header>

When a planet is in <term>Retrograde|vakrī; appearing to move backward in the sky</term>, subtract 1 from its <term>Time-Degrees|kālāṃśa; the degrees of solar separation required for a planet to become visible</term> to find the retrograde time-degrees. For example: Mars is 16, Mercury is 12, Jupiter is 10, and so on.

The <term>Nodes|pāta; the points where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic</term> for Mars and the other planets are as follows: Mars is 11 signs and 8 degrees, Mercury is 11 signs and 9 degrees, Jupiter is 9 signs and 8 degrees, Venus is 10 signs and 0 degrees, and Saturn is 8 signs and 17 degrees. To find the "True Nodes," apply a reverse correction using the planet's <term>Equation of Center|śīghraphala</term>. (If the equation of center is positive, subtract it from the node; if negative, add it). For Mercury and Venus, the true nodes are found by applying their respective <term>Mandaphala|equation of center based on eccentricity</term> corrections.

### Verse 10—Calculating the Celestial Latitude (Shara) of Mars and others.

The values for the <term>Celestial Latitude|vikṣepa or śara; the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</term> in minutes for the five planets are as follows: Mars is 110 minutes, Mercury is 152, Jupiter is 76, Venus is 136, and Saturn is 130.

Add the planet's position to its node. (For Mercury and Venus, add the node to their <term>Apex of Quick Motion|śīghrocca</term>). Find the sine of this sum. Multiply this sine by the specific latitude constant mentioned above and divide by the <term>Variable Hypotenuse|śīghrakarṇa</term> to get the latitude in minutes. Dividing the result in minutes by 3 gives the latitude in <term>Digits|aṅgula; a traditional unit of measure</term>.

### Verse 11, 13—Visibility Correction and Rising/Setting Procedures.

Based on the planet's <term>Anomaly of Speed|śīghrakendrāṃśa</term>, if the planet’s heliacal rising or setting occurs in the East, subtract 3 signs (90 degrees); if in the West, add 3 signs. Calculate the <term>Declination|krānti</term> of this adjusted position. Find the <term>Zenith Distance|natāṃśa</term> by adding the declination and the <term>Terrestrial Latitude|akṣāṃśa</term> if they are in the same direction, or taking their difference if they are in opposite directions. Subtract the zenith distance from 90 to find the <term>Altitude|unnatāṃśa</term>. Calculate the sines for both the zenith distance and the altitude separately. 

Apply the planet's latitude (in digits) to its position. Multiply this by the sine of the zenith distance, then multiply by three, and divide by the sine of the altitude. Apply the resulting correction to the planet's position. If it is an Eastern rising/setting and the latitude and zenith distance are in the same direction, add the correction; if opposite, subtract. For a Western rising/setting, if the latitude and zenith distance are in the same direction, subtract; if opposite, add. After these corrections, the result is the "Visible Planet."

To find the time, take the smaller value between the "Visible Planet" and the Sun as the Sun, and the larger value as the <term>Ascendant|lagna</term>. Calculate the difference in time-units (as per Chapter 3, Verses 5-6).

<column-break/>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>Chapter 7: Elevation of the Horns — 141</header>

If it is a Western rising or setting, add 6 signs (180 degrees) to both the Sun and the Ascendant before calculating the time difference. Multiplying this time difference by 6 gives the "Calculated Time-Degrees." If the Calculated Time-Degrees are greater than the "Standard Time-Degrees" mentioned earlier, the planet's rising has already occurred or its setting is yet to come. If the Calculated Time-Degrees are less than the standard, the rising is yet to come or the setting has already occurred.

Multiply the difference between the calculated and standard time-degrees by 300 and divide by the <term>Rising Period|rāśyudayamāna</term> of the <term>Tropical Sun|sāyana-sūrya</term>. For Western rising/setting, add 6 signs to the tropical Sun before using the rising period. Divide this result by the difference in daily motion between the planet and the Sun to find the number of days until or since the rising/setting. If the planet is retrograde, divide by the sum of their daily motions instead.

### Verse 14—Special Notation.

For a planet in the East (higher longitude than the Sun) or the West (lower longitude), understand the calculated days in reverse: if the calculation indicates the event is "to come," consider it "past," and vice versa. Specifically, if the Calculated Time-Degrees exceed the standard ones, treat the resulting days with this reversed logic.

### Verse 15—Rising and Setting of the star Canopus (Agastya).

When the Sun reaches a degree equal to 98 plus eight times the <term>Equinoctial Noon Shadow|palabhā; the shadow of a 12-digit gnomon at noon on the equinox</term>, <note>original: "अष्टगुणित पलभा को ९८ अंश में जोड़ने पर"</note> Canopus rises. When the Sun reaches a degree equal to 78 minus eight times the equinoctial noon shadow, <note>original: "अष्टगुणित पलभा को ७८ में घटाने पर"</note> Canopus sets.

---

->**Chapter 7: Elevation of the Moon’s Horns**<-

### Verse 1, 2—Calculating Deflection (Valana).

Calculate the <term>Base|bhuja</term> of the Tropical Moon at the time of setting and find its declination. Adjust this declination using the Moon's celestial latitude (add if same direction, subtract if different). Add 6 signs to the Sun, find its base, and calculate its declination. Subtract the Moon's declination from the Sun's declination. Subtract the Sun’s position from the Moon’s, find the sine of that difference, multiply by the terrestrial latitude, and divide by 120. Take this result...

<vocab>kālāṃśa (time-degrees), vakrī (retrograde), bhauma (Mars), budha (Mercury), guru (Jupiter), śukra (Venus), śani (Saturn), pāta (node), śīghraphala (equation of center), vikṣepa/śara (celestial latitude), drik-karma (visibility correction), udayasta (rising and setting), iṣṭa-kālāṃśa (calculated time-degrees), agastya (Canopus), palabhā (equinoctial shadow), śṛṅgonnati (elevation of the moon's horns), valana (deflection)</vocab>

<summary>This page details the complex mathematical steps for calculating when planets become visible or hidden by the Sun's glare and provides specific formulas for the heliacal rising of the star Canopus. It then begins Chapter 7, which explains how to calculate the orientation and "horns" of the crescent moon.</summary>

This section transitions from the geometry of the lunar crescent to the complex calculations of planetary conjunctions and the special solar-lunar alignments known as Patas.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>

-># In the Karanakutuhala<-
->## Chapter 9: On the Aspect of the Nodes<-

One should perform the correction using the <term>Declination|krānti; the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator</term> of the Sun. (If the directions are the same, add them; if the directions are different, take the difference). This will result in the "Total Corrected Declination."

Subtract the Sun's position from the Moon's. Multiply the resulting <term>Base-Sine|bhujā</term> in signs and degrees by 5. Convert the result into minutes of arc and divide the Total Corrected Declination (also in minutes) by this value. The result is the <term>Valana|deflection; the rotation of the celestial sphere relative to the local horizon</term>. The direction of this deflection in <term>Angulas|traditional units of measurement, approximately a finger-breadth</term> will be the same as the direction of the Total Corrected Declination.

## Verses 2, 2 ½ — On the Illuminated and Non-Illuminated Portions.

Subtract the Sun's position from the Moon's and find the <term>Cosine|koṭi</term> of the remainder. Divide this cosine by 15; this is the divisor. Divide 36 by this divisor and set the resulting quotient in two places. In the first place, subtract the divisor from the quotient and halve the remainder to find the <term>Vibhā|the radius used to draw the inner curve of the crescent</term>. In the second place, add the divisor to the quotient and halve the sum to find the <term>Svabhā|the radius used to draw the outer curve of the crescent</term>.

## Verses 3, 4 — On Drawing the Projection.

On a level floor or a sheet of paper, draw a circle representing the Moon's disk with a diameter of six angulas. Mark the cardinal directions: East, West, North, and South. Mark the <term>Valana-Angulas|deflection measurements</term> according to their directions. During the <term>Shukla-paksha|waxing fortnight</term>, mark them toward the East; during the <term>Krishna-paksha|waning fortnight</term>, mark them toward the West. Using the center of the Moon’s disk as a reference, use the endpoints of the *Vibhā* and the *Svabhā* radii to draw the circles that form the shape of the crescent moon. The "horns" (points) of the crescent will appear in the direction opposite to the deflection.

# Chapter 8: On Planetary Conjunctions

## Verses 1, 1 ½ — Calculating the Apparent Disks of Mars and the other Planets.

The angular and linear measurements of the <term>Apparent Disks|bimba</term> of the five planets—Mars and the others—are as follows:

| Planet | Mars | Mercury | Jupiter | Venus | Saturn |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Disk Measurement** | | | | | |
| **In Minutes of Arc** | 5 | 6 | 7 | 9 | 5 |
| **In Yojanas** | 1885 | 279 | 16649 | 1110 | 2955 |

<note>Yojana is an ancient unit of distance, roughly 8-15 kilometers depending on the era.</note>

Multiply the angular disk measurement of the planet by the difference between the <term>Radius|trijyā; usually 120 or 3438 depending on the system</term> and the <term>Hypotenuse of Quick Motion|śīghrakarṇa</term>. Divide the product by three times the constant known as *Parākhya*. 

<column-break/>

Apply this result as an addition or subtraction to the angular disk measurement, then divide by 3 to obtain the disk measurement in angulas.

If the Hypotenuse of Quick Motion is greater than the Radius (120), subtract the result from the angular disk. If the Hypotenuse is less than the Radius, add the result to the angular disk.

## Verses 2, 3 — Determining the Time of Conjunction.

A <term>Conjunction|yuti</term> has already passed if the faster-moving planet has a higher longitude and the slower-moving planet has a lower longitude. Alternatively, if both are in <term>Retrograde|vakrī; appearing to move backward</term>, the conjunction has passed if the faster planet has a lower longitude and the slower has a higher longitude. In any other situation, the conjunction is "upcoming."

**Calculating the Conjunction:** If both planets are moving in the same direction (both direct or both retrograde), find the difference between their longitudes in minutes of arc. Divide this by the difference in their daily motions. The result is the number of days until or since the conjunction. If one planet is retrograde and the other is <term>Direct|mārgī</term>, divide the longitude difference by the *sum* of their daily motions to find the time.

One must also calculate the <term>Celestial Latitude|śara</term> of the planets at the time of conjunction. The planet's relative position depends on the direction of this latitude. If both have North latitudes, the planet with the greater latitude is to the North, and the one with the smaller latitude is to the South. If both have South latitudes, the one with the greater latitude is to the South, and the one with the smaller latitude is to the North.

If both planets have latitudes in the same direction, find the difference between them; if they are in different directions, find the sum. This determines the actual distance between the centers of the two planetary disks.

If this distance is less than the sum of the two planets' radii, the disks will overlap or touch <note>original: "bheda"; meaning a piercing or occultation</note>.

***

# Chapter 9: On the Aspect of the Nodes

## Verse 1 — On the Possibility and Timing of a Node Alignment.

If the sum of the <term>Tropical Sun|sāyana sūrya</term> and the <term>Tropical Moon|sāyana candra</term> <note>Sāyana refers to positions adjusted for the precession of the equinoxes</note> is equal to 6 signs (180°), it is the yoga called <term>Vyatipāta</term>. If the sum is equal to 12 signs (360°), it is the yoga called <term>Vaidhṛti</term>. If the sum of the Sun and Moon is less than 6 or 12 signs, the <term>Pāta|alignment</term> is upcoming; if it is greater, the alignment has already passed.

<summary>This page details the geometry of drawing the lunar crescent and the mathematical procedures for calculating planetary conjunctions, disk sizes, and the specific solar-lunar alignments known as Vyatipata and Vaidhriti.</summary>
<keywords>Valana, crescent moon, planetary conjunction, apparent disk, Vyatipata, Vaidhriti, celestial latitude, Karanakutuhala</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions from Chapter 9 of the Karanakutuhala, focusing on the calculation of astronomical alignments (Patas) and transitions into Chapter 10, which discusses the likelihood of conjunctions such as eclipses.</meta>

### Verses 2, 3—Determining if an Aspect is Past or Future.

At the time of an aspect (<term>Pata|a specific astronomical alignment where the declinations of the Sun and Moon are equal, often considered inauspicious in traditional astrology</term>), one should calculate the positions of the Sun and Moon, the Pata itself, and the celestial latitude using the <term>Latitude-segment|shara-khanda; a mathematical increment used to calculate the Moon's distance from the ecliptic</term>. The determination of whether the alignment is already past or yet to come is as follows:

->If the tropical Moon and the Moon-with-node are in odd quadrants and in the same hemisphere = The Pata is Past<-
->If they are in even quadrants and in different hemispheres = The Pata is Past<-
->If they are in odd quadrants and in different hemispheres = The Pata is Future<-
->If they are in even quadrants and in the same hemisphere = The Pata is Future<-

### Verse 3½—Positive and Negative Values of Declination and Latitude Segments.

By arranging the <term>Declination-segment|krānti-khanda; increments of angular distance from the celestial equator</term> (as described in Chapter 3, verses 13-14) four times in direct and reverse order, there will be 24 segments in total. The first 6 segments are positive, the second 6 are negative, the third 6 are positive, and the fourth 6 are negative.

Similarly, the latitude segments (from the Lunar Eclipse section, verse 6) are arranged four times in direct and reverse order to create 24 segments. Of these, the first 6 are positive, the second 6 are negative, the third 6 are positive, and the fourth 6 are negative.

### Verse 4—Calculating the Elapsed Segments.

Convert the position of the Moon-with-node into degrees and divide by 15; the quotient represents the number of "elapsed" latitude segments, and the remainder represents the degrees of the current "traversable" segment. Similarly, convert the tropical Moon's position into degrees and divide by 15; the quotient gives the elapsed declination segments, and the remainder gives the degrees of the current declination segment.

### Verses 5, 6, 7—Calculating the Past and Future Aspect.

Using the previously mentioned latitude and declination segments: if the aspect is "past" and the Moon-with-node is in an even quadrant, use the segments preceding the current one. If it is in an odd quadrant, use the subsequent segments.

Take the declination value corresponding to the Moon's <term>Ayana|the northward or southward course of a celestial body</term> and the latitude value corresponding to the direction of the Moon-with-node. Apply a <term>Correction|saṃskāra; a mathematical adjustment</term> (add if they are in the same direction, subtract if they are in different directions). Then, further adjust the result by one-thirteenth of the current segment value to find the "True Aspect" (<term>Spashta Pata</term>).

### Verses 8, 9, 10—Calculating the Midpoint of the Aspect.

Multiply the Moon's latitude by 15 and subtract the latitude segment. The number of segments that can be fully subtracted are the "pure" segments; the portion that cannot be subtracted is the "impure" segment. For the remainder,

<column-break/>

Chapter 10: On the Possibility of Conjunctions — 145

divide by the current traversable segment. Add the resulting degrees and minutes to the sum of the pure segments. Multiply this by 60 and divide by the Moon's daily motion to find the time in days and minutes.

If the aspect is "past," the midpoint occurred this many days before the sum of the Sun and Moon's longitudes reached 12 signs (360 degrees). If the aspect is "future," the midpoint will occur this many days afterward.

### Verse 11—Calculating the Duration.

Divide the constant 2203 by the "impure" latitude segment. Subtracting and adding this quotient from the midpoint of the aspect provides the time of contact (start) and the time of release (end), respectively. The interval between contact and release is the <term>Sthiti-ghatika|the duration of the alignment measured in units of 24 minutes</term>.

### Verses 12, 13—Analysis of Pure Segments.

Multiply the latitude derived from the latitude segments by 15. If the product is greater than 480, an aspect (<term>Pata</term>) is impossible. If the product is less than 480, the aspect is possible.

### Verse 14—Possibility of an Aspect and Duration Calculation.

If an aspect is determined to be possible, subtract that latitude value from 48, multiply by 9, and divide by the final latitude segment. Adding and subtracting this quotient from the midpoint of the aspect results in the times of release and contact, respectively.

### Verse 15—On the Equality of Declination.

If the difference between the declinations of the Sun and Moon is less than half the sum of their <term>Angular diameters|bimba; the visual size of the discs of the Sun and Moon</term>, then <term>Kranti-samya|equality of declination</term> occurs. This is the exact moment when the state of the aspect exists.

---

## Chapter 10: On the Possibility of Conjunctions

### Verse 1—Calculating Latitude.

Multiply the <term>Month-count|Masagana; the number of months elapsed since the start of the epoch</term> by 2 and divide by 3. To the resulting degrees and minutes, add 272. Then, add one-twentieth of the month-count to this total.

Take the <term>Sun-time correction|Arka-ghati-phala</term>, divide it by 5, and add or subtract the resulting degrees and minutes. Finally, divide by 30 to convert the total into zodiacal signs.

<summary>This page outlines the mathematical procedures for determining the exact timing, duration, and possibility of "Patas" (alignments of equal declination) and begins Chapter 10's rules for calculating celestial positions for conjunctions.</summary>
<keywords>Pata, Sun and Moon, latitude segments, tropical Moon, Moon with node, declination segments, calculation of duration, Chapter on Conjunctions, solar-time correction, equality of declination, angular diameter, Karanakutuhala</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous calculations of planetary positions, moving from the mathematical determination of celestial latitude (Shara) to the practical rules for predicting whether an eclipse will occur and, subsequently, the astrological methods for forecasting rainfall in Chapter 11.</meta>

**In the Karanakutuhala**

Write down the number of months in the sign position and subtract by 12 to determine the zodiac sign. From the resulting sign, degrees, minutes, and seconds, calculate the <term>Sine|Bhuja; the angular distance used here to find the planet's position relative to the nearest cardinal point</term>. (If this is for a solar eclipse, add 15 degrees before calculating the Sine). By adding half of the Sine, the celestial latitude (<term>Shara|the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the ecliptic</term>) will be determined in digits.

The direction of the Sine calculation determines the direction of the celestial latitude.

### Verse 2—Calculation of Zenith Distance.

Calculate the <term>Zenith Distance|Nata; the angular distance of a celestial body from the zenith</term> at the moment of the new moon. Divide this value by 4 to find the signs. Multiply the remainder by 30 and divide by 4 to find the degrees. Multiply that remainder by 60 and divide by 4 for the minutes, and once more for the seconds. Apply this value to the position of the Sun at the end of the new moon (adding if it is in the west, subtracting if in the east). Add the <term>Precession of the Equinoxes|Ayanamsha</term> and calculate the Sine. Subtract this from 7 signs, multiply by the Sine, and double the result to find the declination (<term>Kranti|the angular distance of the Sun north or south of the celestial equator</term>) in degrees.

By processing the declination and the latitude (adding if in the same direction, subtracting if different), calculate the zenith degrees and divide by 4 to find the <term>Parallax in Latitude|Nati; the shift in the Moon's apparent position due to the observer's location on Earth</term>. By combining the Parallax in Latitude with the previously calculated latitude (adding if in the same direction, subtracting if different), the corrected celestial latitude is found.

### Verse 3—On the Possibility of an Eclipse.

If the Moon’s celestial latitude is less than 19 (half the sum of the diameters of the Sun/Moon and the Earth's shadow), a lunar eclipse is possible. If the Sun’s latitude is less than 11, a solar eclipse is possible. The detailed calculations for lunar and solar eclipses follow the methods previously described.

Subtract the <term>Epochal Constant|Kshepa</term> from the Sun’s position, add them, double the result, add 2, and divide by seven. The remainder determines the <term>Lord of the Node|Parvesha; the deity presiding over that specific eclipse period</term>. For example:

| | | | | | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Remainder: | Brahma | 2 | Remainder: | Moon |
| 3 | ” | Indra | 4 | ” | Kubera |
| 5 | ” | Varuna | 6 | ” | Agni |
| 7 | ” | Yama | | | |

<column-break/>

# Chapter 11: On Clouds (Rainfall)

Verses 1, 2, 3—The <term>Cloud Sun|Neeradarka; a mathematical point used specifically to predict meteorological events</term> is calculated from the position of the Sun at the exact moment of the new moon (the solar eclipse conjunction).

Subtract 1 sign and 14 degrees from the Sun’s true position. Take the remainder and "purify the circle" (subtract from 12 signs) and convert it into minutes. Square this number. Double the squared number and divide it by 21,600 (the total minutes in a circle). The result is in minutes. Divide by 60 for degrees and by 30 for signs; this value is called the <term>Abundant|Adhya</term>.

Place this "Abundant" value in two locations. To the first, add 13 degrees and add the result to the Sun's position at the new moon. Subtract the Sun’s position at sunrise from this sum. Add the "Abundant" value from the second location to the remainder to find the corrected position.

Verses 4, 5, 6—If the Cloud Sun is located in the <term>Navamsha|the ninth division of a zodiac sign, used to fine-tune astrological predictions</term> of the Sun or Mars, there will be no rain (clouds fail). If it is in the Navamsha of Saturn or Mercury, there will be scant or light rain. If it is in the Navamsha of the Moon or Venus, there will be full, heavy rain. If the Cloud Sun is in the Navamsha of Jupiter, there will be much wind but little rain.

One should determine the rainfall based on the ruler of the specific ninth-part (Navamsha) in which the Cloud Sun is positioned according to the Navamsha chart.

->The *Shivamati* Hindi commentary on the *Karanakutuhala*<-
->by Satyendra Mishra is here concluded.<-

### Krishnadas Sanskrit Series-129
## Composed by the Illustrious Bhaskaracharya <note>original: "Śrī-Bhāskara-ācārya-viracitam." # The Curiosity of Astronomical Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a specific genre of astronomical manual designed for practical use. Unlike more theoretical works, it uses a simplified epoch (starting date)—in this case, 1183 CE—to help local astronomers easily calculate planetary positions, lunar phases, and eclipses for the ritual calendar.</note>

->Featuring the Sanskrit commentaries: ‘Moonlight for the Lotus of Calculators’ <note>original: "Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī" by Sumatiharsha Gani.</note> and ‘The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning’ <note>original: "Vāsanā-vibhūṣaṇam." In Indian astronomy, a <term>vāsanā</term> is a commentary that provides the geometric or algebraic proof behind a rule.</note><-

## Krishnadas Sanskrit Series-129
### Composed by the Illustrious Bhaskaracharya <note>original: "Śrī-Bhāskara-ācārya-viracitam." Bhaskaracharya (1114–1185 CE), also known as Bhaskara II, was a preeminent mathematician and astronomer of medieval India who led the famous observatory at Ujjain.</note>

# The Curiosity of Astronomical Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇa-kutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a practical astronomical manual. Unlike a theoretical treatise, it uses a simplified "epoch" (a recent starting date) to allow for easier daily calculations of planetary positions and eclipses.</note>

->Including the Sanskrit commentaries: ‘Moonlight for the Lotus of Astronomers’ and ‘The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning’<- 
<note>original: "Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī" and "Vāsanā-vibhūṣaṇam." In Indian astronomy, <term>vāsanā</term> refers to the "demonstration" or proof behind a mathematical rule, making the abstract formulas understandable.</note>
->Along with the ‘Jewel of Shiva’ Hindi Commentary<-
<note>original: "Śivamanī" (or "Śivamatī"). Traditional commentaries are often named poetically; here, the Hindi commentary is presented as a "jewel" or "thought" dedicated to Lord Shiva.</note>
->Hindi Commentator<-

# The Curiosity of Astronomical Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇa-kutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a practical astronomical manual. # The Curiosity of Astronomical Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇa-kutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a practical astronomical manual. Unlike a purely theoretical treatise (<term>siddhanta</term>), a karana uses a recent "epoch" or starting date to simplify the complex mathematics required for everyday tasks like creating calendars, predicting eclipses, and tracking planetary movements.</note>

->With the ‘Shivamati’ Hindi Commentary<-

# The Curiosity of Astronomical Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇa-kutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a practical astronomical manual. Unlike a purely theoretical treatise (<term>siddhanta</term>), a karana uses a recent "epoch" or starting date to simplify the complex mathematics required for everyday tasks like creating calendars, predicting eclipses, and tracking planetary movements. This specific work was written in 1183 CE.</note>

->Accompanied by the 'Moonlight for the Lotus of Astronomers' and 'The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning' Sanskrit [commentaries]<-
->And the 'Nectar of Shiva' Hindi Commentary<-
<note>original: "Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī," "Vāsanā-vibhūṣaṇam," and "Śivamṛtī." In Sanskrit literary tradition, titles are often metaphorical; here, the "Moonlight" helps the "Lotus" (the astronomer) to bloom. <term>Vāsanā</term> refers to the "demonstration" or proof that explains the underlying logic of mathematical formulas. "Śivamṛtī" translates to "The Nectar of Shiva," implying the commentary provides life-giving clarity to the text.</note>

->Hindi Commentator<-
->**Dr. Satyendra Mishra**<-

->### Krishnadas Sanskrit Series—129<-

->## Composed by the Illustrious Bhaskaracharya<-
<note>original: "Śrī-Bhāskara-ācārya-viracitam." Bhaskaracharya (1114–1185 CE), also known as Bhaskara II, was the preeminent mathematician and astronomer of medieval India. He served as the head of the astronomical observatory at Ujjain.</note>

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." The title translates to "The Curiosity of Calculation" or "The Joy of Astronomical Computation." A <term>karana</term> is a practical astronomical manual used to simplify complex calculations for daily needs like calendars and planetary positions.</note>

### With the Sanskrit commentaries 'Moonlight for the Lotus of Astronomers' and 'The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning'—
<note>original: "Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī" and "Vāsanā-vibhūṣaṇam." Indian scholarly tradition often uses poetic titles. Here, the commentary is the "Moonlight" that makes the "Lotus" (the astronomer) bloom. <term>Vāsanā</term> refers to the logical and mathematical proof behind an astronomical rule.</note>
### and the 'Auspicious Wisdom' Hindi commentary
<note>original: "Śivamayi." Literally "Consisting of Shiva" or "Auspicious," suggesting the commentary is both sacred and intellectually sound.</note>

### Hindi Commentator
## **Dr. Satyendra Mishra**

### Author of the Foreword
## **Prof. Dr. Ramchandra Pandey**
### B. H. U., Varanasi

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a specific type of astronomical treatise designed for practical use, simplifying complex planetary calculations for making calendars and predicting eclipses. "Kutuhala" means curiosity, wonder, or joy.</note>

### With the Sanskrit commentaries ‘Moonlight for the Lotus of Mathematicians’ and ‘The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning’—
<note>original: "Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī" and "Vāsanā-vibhūṣaṇam." Indian titles are often poetic; here, the "Moonlight" (the commentary) causes the "Lotus" (the mathematician) to bloom. <term>Vasana</term> refers to the "demonstration" or underlying logic of a mathematical proof.</note>

### and the ‘Auspicious Wisdom’ Hindi commentary
<note>original: "Śivamati." This suggests a commentary that is both intellectually sound and spiritually favorable.</note>

### Krishnadas Sanskrit Series—129
### Composed by the Illustrious Bhaskaracharya
<note>original: "Śrī-Bhāskara-ācārya-viracitam." Bhaskaracharya (1114–1185 CE), also known as Bhaskara II, was one of medieval India's most renowned mathematicians and astronomers. He headed the observatory at Ujjain, the Greenwich of ancient Indian astronomy.</note>

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a practical astronomical manual used by scholars to calculate planetary positions and create calendars without the complexity of larger theoretical treatises. "Kutuhala" means curiosity, wonder, or joy—suggesting that these calculations are a source of intellectual delight.</note>

## With the Sanskrit commentaries ‘Moonlight for the Lotus of Mathematicians’ and ‘The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning’—
<note>original: "Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī" and "Vāsanā-vibhūṣaṇam." Indian titles are often poetic: "Gaṇaka" (mathematician), "Kumuda" (white lotus), and "Kaumudī" (moonlight). Just as moonlight causes the lotus to bloom, this commentary clarifies the text for the scholar. <term>Vasana</term> refers to the "demonstration" or proof of why a mathematical rule works.</note>

## And the ‘Auspicious Intellect’ Hindi Commentary
<note>original: "Shivamati." This modern commentary is named to evoke "Shiva" (the auspicious one) and "Mati" (intellect/understanding), implying the work is both helpful and intellectually sound.</note>

### Krishnadas Sanskrit Series—129
## Composed by the Illustrious Bhaskaracharya
<note>original: "Śrī-Bhāskara-ācārya-viracitam." Bhaskaracharya (1114–1185 CE), also known as Bhaskara II, was the preeminent mathematician and astronomer of medieval India. He served as the head of the astronomical observatory at Ujjain, the historic center of Indian mathematical science.</note>

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a specialized type of astronomical treatise designed for practical use, allowing practitioners to calculate planetary positions and calendar data for a specific era without the heavy burden of full theoretical proofs. "Kutuhala" translates to curiosity or wonder, suggesting the joy found in mathematical precision.</note>

->With the Sanskrit commentaries ‘Moonlight for the Lotus of Mathematicians’ and ‘The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning’—<-
<note>original: "Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī" and "Vāsanā-vibhūṣaṇam." Indian scholarly titles are frequently metaphorical. "Gaṇaka" (mathematician) is compared to a "Kumuda" (white lotus), which "Kaumudī" (moonlight) causes to bloom. <term>Vasana</term> refers to the "demonstration" or "exposition" that provides the logic behind mathematical rules.</note>

->and the ‘Auspicious Intellect’ Hindi commentary<-
<note>original: "Śivamatī." This modern Hindi commentary is titled to suggest that the knowledge provided is both "Shiva" (auspicious/beneficial) and an aid to the "Mati" (intellect).</note>

->Hindi Commentator<-
->**Dr. Satyendra Mishra**<-

->Author of the Foreword<-
->**Prof. Dr. Ramchandra Pandey**<-
->B. H. U., Varanasi<-

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a specialized type of astronomical treatise designed for practical use, allowing practitioners to calculate planetary positions and calendar data for a specific era without the heavy burden of full theoretical proofs. "Kutuhala" translates to curiosity, wonder, or interest, suggesting the intellectual joy found in mathematical precision.</note>

->With the Sanskrit commentaries ‘Moonlight for the Lotus of Mathematicians’ and ‘The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning’—<-
<note>original: "Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī" and "Vāsanā-vibhūṣaṇam." Indian scholarly titles are frequently metaphorical. "Gaṇaka" (mathematician) is compared to a "Kumuda" (white lotus), which "Kaumudī" (moonlight) causes to bloom. <term>Vasana</term> refers to the "demonstration" or "exposition" that provides the underlying logic or rationale behind mathematical rules.</note>

->and the ‘Auspicious Intellect’ Hindi commentary<-
<note>original: "Śivamatī." This modern Hindi commentary is titled to suggest that the knowledge provided is both "Shiva" (auspicious and beneficial) and an aid to the "Mati" (intellect).</note>

->Hindi Commentator<-
## **Dr. Satyendra Mishra**

->Author of the Foreword<-
## **Prof. Dr. Ramchandra Pandey**
->B.H.U., Varanasi<-
<note>B.H.U. refers to Banaras Hindu University, one of India's most prestigious centers for traditional and modern learning.</note>

---

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." ### Krishnadas Sanskrit Series—129
## Composed by the Illustrious **Bhaskaracharya**
<note>original: "Śrī-Bhāskara-ācārya-viracitam." Bhaskaracharya (1114–1185 CE), also known as Bhaskara II, was a legendary Indian mathematician and astronomer who led the astronomical observatory at Ujjain.</note>

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a practical astronomical manual used to calculate planetary positions for a specific date (the "epoch") without the exhaustive computations required by a full theoretical treatise (<term>siddhanta</term>). "Kutuhalam" means curiosity, wonder, or interest.</note>

->With the Sanskrit commentaries 'Moonlight for the Lotus of Mathematicians' and 'The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning'—<-
<note>original: "Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī" and "Vāsanā-vibhūṣaṇam." Indian titles are often poetic: a <term>ganaka</term> is a calculator or astronomer, and <term>vasana</term> refers to the mathematical proof or rationalization of a rule.</note>
->Together with the 'Shivamati' Hindi Commentary<-
<note>original: "Śivamatī-hindī-ṭīkā-yutam." "Shivamati" literally means "the mind of Shiva" or "Auspicious Thought," the title given to this specific modern Hindi explanation.</note>

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a practical astronomical manual used by calendar-makers to calculate planetary positions and eclipses for a specific era without needing complex long-term tables. <term>Kutuhalam</term> translates to "delight" or "curiosity."</note>

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." A <term>karana</term> is a practical astronomical handbook designed to simplify the calculation of planetary positions for a specific starting date, rather than using long-term cycles. <term>Kutuhalam</term> translates to "curiosity" or "joy."</note>

## With the Sanskrit Commentaries ‘Moonlight for the Lotus of Mathematicians’ and ‘The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning’—

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." In Indian astronomy, a <term>karana</term> is a practical manual used to calculate planetary positions and astronomical events for a specific epoch (starting date). The word <term>kutuhalam</term> translates to "curiosity," "joy," or "wonder," suggesting the delight found in mathematical precision.</note>

->With the Sanskrit commentaries 'Moonlight for the Lotus of Astronomers' and 'The Ornament of Explanatory Logic'<-
<note>original: "Gaṇaka-kumuda-kaumudī" and "Vāsanā-vibhūṣaṇam." A <term>ganaka</term> is a calculator or astronomer. In Sanskrit literature, "Moonlight" is a common metaphor for a commentary that illuminates a "lotus" (the text). <term>Vasana</term> refers to the mathematical proof or the underlying reasoning behind a rule.</note>

->Together with the 'Auspicious Intellect' Hindi Commentary<-
<note>original: "Śivamatī-hindī-ṭīkā-yutam." "Shivamati" (meaning "Auspicious Mind") is the title of the modern interpretive translation included in this edition.</note>

->Hindi Commentator<-
->**Dr. Satyendra Mishra**<-

->Author of the Foreword<-
->**Prof. Dr. Ramchandra Pandey**<-
->B. H. U., Varanasi.<-
<note>B. H. U. stands for Banaras Hindu University, a prominent center for the study of traditional Indian sciences in the holy city of Varanasi.</note>

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." The word <term>kutuhalam</term> translates to "curiosity," "joy," or "wonder," suggesting the intellectual delight found in mathematical precision.</note>

->Accompanied by the Sanskrit commentaries ‘Moonlight for the Lotus of Astronomers’ and ‘The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning’<-

# The Curiosity of Calculation
<note>original: "Karaṇakutūhalam." In Indian astronomy, a <term>karana</term> is a practical handbook or concise manual used to calculate planetary positions and astronomical events for a specific point in time (an epoch). The word <term>kutuhalam</term> means "curiosity," "joy," or "wonder," highlighting the intellectual delight found in the precision of the heavens.</note>

## With the Sanskrit commentaries ‘Moonlight for the Lotus of Astronomers’ and ‘The Ornament of Explanatory Reasoning,’
## and the ‘Auspicious Intellect’ Hindi Commentary

In Sanskrit literature, "Moonlight" (<term>kaumudi</term>) is a common metaphor for a commentary that clarifies or "illuminates" a complex text. <term>Vasana</term> refers to the mathematical proof or the underlying logic behind an astronomical rule, while <term>vibhushana</term> means "ornament."
